JANUARY 15, 1948 

Angry

No. 1

New Constitution Takes Effect 

The new Constitution of the Republic of China,¹ adopted by the National Assembly a year ago, went into effect on December 25, 1947. This marks the end of a half-century struggle of the Chinese people for constitutional government and the beginning of a new era in the political history of China. Time will test the merits, show up defects, and suggest improvements in this Constitution. Meanwhile, it stands as a concrete milestone for China on her way toward a modern democracy. 

The story of constitution-making in China goes back to the last years of the tottering Manchu dynasty. 

Stung by the ignominy of defeat in the first Sino- Japanese War and goaded by the clamor of public opinion for constitutional government, the Empress Dowager sent a mission in 1905 to study constitutional governments in different countries, including the United States, Britain, France, Germany, and Japan. Three years later, the imperial government issued a set of "Principles of Constitution," and announced a nine-year program leading to a constitutional monarchy. This step did not satisfy people like 

Dr. Sun Yat-sen and his followers in the revolutionary movement, who were convinced that nothing short of an overthrow of the monarchy would suffice. 

In March, 1912, the new government of the Republic proclaimed a "Provisional Compact," to serve until a permanent constitution could be adopted. But the turbulent times during the first sixteen years of the young Republic prevented any lasting achievement in this direction. All the preliminary "drafts" and ephemeral "constitutions" went by the board when the Nationalist Revolution, led by Chiang Kai-shek, established a new government in 1928, under the system of "political tutelage" originally advocated by Dr. Sun Yat-sen. 

The Constitution of the Republic of China. Chinese News Service, 

New York, 1947.

According to Dr. Sun, the complete program of the revolution must be realized in three successive stages: military unification, political tutelage, and constitutional government. The first stage was essential to overcome the entrenched forces of reactionary feudalism. The second was necessary to prepare the people gradually for self-government. Only when the people were educated to the use of their democratic rights would the country be ready for a constitutional regime. 

Accordingly, the Nationalist Government in 1931 adopted a "Provisional Constitution for the Political Tutelage Period." A committee was appointed by the 

Legislative Yuan in January, 1933, to draw up a constitution based on the Three People's Principles and the five-power system of government, following the bequeathed teachings of Dr. Sun. The first draft was published by the Legislative Yuan on March 12, 1934, and comments and criticisms were invited from the public. Benefiting by the numerous expressions of public opinion, the committee produced a second draft on July 9, and a third on October 16, 1934, which was submitted by the Legislative Yuan to the National Government. After further revisions by the 

Legislative Yuan and the Kuomintang National Con gress, the final Draft Constitution was proclaimed by the Government on May 5, 1936. 

At this stage, the Draft Constitution was ready for adoption by the National Assembly, set to be convened in late 1936. But the intervening conflict with 

Japan caused the National Assembly to be postponed until November 15, 1946, a year after the end of the war. The Constitution as finally adopted by the 

National Assembly on December 25, 1946, embodied a number of amendments agreed upon by all the parties participating in a Political Consultation Conference held earlier that year. Thus the final docu- 

2 China's Draft Constitution. Chinese News Service, New York, 1945.

ment actually represents a consensus of all the political elements in China, while retaining the influence of the teachings of Dr. Sun Yat-sen. 

Legacy of Dr. Sun's Teachings 

The essence of Dr. Sun's poitical and social teachings is found in his famous San Min Chu I, or Three People's Principles. These fundamental principles of the Chinese Republic are thus paraphrased by Dr. 

Sun Fo, President of the Legislative Yuan, who has taken a leading part throughout the formulation of the Constitution:

The purpose of Min Tzu Chu I (Principle of Nationalism) is to make China an entirely independent state, free from the control of any other country or nation. The purpose of Min Chuan 

Chu I (Principle of Democracy) is to make China a really democratic state in which its sovereignty will be vested in the body of its citizens. The purpose of Min Sheng Chu I (Principle of People's Livelihood) is to improve our social and economic systems, so that all the people will be able to find means for satisfying their livelihood and their right of existence. Although this is the simplest and most rudimentary interpretation of San Min Chu I, yet it is precisely what this great doctrine means: and, we may say, it is also precisely what we want China to be.³

It is fairly evident that Dr. Sun's teachings have, in fact, permeated the whole of the present Constitution. For example, the system of government outlined in the Constitution follows closely the theory of Dr. 

Sun, who advocated that, for a truly democratic and efficient government, there must be a distinction between the "political power" of the people and the "governing power" of the government. The former consists of the people's rights of election, recall, initiative, and referendum. The latter includes the separate powers of administration, legislation, the judiciary, examination, and control.

Chapter XII of the Constitution defines the people's rights of election, recall, initiative, and referendum. 

Any citizen of the Republic of China, at the age of twenty years, has the right of election; and any citizen at the age of twenty-three years has the right to be elected (Article 130). The people will elect a 

National Assembly to exercise political power on their behalf. Specifically, this power of the National Assembly will consist of: (1) election of the President and the Vice-President; (2) recall of the President and the 

Vice-President; (3) amendment of the Constitution; and (4) ratification of amendments to the Constitution proposed by the Legislative Yuan. The Constitution also provides that:

With respect to the exercise of the powers of initiative and referendum, besides what is stipu- 3 Quoted in Mei Ju-ao, "On the Eve of Constitutional Government in 

China," Tien Hsia Monthly, II, 5 (May, 1936), p. 449. Shanghai, 1936. lated in the preceding third and fourth sections, the National Assembly shall institute measures pertaining thereto and enforce them, after the said two powers shall have been exercised in onehalf of the hsien (counties) and municipalities of the whole country (Article 27).

The National Assembly 

As the supreme organ through which the people will exercise their constitutional rights, the National Assembly will be composed of about three thousand representatives, including: (1) one representative from every hsien, municipality, or area of equivalent status, and, in case the population exceeds five hundred thousand, one additional representative for every additional five hundred thousand; (2) representatives from 

Mongolia and Tibet, and from various racial groups in the border regions; (3) representatives of Chinese nationals residing abroad; and (4) representatives elected by occupational groups and by women's or ganizations (Article 26). 

Representatives to the National Assemly are to be elected every six years. Incumbent government officials may not be elected representatives to the National Assembly in constituencies where they hold office (Article 28). Regular sessions of the National 

Assembly are held ninety days prior to the date of expiration of the term of each presidency. Extraordinary sessions of the National Assembly may be convened: (1) when it is necessary to hold a supplementary election of the President and the Vice- President, due to the simultaneous vacancy of both of these offices; (2) when an impeachment of the 

President or the Vice-President is instituted by the Control Yuan; (3) when an amendment of the Constitution is proposed by the Legislative Yuan; or (4) when requested by over two-fifths of the repre sentatives of the National Assembly (Articles 29, 30). 

The President 

Any citizen of the Republic of China, at the age of forty years, is eligible to the office of the President or the Vice-President. The term of office is six with years, possibility of re-election for a second term (Articles 45, 47). In addition to the usual titular functions of his office, the President may declare martial law or issue an emergency decree, subject to confirmation by the Legislative Yuan (Articles 39, 43).

The President will have the power to appoint, with the consent of the Legislative Yuan, the President of the Executive Yuan; and, upon the recommendation of the latter, the Vice-President, Heads of various Ministries and Commissions, and Members without portfolio, of the Executive Yuan (Articles 55, 56). He also appoints, with the consent of the Control Yuan, the President, the Vice-President, and Grand Judges of the Judicial Yuan; similarly, the President, the

Vice-President, and Examiners of the Examination 

Yuan (Articles 79, 84).

The Five-Power Government 

The core of the new constitutional system, following the theory of Dr. Sun's teachings, is in the organization of the central government in five separate branches, each charged with one of the "governing powers." In addition to the usual executive, legislative, and judicial branches, there will be two other units, exercising the powers of examination and control, following the precedent of two traditional Chinese institutions, remodeled to form parts of a modern democratic government. The Examination Yuan is charged with the responsibility of holding examinations for public functionaries. No person may be appointed to a public office without having passed an examination (Article 85). The Control Yuan exer cises the powers of impeachment and auditing, besides the power of consent. The power of impeachment may be exercised over the President or the Vice- 

President of the Republic (Articles 97 to 100). 

Members of the Legislative Yuan, who are elected for three-year terms and are eligible for re-election, are not permitted to hold public office during their term (Articles 65, 75). Members of the Control Yuan, who are elected for six years, and are eligible for reelection, may not concurrently hold public office or engage in a professional practice (Articles 93, 103).

Judges, appointed to the Judicial Yuan by the President of the Republic, are to hold office for life. They must be independent of party affiliations (Articles 79 to 81). Examiners for the Examination Yuan must likewise be independent of party affiliations (Article 88).

As to the relations among the different Yuan, the Constitution specifies that the Executive Yuan shall be responsible to the Legislative Yuan in accordance with the following provisions: 

1. The Executive Yuan has the responsibility to present to the Legislative Yuan its administrative policies and its administrative reports. Members of the Legislative Yuan have, in the sessions of the Legislative Yuan, the right to interpellate the President and the Heads of the various Ministries and Commissions of the Executive Yuan. 

2. If the Legislative Yuan dissents to any important policy of the Executive Yuan, it may, by resolution, ask the Executive Yuan to alter such policy. With respect to such resolution, the Executive Yuan may, with the approval of the President of the Republic, request the Legislative Yuan for reconsideration. If, in reconsideration, two-thirds of the attending members of the Legislative Yuan uphold the original resolution, the President of the Executive Yuan shall either abide by the same or resign from office. 

3. If the Executive Yuan deems a resolution 

See Hu Shih, "Historical Foundations for a Democratic China," in

University of Illinois, Edmund J. James Lectures on Government, Second 

Series, p. 53-64. Urbana, 1941. passed by the Legislative Yuan on a statutory, budgetary, or treaty bill difficult and inexpedient for execution, it may, with the approval of the President of the Republic, request, within ten days after the delivery of the said resolution to the Executive Yuan, the Legislative Yuan for reconsideration. If, in reconsideration, two-thirds of the attending members of the Legislative Yuan uphold the original resolution, the President of the Executive Yuan shall either abide by the same or resign from office (Article 57).

It will be noted that the relationship between the executive and legislative branches differs from that usually found in a country with a cabinet-parliament system. In the first place, there is no provision for a "no confidence vote" in the Legislative Yuan; the 

Legislative Yuan cannot compel the Executive Yuan to resign, so long as the latter abides by the resolution of the former. On the other hand, neither has the 

Executive Yuan the right to dissolve the Legislative Yuan. Where any difference of opinion arises among the different Yuan which is not covered by the Constitution, the President of the Republic may summon a meeting of the presidents of the Yuan concerned for consultation to settle the difference (Article 44).

Central and Local Governments 

The powers of the central and local governments are spelled out in the Constitution, with the following matters expressly reserved for the Central Government: (1) Foreign affairs; (2) national defense and military affairs concerning national defense; (3) nationality law, and criminal, civil, and commercial laws; (4) judicial system; (5) aviation, national highways, state-owned railways, navigation, postal, and telegraph services; (6) Central Government finance and national revenues; (7) demarcation between national and provincial or hsien revenues; (8) state-operated economic enterprises; (9) currency system and state banks; (10) weights and measures; (11) policies of international trade; (12) financial and economic matters of an international nature; and (13) other matters of the Central Government as stipulated in the 

Constitution (Article 107). 

Other matters are listed as subjects of legislation and execution by the Central Government, but whose execution may be delegated to provincial and hsien governments; or as subjects of legislation and execution by the province, but whose execution may be delegated to the hsien; or as subjects of legislation and execution by the hsien (Articles 108 to 110). Any dispute over jurisdiction between the national and local governments is to be settled by the Legislative Yuan (Article 111).

Self-government is prescribed for the province, hsien, or municipality, the Mongolian leagues and banners, and the territory of Tibet. The people will 7 4 4 6 3 0

elect, according to their self-government laws, their own provincial, hsien, or municipal councils, as well as provincial governors, hsien magistrates, and other local administrative officers (Articles 112 to 128).

Fundamental National Policies 

The new Chinese Constitution has the distinction of being thus far the only national constitution which has written into it a special clause pledging support of the United Nations Charter. This clause occurs in Chapter XIII, in the article dealing with foreign policy, which reads:

The foreign policy of the Republic of China shall, in a spirit of independence and initiative and on the basis of the principles of equality and reciprocity, cultivate good neighborliness with other nations, and respect treaties and the United Nations Charter, in order to protect the rights and interests of overseas Chinese nationals, promote international cooperation, advance international justice, and ensure world peace (Article 141).

The largest part of this chapter deals with national economy, in general charting a middle course between the extremes of capitalism and socialism. Private ownership of land is to be both protected and restricted. Mineral wealth embedded in the land and natural power which may be utilized for public benefit shall belong to the state. On any land which has an increase in its value not through exertion of labor and the employment of capital, the state shall levy an increment tax. Self-farming landowners and persons who make use of the land by themselves are to be assisted by the state, which shall also regulate their appropriate areas of operation (Article 143). 

Public utilities and other enterprises of monopolistic nature are in principle to be operated by the state. 

They may, however, if permitted by law, be operated by citizens (Article 144). With respect to private wealth and privately operated enterprises, the state shall restrict them by law if they are deemed obstructive to the balanced development of public economy and private livelihood (Article 145).

A noteworthy section on social security requires the state to provide opportunity of employment to people who are capable of work; to improve the livelihood of laborers and farmers; to accord special protection to women and children engaged in labor; to provide by law for mediation and arbitration of disputes between capital and labor; to enforce a social insurance system and to extend assistance and relief to the aged, the infirm, and the crippled among the people who are unable to earn a living; to protect motherhood and promote the welfare of women and children; and to establish a system of socialized medical service (Articles 152 to 157).

In regard to education, all children of school age from six to twelve years are to receive free primary education, with free textbooks for those who are poor. 

All citizens beyond school age who have not received primary education are to receive supplementary education free, and also be supplied with textbooks. 

Government scholarships are to aid students who, possessing a good record in scholarship and conduct, are financially unable to pursue advanced studies (Articles 160, 161).

The government is required to spend, for educational, scientific and cultural purposes, not less than fifteen per cent of the total national budget; not less than twenty-five per cent of the provincial budget; and not less than thirty-five per cent of the hsien budget (Article 164). Encouragement is to be given by the state to persons who work in the fields of education, science, and arts; who make scientific discoveries and inventions; and who operate educational enterprises with good record (Articles 165 to 167).

Rights and Duties of the People 

What amounts to an extended Bill of Rights is found in Chapter II of the Constitution, which provides that all citizens of the Republic, irrespective of sex, religion, race, class, or party affiliation, shall be equal before the law (Article 7). Freedom of the person, freedom of domicile, of speech, of religious belief, of assembly, and of association are guaranteed (Articles 8 to 14). The fundamental right to exist, the right to work, and the right of property are recognized (Article 15).

Besides the political rights of election, recall, initiative, and referendum, the people will also have the right to present petitions, file complaints, or institute legal proceedings; to take public examinations and to hold public offices (Article 16 to 18). The people's duties to pay taxes and to perform military service are specified (Articles 19, 20). It is considered both a right and a duty of the people to receive citizen's education (Article 21). All other liberties and rights of the people that are not inimical to social order or public interest are guaranteed under the Constitution 

(Article 22). 

Interpretation and Amendment 

The power to interpret the Constitution rests with the Judicial Yuan. All laws and ordinances that are in contravention of the Constitution are null and void. In case of doubt as to wether or not a law is in contravention of the Constitution, interpretation thercon shall be made by the Judicial Yuan (Articles 

171 to 173). 

Amendments to the Constitution may be made: (1) by resolution of the National Assembly; or (2) by resolution of the Legislative Yuan, subject to ratifi cation by the National Assembly (Article 174).

FEBRUARY 1, 1948 No. 2 How is China Governed? 

Now that China has voted herself a permanent Constitution and is soon to have a new government organized under the Constitution, it is timely to touch upon a few of the questions often raised about the present government administration in China, such as: 

When did the present government come into being? 

How does it derive its authority? 

How is the government organized?

What functions are performed by each government organ?

What is the relationship between the national and local governments?

How is the change to be made from the present to the new government?

This article will explain briefly the status of the government of China at present in being, how it is organized for carrying on its functions, and what steps will be taken in the change-over to a constitutional government.1

Period of Political Tutelage 

First of all, it is necessary to understand that, in accordance with the revolutionary program outlined by the late Dr. Sun Yat-sen, China has passed through a period of "political tutelage" under the Kuomintang, dating from the establishment of the National 

Government in 1928.

In this twenty-year period, which included the long years of war with Japan, the Kuomintang, as the ruling party, has exercised the powers of government. 

The actual machinery by which the party at first ex- For an illuminating account of the organization and functioning of the Chinese National Government during the war years, see Paul M. A. 

Linebarger. The China of Chiang Kai-shek. World Peace Foundation, 

Boston, 1941. ercised its governing powers was the Central Political Council of the Kuomintang National Congress. 

In August, 1937, after the outbreak of the war, the Central Political Council was superseded by the Supreme National Defense Council, which not only took over all the functions of the former council, but also exercised a number of emergency powers made necessary by the national crisis. 

As a result of discussions which took place after the end of the war between the Kuomintang, other political parties, and non-partisans, the Supreme National Defense Council was dissolved in April, 1947, and a new, multi-party State Council became the highest policy-making body of the government. 

Thus, the Kuomintang is no longer the sole ruling party in China today, though the period of "political tutelage" does not officially end until a constitutional government is finally established. 

The National Government 

Meanwhile, the National Government, which was set up under the Provisional Constitution for the Period of Political Tutelage, has functioned throughout this period under its own Organic Law, revised from time to time in keeping with the changing needs and circumstances. 

According to the latest revisions made in April, 1947, the National Government of China is organized as follows: 

1. A State Council of 40 members, representing all political parties and independents, constitutes the highest governing body of the country. (Actually, the 

State Council has only 29 members at present, since the Communist Party and the Democratic League refused to participate in the multi-party government except on their own terms.)

2. The State Council is presided over by the President and Vice-President of the National Government. (Chiang Kai-shek was elected President in September, 1943; Dr. Sun Fo became Vice-President in April, 1947.) 

3. Under the State Council, and subject to its supervision, are five separate branches of the National Government, namely, the Executive Yuan, the Legislative 

Yuan, the Judicial Yuan, the Examination Yuan, and the Control Yuan. 

4. There are two departments, of limited authority and functions, directly attached to the office of the President, namely, a Department of Civil Affairs and a Department of Military Affairs. These serve mainly as transmission and protocol offices for the President. 

5. A Comptroller-General's Office, under the direct supervision of the President of the National Govern ment, has charge of the examination of budgets and accounts and the gathering of statistics of all government organs. (A change proposed recently in the 

Legislative Yuan will place this office in the future under the Executive Yuan.) 

6. Also directly under the supervision of the President is a national research organization, known as the Academia Sinica, which operates fourteen research institutes in the various physical and social sciences. 

7. A temporary representative body, known as the 

People's Political Council, was created in the government during the war as an experiment in practical democracy. This body has served in a consultative and advisory capacity throughout the war and dur ing the period preceding the inauguration of the constitutional regime. 

The Executive Yuan 

By far the largest and most important branch of the National Government is the Executive Yuan, which roughly corresponds to a cabinet in a western democratic government. The Executive Yuan has a president and a vice-president, roughly corresponding to a premier and a deputy premier. (General Chang Chun, a Kuomintang member, is President of the Executive 

Yuan; Mr. Wang Yun-wu, an independent, is Vice- President.) 

There are fourteen ministries, three commissions, and one independent office under the Executive Yuan, 

Ministry of Interior Ministry of Foreign Affairs Ministry of National Defense Ministry of Finance Ministry of Economic Affairs Ministry of Education Ministry of Communications Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry Ministry of Social Affairs Ministry of Food Ministry of Water Conservancy 

Ministry of Justice

Ministry of Land Administration Ministry of Health National Resources Commission Mongolian and Tibetan Affairs Commission Overseas Chinese Affairs Commission Government Information Office 

In addition to the heads of these ministries, commissions, and office of information, there are a number of ministers without portfolio who participate in the discussion and formulation of government policy in administration.

The multi-party character of the government is shown in the fact that, among the 24 ministers of state, with or without portfolio, there are fourteen Kuomintang members, three each representing the 

Young China Party and the Democratic Socialist Party, and four who are independent of party affiliations. 

The functions of the various ministries are generally similar to those found in most democratic governments. In particular, the Ministry of Education supervises the development of education on all levels; maintains a number of national universities, colleges, and polytechnical institutes; grants subsidies and allowances to students and professors; and devotes special attention to the educational needs of young people in the frontier provinces and among the Chinese nationals residing abroad. 

The Ministry of Social Affairs has the special responsibility of promoting the welfare of workers, strengthening the organization of labor unions, encouraging labor-capital cooperation, and instituting a general program of social security. The Ministry of 

Food was set up during the war to conserve the supply and control the price of food. The Ministry of 

Health supervises medical organizations, registers medical personnel, controls drugs and narcotics, and promotes a program of state medicine. 

The Ministry of Water Conservancy has charge of such matters as irrigation, flood prevention, hydroelectric power development, and the improvement of navigation channels. The Ministry of Land Administration is responsible for the administration of the Land Law, aimed at an equitable distribution of land ownership and the protection of both tenant and independent farmers.

The National Resources Commission is entrusted with the operation of mines, power plants, and various government-owned industries. The other two commissions look after special problems related to Mongolian and Tibetan affairs and to the Chinese nationals residing abroad. 

The Government Information Office performs its function both within China and through certain overseas branch offices. Such offices are maintained at present in the United States, the United Kingdom, France, and Canada.

The Legislative Yuan 

The legislative branch of the National Government is composed of a president, a vice-president, and 99 members appointed by the President of the National Government upon nomination of the President of the 

Legislative Yuan. (Dr. Sun Fo has been President of the Legislative Yuan since 1932.)

The work of the Legislative Yuan is carried on through its five standing committees and a number of special committees. Bills can be initiated by the President of the National Government; the President of the Executive, Judicial, Examination, or Control 

Yuan; or by a joint proposal of five or more members of the Legislative Yuan. A quorum of one-third of the total members of the Yuan is required for a Yuan Meeting, and decisions are taken by a simple majority of the members present.

As an indication of the amount of work done during the war years, the Legislative Yuan, from November, 1937 to March, 1945, passed a total of 791 bills, including 463 laws and regulations, 297 budgetary bills, one declaration of war, and 30 treaties. 

The Judicial Yuan 

The Judicial Yuan has a president, a vice-president, and a number of judges appointed by the President of the National Government upon recommendation of the President of the Judicial Yuan. (President of the Judicial Yuan since 1932 is Chu Cheng, veteran 

Kuomintang leader.)

There are three component organs of the Judicial 

Yuan: the Supreme Court, the Administrative Court, and the Commission for the Disciplinary Punishment of Public Functionaries. The President of the Judi cial Yuan is concurrently President of the Supreme Court. As the highest tribunal of the land, the Supreme Court handles all of the nation's civil and criminal cases for third trials.

The Administrative Court is set up to receive suits against government organs filed by those who have suffered losses through breach of law by such organs. 

The Commission for the Disciplinary Punishment of Public Functionaries has jurisdiction over all cases of embezzlement, default, dereliction of duty, breach of law, and other legal offenses of all government officials excepting those holding the highest, or special appointment, rank.

The Judicial Yuan has the power of interpreting all laws and orders and the power of altering court sentences. In addition, the Yuan may introduce in the 

Legislative Yuan bills on matters within its own competence, and may petition the President of the National Government on such matters as special pardon, commutation of sentence, or the restoration of civil rights.

The Examination Yuan 

The Examination Yuan, established in its present form in 1930, has a president and a vice-president, and two subordinate organs: the Commission of Civil 

Service Examinations and the Ministry of Personnel. 

(President of the Examination Yuan since its establishment is Tai Chuan-hsien, a Kuomintang elder statesman.)

The Commission of Civil Service Examinations is in charge of public examinations for candidates who wish to qualify for government appointments of all except the highest rank. It also conducts qualifying examinations for certain professional and technical personnel, such as lawyers, accountants, doctors, pharmacists, dentists, midwives, nurses, navigators, pilots, and various types of licensed technicians. 

The Ministry of Personnel checks the appointment and service records of all public functionaries and makes recommendations to various government organs regarding such matters as salary scales, promotion systems, and personnel management. 

The Control Yuan 

What may be regarded as a uniquely Chinese institution in the machinery of democratic government is the Control Yuan, one of the five separate branches of the National Government.2 

Established in 1931, the Control Yuan, in addition to its president and vice-president, has 49 supervisory members appointed by the President of the National 

Government. (President of the Control Yuan is Kuo mintang elder statesman Yu Yu-jen.) 

The two principal powers of the Control Yuan are impeachment and auditing. Public functionaries of all ranks are subject to impeachment by the Control Yuan. For its performance of this function, the Control Yuan has organized thirteen supervisory districts in the country, each headed by a supervisory commissioner.

Impeachment charges against any public functionary may be brought by a supervisory member on his own information or on information given by the people. The charges are examined by three supervisory members other than the informant, and, if substantiated, the person is to be tried and punished by the proper judicial authorities.

The power of auditing is exercised by the Control Yuan through its Ministry of Audit, which has departments of audit in twelve provinces and auditors appointed to those government offices which handle financial matters. 

People's Political Council 

Created as a wartime expedient, the People's Political Council has been a significant demonstration of the possibility of national unity and political democracy in China. Through a succession of four councils, representing virtually every shade of political opinion, including the Kuomintang, the Communists, the various smaller parties, and independents, this body has served as a consultative organ in the Na- 

2See Richard L. Walker. "The Control System of the Chinese Gov ernment," The Far Eastern Quarterly, VII, I (Nov. 1947), p. 3-21.

tional Government during the period of transition from political tutelage to constitutional government. 

Specifically, the People's Political Council had the power: (1) to discuss all important measures of domestic or foreign policy before they were enacted by the government, (2) to submit proposals to the gov ernment, (3) to hear reports from, and to interpellate, officers of the government. 

The first Council, convened in July, 1938, was made up of 200 members, including representatives of the provinces and special municipalities, Mongolia and Tibet, overseas Chinese, and various cultural and economic organizations of the country. 

A total of five sessions of ten days each was held by the first Council between July, 1938 and April, 1940. The Council supported the government's Program of National Resistance and Reconstruction, advocated the establishment of local self-government institutions, and urged the early convocation of the National Assembly and the inauguration of the constitutional regime. 

The second Council, consisting of 240 members, met for two sessions in March and November, 1941. 

It reaffirmed the necessity of national unity behind the war effort and the broadening of the popular foundations for a democratic government. 

The third Council, also of 240 members, met for three sessions between October, 1942 and September, 1944. It recommended the adoption by the Government of a price-control program and the institution of a Committee for the Establishment of Constitutional Government. 

The fourth and last Council, with membership eventually increased to 362, met for three sessions in 

July, 1945; March, 1946; and May, 1947. Chief concern of this Council was the convocation of the National Assembly and settlement of the Communist problem. The National Assembly was finally convened in November, 1946, resulting in the adoption of the Constitution. The Council tried very hard but was unable to settle the Communist problem. 

Local Governments 

For administrative purposes, China is divided into 35 provinces plus the territory of Tibet. The government of Tibet under the Dalai Lama is a religious autarchy, autonomous in its relationship with the 

National Government. 

The Province of Sinkiang, by special agreement with the National Government, has a semi-autonomous government. The rest of the provinces are administered according to the Revised Provincial Government Law of 1944.

Each province is governed by a provincial commission of from seven to eleven members, including a chairman (governor), all appointed by the National Government. The provincial government has jurisdiction over all provincial affairs so long as it does not conflict with the laws and orders of the National Government.

There two kinds of municipalities in China. Special municipalities, including Nanking, the national capital, come under the direct jurisdiction of the 

Executive Yuan. Ordinary municipalities are within the jurisdiction of the provincial governments. 

The municipal government is headed by a mayor, appointed by the National Government at the instance of the Executive Yuan. In the case of an ordinary municipality, the appointment is made upon the recommendation of the provincial government concerned.

The hsien (county) is regarded as the basic unit for local self-government. Since 1939, a New Hsien 

System has been in force, under which local self-government is progressively developed.3 

As a preliminary step toward local self-government, provincial councils, on the order of the People's Political Council, have been established in 20 provinces, while provisional provincial councils are in existence in six other provinces. Municipal councils and provisional municipal councils have been organized in six of the special municipalities. 

In addition, regular and provisional hsien and municipal councils have been established in 1,770 out of a total of 2,107 units in the whole country. 

Transition to Constitutional Regime 

Convocation of the newly-elected National Assembly is set for March 29 of this year. The following steps are scheduled to take place in the change-over of the government from the tutelage to the constitu tional regime: 

1. The power of the President, State Council, and all subsidiary organs of the National Government except the five Yuan will terminate as soon as the President elected under the new Constitution is inaugu rated. 

2. The powers of the present Legislative and Control Yuan will end on the day when the new Legisla tive and Control Yuan, elected under the Constitution, hold their respective first meetings. 

3. The present Executive, Judicial, and Examination Yuan will carry on their functions until these three Yuan are reorganized under the new National Government. 

4. Self-government in all the provinces, hsien, and municipalities will be carried out as soon as elections can be held for the local assemblies and for the officers of the various levels of local government. 

See "The New County System in China," Contemporary China, I, 20

PERMIT NO. 8383 

Published Twice Monthly by Chinese News Service, An Agency of the Chinese Government 

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y.

FEBRUARY 15, 1948 No. 3 

Help for the Chinese Farmer

February 5, in the traditional calendar of the Chinese farmer, marks the beginning of spring. In recent years, it has been officially observed in China as Farmer's Day. Since about three out of every four Chinese are farmers, whatever is done to help the Chinese farmer benefits the nation as a whole.

In a recent statement on United States aid to China, Premier Chang Chun announced a ten-point program of "self-help" designed by the Chinese Government "to secure the maximum benefit from external aid." 

This program, outlining the major financial and economic reform measures soon to be, or are being, undertaken by the Chinese Government, includes improvement of agricultural production and rural conditions and land reforms through the early adoption of some of the recommendations of the Chinese-American Agricultural Mission, whose report was published in 

February of last year.¹

The Mission was composed of ten agricultural specialists chosen by the United States Government and thirteen by the Chinese Government, who were assisted in their work by other specialists, both Chinese and American. The Mission began its work on June 27, 1946, spent eleven weeks in field travels covering fifteen provinces, including Taiwan, and finished its report in December, 1946.

Dr. Claude B. Hutchison, Dean of the College of Agriculture and Vice-President of the University of 

California, who headed the American section of the 

Mission, visited China again last December and discussed with the Chinese Government ways and means of implementing some of the Mission's recommendations.

Recognizing the great importance of a well-balanced and progressive agricultural program for China, where 

1 U. S. Department of Agriculture. Office of Foreign Agricultural Relations. Report of the China-United States Agricultural Mission. Washing ton, D. C., 1947. 

75 per cent of the people are engaged in farming occupations, the Mission, in its report, stated its belief that a substantial improvement in the living condi tions of rural people in China would contribute materially to a fundamental solution of her present internal problems.

The Mission's Recommendations 

The Mission made ten over-all recommendations, which it hoped could form the basis for future collaboration between the two countries. These recommendations are: 

1. That increased emphasis be placed on the construction of chemical fertilizer plants; on the development of irrigation; on the improvement of plants and animals and their protection from insects and diseases; on forestry to provide timber for construction and fuel; and on the production of fruits, vegetables, and livestock to improve diets and nutrition. 

2. That action be taken to adjust the currency exchange rate; to lower costs of transportation and credit; or to take other steps which will encourage the production and export of important agricultural commodities, such as tung oil, silk, tea, and carpet wool, and which will revive the present depressed domestic industries related to them. 

3. That means be found to provide adequate farm credit at low cost; to assist farmers in marketing their products; to improve the conditions of tenancy where serious tenancy problems exist; to advance as rapidly as possible land surveys, registration, and appraisal; and to enforce the provisions of the Land Law of 1946 with respect to the taxation of land.

1. That provision be made for standardization, grading, inspection, quarantine, and market regulations for agricultural products moving in foreign trade and in trade within the country. 

5. That problems relating to general education, public health, sanitation, transportation, river conservancy, and flood control be advanced as rapidly as possible. 

6. That an integrated system of regional agricul tural colleges and experiment stations be developed by the Ministry of Education and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, working together; and that the present Agricultural Extension Commission be reorganized into a 

National Agricultural Extension Administra tion under the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, with appropriate regional, provincial, and hsien offices. 

7. That nine strong centers of agricultural instruction, research, and extension be established at points from which they can serve all sections of the country, at which there should be a regional college of agriculture, a regional agricultural experiment station, a regional agricultural extension office, and a regional agricultural li brary. The centers proposed are: Nanking, Peiping, Changchun, Lanchow, Wukung, Chengtu, Wuchang, Canton, and Taipeh. 

8. That there be established in the Ministry of 

Agriculture and Forestry a National Agricul tural Regulatory Administration with sixteen regional offices. 

9. That there be established a single governmentsponsored bank to serve agricultural needs, to be known as the Agricultural Bank, by bringing into a single organization the present Farmers Bank of China and the Central Cooperative Bank. 

10. That the Ministry of Social Affairs consider action which might suitably be taken by the Government to guard against a rapid increase in the growth of population. 

A National Agricultural Program 

In the opinion of the Mission, a national agricultural program for China should have a three-fold objective: (1) to increase the production of agricultural products; (2) to raise the level of living in rural areas; and (3) to guide farmers into the proper utilization of the nation's land and water resources. 

The Mission recommended for consideration and action the following different aspects of a broad and comprehensive agricultural program: 

1. Physical aspects, including the questions of improved irrigation, land reclamation, and soil conservation; increased use of suitable fertilizers; continued work in plant breeding and insect and disease control; improved methods of cultivation; a nation-wide forestry program; improvement of livestock production; expansion of fishing industry; application of agricultural engineering; and extension of a scientific soil survey. 

2. Economic and social aspects, involving the best utilization of land, economic management of the farm, development of rural industries, adequate pricing and efficient marketing of farm products, and improvement in agricultural finance, as well as the problems of land tenancy, rural organization, and overpopulation. 

3. Aspects relating to farm supplies, which should make available the materials and equipment necessary for an improved agriculture, such as chemical fertilizers, farm implements, insecticides, fungicides, serums and vaccines, and improved seeds and plants for multiplication and distribution. 

4. Other aspects which pertain to the whole national economy but which vitally concern and affect agriculture, such as general education, public health, river conservancy, flood control, transportation, national unity, and democratic government. 

The Mission pointed out specifically five principal requirements in the light of needs foreseen during the next few years: (1) foodstuffs for an improved diet, (2) food needs of a growing population, (3) urgent need for forest products, (4) agricultural raw materials for industry, and (5) attention to agricultural export commodities.

Other sections of the report dealt with special studies and recommendations on: agricultural credit, land policy, farm tenancy, marketing services, production and marketing statistics, education and research, agricultural extension work, organization of agricultural services, a proposed national agricultural regulatory service, and the subject of international trade. 

Agricultural Credit 

Over two-thirds of the funds available for agricultural production and consumers' credit in China are obtained locally from landlords, relatives, merchants, and others. Because of the usual inadequacy of such funds and the high rate of interest, farmers find it difficult to practice the economies needed in efficient. production. The great bulk of farmer indebtedness in 

China consists of short-term debts incurred for consumption goods and production, leaving little for the purchase of industrial products. 

The Mission therefore recommended that a National Agricultural Bank be organized to provide deposit and loan facilities to all farmers through the channels of farmers' associations, farmer cooperatives, credit cooperatives, and local cooperative banks.

National Land Policy 

The objectives of a national agricultural land policy are to produce the maximum supply of agricultural products consistent with improved diets, the welfare of the people, and the conservation of natural resources for the use of posterity. A land policy should therefore be broad enough to include the social, economic, and physical forces concerned with these objectives.

The Mission made specific recommendations in regard to: land surveys, land registration and appraisal, land classification and use, land conservation and reclamation, size of farm, farm-land consolidation, taxation, and land administration. 

Farm Tenancy

In China, there are landlords, owner-operators, partowners, and tenant farmers. Various sample studies indicate that, for all China, owner-operators constitute approximately half of all farmers, and, of the remainder, part-owners probably outnumber tenants. 

Over one-half of the tenancy in China is on a cash basis consisting of: (1) payments of definite amounts of money, or (2) the market price of a definite amount of crop, or (3) payment of a fixed amount of produce delivered to the landlord.

Share-renting, which makes up most of the remaining tenancy, shows many variations, such as: (1) halfand-half, with most landlords furnishing land, buildings, and seed; (2) a share of grain and straw, which varies with the grade of land and kind of crops, with the landlord furnishing land, windmill, irrigation pumps, one-half the seed and fertilizer, and one-half the labor of irrigating or draining in dry or wet years. 

The Mission suggested two ways of approaching the problems incident to tenancy. First, many of the evils of tenancy may be corrected by introducing better farm-leasing practices; and secondly, tenants may be financed in the purchase of land through the development of farm-credit facilities.

Marketing Services and Statistics 

Marketing methods in China have been developed over a long period without much influence of modern practices. Farmers sell products either through a local middleman, who has a place of business in the market town or the hsien city, or through a local traveling dealer.

Present marketing methods are often wasteful through improper storage, packaging, transit facilities, and handling of the product. Absence of grading discourages farmers from producing high-quality products. Processing to preserve and improve the quality of the products is inadequate. 

Modern marketing methods now in use are limited to the testing and grading of a few export commodities; cooperative marketing of cotton, tobacco, and oranges; and the existence of a few good warehouses. 

The Mission suggested that improved marketing methods should take into consideration such factors as: quality of products, marketing facilities which maintain quality, market organization which expedites transactions and eliminates unfair practices, market statistics and a market-news service which informs both buyer and seller of market conditions, and government regulation and enforcement which provide the basis for transactions in standardized products under fair and competitive conditions. 

Education and Research 

A good start in agricultural education at the higher level has been made in China during the past 30 years. 

Agricultural colleges, organized in seventeen of China's 35 provinces, have graduated to date more than 6,000 students. Vocational education in agriculture at the middle school level has not been generally successful, except in very specialized fields, such as sericulture.

Agricultural research at the national level is at present centered in four national research bureaus in agriculture, animal husbandry, forestry, and fisheries. 

A fifth research bureau in agricultural economics is to be established soon. At the provincial level, a large number of agricultural research institutes, agricultural improvement bureaus, and other similar centers have been developed, but these are still understaffed and inadequately equipped and supported. 

The Mission recommended an integrated system of agricultural education and research, to be developed jointly by the Ministry of Education and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry. It was proposed that nine of the existing colleges of agriculture be developed into regional colleges, with strong branch experiment stations of the central research bureaus established at or near these colleges. 

It was suggested that one, and perhaps three as soon as possible, of the proposed regional agricultural colleges develop graduate study in the agricultural sciences.

Agricultural Extension Work 

Extension work in China, the Mission felt, should be more than the distribution of improved seeds, animals, fertilizers, insecticides, etc. It should include educational measures for adults as well as youth and should bring to the farmer an understanding of why as well as how things are done. 

Suggestions were made by the Mission on effective methods and procedures in extension work, recruiting and training of extension workers, and establishment of an information service in connection with the extension program. 

Agricultural Regulatory Service 

The Mission recommended the establishment of a national agricultural regulatory service under the jurisdiction of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, to provide for standardization, grading, inspection, quarantine, and market regulations for the principal exports, for all agricultural imports, and for trade in such commodities within the country. It was

suggested that government regulation and organization of marketing should be effectively administered toward a better and more efficient system of merchandising and distribution.

Organization of Agricultural Services 

The Mission proposed the organization of nine regional centers, each with a National Agricultural Col lege, a National Agricultural Experiment Station, a National Agricutural Extension Office, and a National 

Agricultural Library, located in close proximity to each other. Each of these centers would serve a group of provinces. The Mission also recommended that: 

A number of farmers' representatives and agricultural leaders should be invited to form a regional council for each region to promote cooperation between the national and provincial governments and the farmers.

The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, besides its existing structure, should include: 

1. A National Agricultural Research Administra tion, which will have under its administration the various existing and proposed national research bureaus, and the proposed nine regional experiment stations. 

2. A National Agricultural Extension Administra tion, which will be responsible for the whole agricultural extension program. There should be a regional extension office in each of the proposed nine regions. 

3. A National Agricultural Regulatory Administra tion, which will take charge of plant and animal quarantine; of inspecting agricultural commodities for interprovincial trade, as well as for export and import; and of market regulation. 

4. A National Agricultural Library Administration, which will have charge of a National Agricultural Library and nine branch libraries and be responsible for collecting agricultural reference material from the whole world for the use of agricultural organizations and individuals.

Export Trade and Commodities 

At present, China's export of agricultural commodities is only a fraction of its prewar volume. In the past, prices of export products have fluctuated sharply because of unorganized marketing procedure. The situation can be changed by better transportation, by improved communications, by organized markets, by regulatory services, by a market-news service, and by development of cooperative marketing by farmers. 

Among the exportable agricultural commodities, the Mission discussed in detail the status and possible development of the tung oil, silk, tea, and wool industries.

Implementation 

The Mission recognized that some of its recommendations would require time for completion. Furthermore, substantial increases in appropriations would be required to give effect to these recommendations. 

However, in some respects, the Mission's recommendations are already being implemented by recent actions of the National Government in improving the condition of the farmer, particularly in respect of farm tenancy.

For example, under the Land Law of 1946, a landlord is allowed to collect no more than eight per cent of the value of his land as annual rent from his tenants. In many places, where land value has not yet been assessed, the Executive Yuan has ordered that rent should be no more than 37.5 per cent of the yield. 

Other laws enacted to better the lot of the tenant farmer provide that: (1) in areas recovered from the 

Communists, land rent should be not more than onethird of the yield; (2) in all areas seriously affected by the war, rent paid by tenant farmers should be reduced by one-fourth.

The Government has also sponsored fair rental contracts between landlords and tenant farmers, which generally include a reduction of land rent and protection against arbitrary eviction of tenants. In the 

Province of Kwangsi alone, more than 200,000 such rental contracts have been concluded in recent months. 

The Ministry of Finance recently reported that, for the first eight months of 1947, agricultural loans extended through various farmers' associations and cooperatives amounted to a total of $930,000,000,000 in Chinese national currency. These loans were given for production of foodstuffs, cotton, tea, sugar, tobacco, and silk; rehabilitation of flood areas; and assistance to poor farmers in the areas recovered from the Communists.

In addition, the Finance Ministry appropriated $36,000,000,000 for the year 1947, to be extended as loans to farmers for the purchase of land. For this year, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry has a budget of some $580,000,000,000 for the purpose of increasing agricultural production. One-fifth of this budget is authorized to be used immediately on land survey and various research projects, including the improvement of tung trees. 

On February 3, another $30,000,000,000 was allocated to the Ministry, in addition to $120,000,000,000 already appropriated, for the purchase of improved seeds, chemical fertilizers, and the carrying out of small-scale irrigation projects.

MARCH 1, 1948 

No. 4 

Cooperatives in China

One of the most promising socio-economic developments in China during the recent war and postwar years is the rapid growth of the cooperative movement. Up to 1937, when China began her war against 

Japanese aggression, there were altogether about fifty thousand cooperatives in the country with a membership of slightly over two million. The latest figures available from the Ministry of Social Affairs show a total of 163,230 cooperative societies as of August 1, 1947, with a membership of 20,420,000. In other words, those ten critical years saw an increase of more than three times in the number, and almost tenfold in the membership, of cooperatives in China. 

History of the Movement

Although the cooperative movement, in the modern sense, is barely forty years old in China, the germ idea of the cooperative way of life is not new to the Chinese people. In fact, some form of cooperative living, economically and socially, has been practiced in the families and villages of China since the beginning of her recorded history.

The Chinese family itself, often with three or four generations living under the same roof, is an economic unit. Every member of the family, regardless of age or sex, receives a share of the family income according to his or her needs. The system is, in effect, a form of collective economy. It has given security to all its members, old and young, ill and well. In spite of the gradual change in the structure of the Chinese family which began after the revolution and gained added momentum during the war, the tradition of each family caring for all its members relieved the Government of many of its obligations to the unemployed, the refugees, and the wounded during the war years.

In the village, cooperation has always been practiced in a number of ways. For example, after a bumper harvest, many families in a village would make a voluntary contribution to a collective village granary. The grain thus stored in the granary would, in times of shortage, be distributed to those in need. 

Another common organization in a Chinese village is known as the "Green Crop Society." When the crops are young and need protection from thieves and stray animals, the society employs a number of persons to watch the farms collectively. These paid watchers, responsible to a number of farmers, usually do a much better job than each farmer can manage for himself individually. 

Still another form of cooperation, found in both rural and urban communities, is the "Money Loan Society," by which the members help each other with money needed for various purposes, without recourse to money-lenders. For example, a group of ten men agree to put up ten dollars apiece each month for a period of ten months. The man who starts the scheme would draw $100 at once, while each of the other members in turn would have the use of that sum of money at one time during the period of the agreement.

It was not until 1909, however, when the first modern cooperative society was organized in Shanghai by one Hsueh Hsien-chou, who had returned from Germany, where, as a student, he had been much impressed by the work of the German cooperative credit societies. The Shanghai People's Cooperative Savings 

Bank, founded by Mr. Hsueh, did not long survive his death, but the idea caught on.

After the big famine of 1920 in north China, the various voluntary relief organizations joined in forming the China International Famine Relief Commission, with the aim of pooling the resources of the relief bodies towards the prevention of famine conditions. The first project sponsored by the Commission was the organization of farmers' cooperative credit societies. These societies were formed to help mem-

bers in difficult times by the loan of money at nominal interest for the resettlement of land damaged by drought or flood. The first cooperative under these auspices was set up early in 1923 at a village near Peiping.

By 1929 there were 946 cooperative credit societies. functioning in the Province of Hopei, with a membership of 25,727 and a paid-up share-capital of $45,748. 

Meanwhile, efforts to promote cooperative organizations had been started in other parts of China, particularly in the area around Nanking, where the faculty and students of Nanking University took an active part in the movement.

When the National Government was established in Nanking in 1928, it listed the development of cooperatives as one of the chief items of its program for rural reconstruction. In a wave of reconstruction fervor that swept the country in the following years, many farmers' cooperatives were organized in the provinces of Kiangsu, Chekiang, Anhwei, Shantung, Hupeh, and Hunan. Farmers' banks were opened in those provinces to promote and finance farmers' cooperative societies. 

In 1934, the Legislative Yuan passed the Cooperative Society Law. The next year, the first National 

Cooperative Conference was held in Nanking and a department of cooperative administration was set up in the Ministry of Industry, with similar departments in many of the provincial governments. 

The outbreak of the war with Japan dealt a tremendous blow to the cooperative movement. Most of the areas where the bulk of the cooperatives had been located were overrun by the Japanese, and the cooperative structure, still in its infancy, received a temporary setback. Yet the experience gained during the thirty years of pioneering work helped greatly in the development of the cooperative movement in later years, particularly in the work of the Chinese Industrial Cooperatives.

Chinese Industrial Cooperatives 

The story of the Chinese industrial cooperatives has been told many times during the war, often with dramatic overtones. Being a wartime development, born and nurtured under exceptional circumstances, the industrial cooperatives played their part in the war effort of the hard-pressed Chinese nation. They gave employment to thousands of refugees and supplied the nation with many articles of daily necessity. 

Briefly, here is the account of the beginnings of the movement for Chinese industrial cooperatives, as told by Lu Kuang-mien, one of the organizers who took a prominent part in the movement:¹ 

When the war started in 1937, it was immediately realized that China was confronted with the gravest life-and-death struggle in her long history. Japan was waging against China a 'total war' of aggression. She was destroying and seizing many of China's most advanced economic bases. In the seizure of the Shanghai area alone, 1 The Chinese Industrial Cooperatives. Chinese Ministry of Intormation (United Kingdom Office), London, 1946. 

Japan immobilized nearly 70 per cent of China's modern industry....

A small group of people met in Shanghai to devise ways and means of coping with the serious situation. After much discussion, they reached the conclusion that the only way to provide China's economic necessities within the shortest possible time would be by the organization of innumerable small decentralized industrial cooperatives manufacturing a thousand and one articles formerly produced by China's lost industrial plant in the Lower Yangtze.

A planning committee was then formed in April, 1938, to work out further details. The committee was composed of a number of prominent people, among them the British and American Ambassadors and Mr. Hsu Hsin-loh, the wellknown progressive banker. . . . The committee met regularly every week and several weeks later a detailed plan emerged. . .

It was presented to the National Government in Hankow. Both Madame Chiang Kai-shek and 

Madame H. H. Kung took a great interest in it and gave it their hearty support. It was approved by President Chiang Kai-shek and Dr. H. H. 

Kung, Vice-President of the Executive Yuan.

The Chinese Industrial Cooperative Association was then established on August 5, 1938, in Han kow, with Dr. H. H. Kung as President: K. P. 

Liu, General Secretary: Rewi Alley, Technical 

Advisor; K. M. Lu, Chief Cooperative Organizer; and Frank Lem, Chief Engineer.

The initial five million dollars asked for were granted to the Association to be used as loan capital for the cooperatives. The first task of the 

Association was to get work started, and men were sent to establish the first headquarters, namely those of the Northwest, the Southeast, and the Southwest. Work in the Northwest was immediately started and made good progress. By the end of 1938, altogether 62 cooperatives were organized. The work of the other two regions started a few months later and made equally good progress. 

Results in Northwest China

The first industrial cooperatives to be organized under the wartime impetus was in Paoki, at the western terminus of the Lunghai Railway. Here Mr. Lu arrived on August 23, 1938, together with a young man who had been a foreman in a Shanghai factory. 

With $40,000 from general headquarters as loan capital, supplemented by additional loans from local banks, cooperatives were soon started in the northwest region in weaving, printing, tailoring, shoemaking, and for producing foodstuffs, towels, blankets, and many other articles of daily use. 

By the end of December, 1943, five years after their beginning, the industrial cooperatives in the northwest region numbered 336, with a total membership of 3,608, and a paid-up share-capital of $3,610,631.

During the five-year period they had produced, among other things: 

1,427,944 army blankets 

139,648 bales of army cloth 

55,333 bales of canvas 

122,314 yards of woolen cloth

3,310,162 brush pens

On the occasion of the fifth anniversary of the Chi nese Industrial Cooperatives, President Chiang Kai shek said: "The industrial cooperatives are a new enterprise arising out of the war. Their contribution is not confined to providing employment. . . . The organization of the C.I.C. is especially fitted to undertake the task of industrializing the rural areas, and the Government will do everything it possibly can to help. ... I want you to go on and work harder than ever and help build a firm foundation for the economic prosperity of China."

Progress During the War 

The movement, with the support of the Government, made rapid progress during the early part of the war, but experienced a gradual decline towards the end. The following table shows the changes in number and membership of the industrial cooperatives from December, 1938, to March, 1945:2

Administrative Machinery 

In Article 145 of the new Constitution, adopted by the National Assembly in December, 1946, it is stated as part of China's national policies that "Cooperative enterprises shall receive encouragement and assistance by the state."

At present, the administrative set-up in the Government to carry out this policy is centered in the Cen tral Cooperative Administration of the Ministry of Social Affairs. Under its jurisdiction is a Cooperative 

Enterprises Advisory Group whose duties are to assist local cooperative administrative organs in publicity and promotion and to assist local cooperative organizations in the conduct of their business and training of personnel.

On the provincial level, there are offices of cooperative administration in 26 provinces and the special municipalities of Nanking, Peiping, Tientsin, Tsingtao, Chungking, and Shanghai. These offices are established directly under the provincial government in ten provinces; under the provincial department of reconstruction in five provinces; under the department of social affairs in nine provinces; under the department of finance in the province of Sikang; and under the department of civil affairs in the province of Taiwan. 

In the case of the special municipalities, they all come under the jurisdiction of the bureau of social affairs. 

On the hsien (county) level, cooperative bureaus or agents are found in some or in most of the hsien governments in all provinces except Sinkiang, Chahar, Jehol, and the nine northeastern provinces.

Cooperative Organizations 

Planned promotion of cooperative enterprises began before the war and continues to be the policy of

the Government. In accordance with the organizational scheme for local self-government developed under the new hsien system, the Government seeks to promote the organization of cooperative societies in every hsiang (village), chen (town) or pao (borough).

The latter are found in the provinces of Chekiang, Kiangsi, Hupeh, Shansi, Yunnan, and Kweichow, and in the special municipalities of Peiping, Tientsin, and Chungking.

New Emphases and Developments 

It is the policy of the Government to give special encouragement to cooperatives organized for agricultural or industrial production. Whereas before the war, nearly three-fourths of the cooperatives were of the credit type, and only about six per cent represented producers' cooperatives, the distribution, as of June, 1947, shows less than one-third of the total as credit cooperatives and more than one-fourth as agricultural or industrial producers' cooperatives.

During the war, a Joint Supply and Marketing Service had been organized for the industrial cooperatives to overcome, as far as possible, the disadvantages of small-scale decentralized production. This service not only supplied raw materials to the cooperatives and sold for them their finished products but also helped to co-ordinate and plan on the production side so as to weld the numerous cooperative units into a more efficient system. Trained buyers were engaged to purchase the raw materials in place of wasteful buying by individual cooperatives. Attempts were made to help in the standardization of cooperative products. 

At present, the main stores of the Joint Supply and 

Marketing Service are in Shanghai, with branch stores established at Chungking, Canton, Tientsin, Hankow,

Shenyang (Mukden), Nanking, Peiping, and in Taiwan (Formosa). During the year 1946-1947, the stores handled a total volume of supplies amounting to $256,965,113 and of sales amounting to $286,703,243 

Chinese national currency.

One of the new developments since the end of the war is the experiment with cooperative farms. These are now organized in the Province of Honan on land reclaimed from the Yellow River flood and in Taiwan on land previously owned by the Japanese government. Other cooperative farms are found in the provinces of Kwangsi, Fukien, and Chekiang.

In addition, new projects are sponsored by the Central Cooperative Administration in the organization of livestock cooperatives in the northwestern area and of fishery cooperatives in the coastal provinces. During the past year, 102 livestock cooperatives were organized in the province of Kansu, which raised and marketed some 100,000 sheep, 2,000 horses, and 10,000 pigs.

In the province of Fukien, 24 fish-breeding and three fish-marketing cooperatives have been formed, with a total of 4,857 members. A fishing cooperative, formed in the province of Kwangtung with a hundred members, produced over 200,000 pounds of fish last year, worth about $70,000,000. 

Financing of Cooperatives

The cooperatives are financed in part by the sharecapital of the members and in part by loans from the government and other banks. As of August, 1947, the total capital of the 163,230 cooperative societies in 

China amounted to $26,072,680,000 Chinese national currency. This represents an average of over $1,200 per member for the 20,420,000 members.

At the same time, outstanding loans to all cooperatives totalled $69,501,161,000. Thirty-four cooperative banks have been established in key cities throughout the country for the purpose of serving the cooperatives with low-interest loans. In addition, cooperative treasuries have been set up by the Government in Nanking and other cities since the end of the war, particularly in areas recently recovered from the Communists.

According to government regulations, there is a 

Central Cooperative Treasury under the jurisdiction of the Central Cooperative Administration, with branch treasuries in provincial capitals and special municipalities.

These branch treasuries are now established in 

Shanghai, Hankow, Peiping, Tientsin, Tsinan, Chengchow, Sian, and Shenyang (Mukden).
