CHINA INFORMATION BULLETIN 

Published Twice Monthly by Chinese News Service, An Agency of the Chinese Government 

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y. 

MARCH 15, 1948 

No. 5

Education for Democracy 

More than two thousand years ago, the great Chinese philosopher, Mencius, taught: "The people count for most, the state counts for less, and the king for least of all." Mencius even went so far as to declare: "When a ruler treats his subjects like grass and dirt, then the subjects should treat him as a bandit and an enemy." China has also long recognized the principle of human equality before the law. There is a popular 

Chinese saying, widely invoked even in imperial days: "If a prince violates the law, he shall receive the same penalty as the common people." The concept of democracy is not new in China.

To apply the principles of democracy to political practices is China's goal today. Since China is principally an agricultural country and a preponderant number of her population is uneducated, a democratic form of government there, such as practiced in the 

United States, cannot be expected over night. Educating the people would take years and years. In the first general election of representatives of the National 

Assembly held in last November, for instance, officers had to be posted at polling stations to give help to those who could not read or write. Commenting on the election, Dr. Hu Shih, Chancellor of National 

Peking University, remarked: "In some of the constituencies where there are heavy concentrations of intellectuals, the election was carefully and efficiently conducted, and the attendance was usually heavy. 

This clearly indicates the importance of education in constitutional democracy."

The importance of education in the realization of political democracy has long been recognized. As soon as the northern expedition came to an end in 1928, the National Government inaugurated in the following year the period of political tutelage during which the people were to be taught the exercise of their political rights. In the Draft Constitution of the Republic of 

China promulgated on May 5, 1936, the educational aims of training the people for self-government and of giving equal opportunity to every citizen to receive education were emphasized. China's new Constitution, which became effective on December 25, 1947, stipu lates that an aim of education shall be the development of democratic spirit. Part 5 of the new Constitution reads:

ARTICLE 158-Education and culture shall have as its aim the development among citizens of a national spirit, a democratic spirit, national morality, sound and healthy physique, sciences, and the knowledge and ability to earn a living.

ARTICLE 159-Citizens shall have equal opportunity to receive education.

ARTICLE 160-All children of the school age from six to twelve years shall receive primary education free, and those who are poor shall be supplied with textbooks by the government. 

All citizens beyond school age who have not received primary education shall receive supplementary education free, and shall also be supplied with textbooks by the government. 

Illiteracy Greatly Reduced 

In the past twenty years, remarkable progress in the field of education has been made in China despite the interruptions of war. Following the inauguration of the period of political tutelage in 1928, the National Government in Nanking immediately introduced compulsory education for children and supplementary education for adults. The Regulations for People's 

School were promulgated by the Ministry of Education in 1929. Detailed educational programs were worked out in the Second National Conference of Education held in 1930. It was a tremendous task to provide schooling to China's uneducated millions. 

Nevertheless, the campaign for eliminating illiteracy gained momentum steadily. In 1928, 206,021 persons

acquired literacy; 887,642, in 1929; 944,289 in 1930; 

1,062,161, in 1931; 1,109,857, in 1932; 1,292,672, in 

1933; 1,353,668, in 1934; and 1,446,254 in 1935.1

To accelerate the progress of the literacy movement, the Ministry of Education, in 1936, promulgated additional regulations governing the enforcement of compulsory and supplementary education. The new regulations aimed at the elimination of illiteracy in China within six years. The number of people who received some schooling was increased from 3,121,820 in 1936 to 3,937,271 in 1937.2 Unfortunately, the program was not completed on account of the Sino-Japanese War. 

The work, however, was kept going during the war years in the areas under the control of the National 

Government. The Ministry of Education estimated that up to the beginning of 1944 a total of 47,316,540 of China's estimated 360,000,000 illiterates 3 had acquired the ability to read and write. Recent estimates indicate that there are still approximately 170,000,000 illiterates in China, which is about 39 per cent of China's population.¹

Growth of Primary Schools 

The progress of elementary education in China is also remarkable. Primary school attendance increased by approximately 10,000,000 in fifteen years despite war and disturbances. In the period from 1912 to 

1931, the enrollment in primary schools increased from 

2,795,475 to 11,684,300, showing an average annual increase of 450,000. It was estimated in 1931 that children of school age in China numbered 50,140,000, and the proportion of those in school was only 21.8 per cent of the total. It was estimated that, at this rate of increase, it would take approximately seventy years to accomplish anything approaching universal compulsory education. The National Government, therefore, decided to take steps to accelerate the progress. 

But the problem was not a simple one for it was estimated in 1930 that approximately 1,400,000 additional teachers and an annual budget of $280,000,000 would be necessary, and there was no likelihood of attaining these figures within a few years.5 

In 1932, the Ministry of Education issued the Practical Procedure on Enforcing Short-term Compulsory 

Education and the Outline for Enforcing Compul sory Education in its first stage. The former required only a one-year course, and the latter was practically the same as the requirements of a four-year primary school course. Measures were quickly adopted in various provinces in accordance with the Procedure and Outline. By 1935 enrollment in primary schools increased to 15,559,848 or 34 per cent of the total of children of school age in that year.6

In the summer of 1935, the National Government adopted a five-year plan for compulsory education, 1 See "People's Education" (in Chinese), p. 8. Published by Govern. ment Information Office, Nanking, China in September, 1947. 

2 Op. cit. 

See China Handbook, 1937-43, p. 396. Macmillan, New York, 1943. 

See Voice of China, September 15, 1947, Chinese News Service. 

See The Chinese Year Book, 1935-36, pp. 482-487. Commercial Press 

Co., Shangbai. 1935. 

See People's Education, p. 7. effective from 1935 to 1939, inclusive. At the end of the five-year period, it was expected, more than 40 per cent of the entire number of illiterate children would have received compulsory education for one year. By the end of 1936, 38,117 part-time schools and 13,267 ordinary primary schools had been established, and an additional 161 simplified primary schools were in operation. The total of primary school attendance in 

1936 reached 18,285,129.7 The five-year program was interrupted by the outbreak of the war in July, 1937, which accounted for the drop in primary school enrollment to 12,847,924 in that year. By July, 1941, there were 232,145 primary schools with an enrollment of 15,058,051 pupils. The increase was due to the promotion of the people's education program adopted by the Ministry of Education in 1940 in line with the new system of hsien (county) government. Up to 1946 the enrollment in primary schools totaled 21,831,898.º 

Increase of Secondary Schools 

The establishment of secondary schools in China began about eighty years ago and they grew rapidly at the turn of the century. The secondary education system went through a succession of changes. In 1922, the system was remodeled after that of the United States, giving three-year courses for the junior and senior high schools respectively (the beginning of the so-called 3-3 plan for middle schools), with the option of giving additional normal and vocational courses. 

In 1932, however, the principle of separating the middle school and vocational school was settled. After the founding of the National Government in Nanking in 1928, the educational authorities concentrated on the standardization of the subjects taught and emphasized equality of arts and sciences. At that time, some middle schools competed to offer selective courses and emphasized particularly social sciences and literature, resulting in confusion in middle school curriculum. To do away with the confusion, a tentative standard for each of the subjects taught in the senior and junior middle schools was decided upon in 1929 by the Ministry of Education. The official standard of middle school subjects was published part by part beginning from November, 1932.

In the period before the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese War, both students and secondary schools increased considerably. In 1937, the number of students in secondary schools reached 389,948 and of schools, 1,896. During the war years, the advancement of secondary education was slow but steady. Secondary schools increased to 2,278 in 1939, 3,455 in 1943 and 4,530 in 1945. The number of schools was nearly tripled in the eight years of war. In the corresponding period, enrollment in secondary schools rose to 622,803 in 1939, 1,101,087 in 1943 and 1,394,844 in 1945. ? Op. cit.

Development of Higher Education 

Although the history of institutions of higher learning in China can be traced back to Han Dynasty of more than two thousand years ago, the modern form began in 1862 when Tung Wen Kwan was established in Peking. The first set of regulations governing institutions of higher learning was promulgated in 1903 by the Manchu government. By 1909, two years before the establishment of the Republic of China, there were in China three universities and 24 institutions, with a total enrollment of 4,876. After the founding of the Chinese Republic, the system of higher education went through many changes. In 

1929, the National Government promulgated the basic law governing the organization of universities, introducing thereby the present system of university education. According to the law, a university must be consisted of at least three colleges, in which one was to be a college of science, of agriculture, of technology or of medical science. Otherwise, it should be known as an independent college only. This law and subsequent efforts of the National Government at reorganization and consolidation occasioned considerable changes in higher education. During the years before the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese War, university enrollment increased rapidly while the scholastic standing of the leading universities attained a higher level. From 74 in 1928 the number of universities and colleges was increased to 108 in 1937. The enrollment of 1937 reached 41,609. Dr. Han Li-wu,

Vice-Minister of Education, described this period as the "golden age" in a speech made recently at China 

Institute in New York.

The Japanese invasion in 1937 dealt China's higher education a serious blow. Buildings and equipment of the universities and colleges in the coastal provinces and municipalities were destroyed and professors and students were forced to move to the interior. The mass migrations involved 83 colleges and universities. The first migration took place in August and September, 1937, when Peiping and Tientsin fell to the enemy. 

The fall of Shanghai, Soochow, Nanking and Hangchow in November and December of the same year led to the second migration.

When the war came to an end in 1945, there were 145 colleges and universities in China, with a total of 

10 Op. cit., p. 95. 

11 See China Handbook, 1937-43, p. 368. 

78,909 students, doubling the enrollment in eight hard years. As soon as the National Government settled down in Chungking, the wartime capital, plans for reviving higher education were formulated immediately by the Ministry of Education. In accordance with these plans, new colleges were established to supplement those institutions which moved to the interior from the coastal areas. Up to 1939, the university enrollment reached 42,422, which was slightly over the number before the outbreak of the war. In 1947, the number of colleges and universities grew to 185 with 

129,336 students.

NUMBER OF STUDENTS AND SCHOOLS Of Higher EDUCATION 12 1937-1946 Academic Years Academic Year Number of Schools

The equipment, facilities and teaching personnel of colleges and universities can no longer meet the multiplied demands, and standards have shown a downward trend. In view of this danger, plans for promotion of a higher scholastic standing have been proposed recently. Dr. Hu Shih, for instance, proposed last October a ten-year plan for the development of Chinese universities to lay the foundation for China's "academic independence." His first proposal is that the Government should not establish any new university or independent colleges in the next ten years. "Under the plan," Dr. Hu said, "maximum efforts of the nation should be concentrated for the next ten years on improving from five to ten universities with excellent records and enabling them to develop fully their research projects, to become important centers of learning in China and serve as operation bases for the movement of national academic independence." Dr. Hu insists that, in the development of 

Chinese universities, the main emphasis should be on the improvement of their post-graduate departments. "Any university worthy of its name must be capable of training its scholars to do research work. It must have competent professors and research fellows to conduct independent scientific research. . . . Based on this point of view, the current system of higher education soon should be thoroughly revised. Bureaucratic interference in academic pursuits should be cut to a minimum, while freedom and responsibility of the university should be considerably increased." The plan has aroused nation-wide interest and won warm support in Chinese educational circles.18 

12 See Statistical Abstract of the Republic of China, pp. 91-92. 

13 See The China Magazine, December, 1947, pp. 25-33. Chinese News Service.

Student Subsidy 

Latest reports from China indicate that students are agitating for the extension of government subsidy to all students.

The unique system of student subsidy was originally a temporary wartime measure started in February, 1938, as a Student Loan Program to assist refugee students. The loan program was changed to a subsidy system in 1940 when a large number of students were cut off from financial support and from their families in Japanese occupied areas. An enormous amount of 

Government money has gone to finance this system ever since. In 1943, the subsidy took up approximately one-third of the nation's educational funds. 

The wartime subsidy measure was supposed to terminate with the end of the war. But in view of the difficult financial condition of students in general, the Government decided to continue it. This heavy drain on educational funds has, however, greatly handicapped normal expansion and the acquisition of better school equipment. Both the Government and the public have been conscious of this defect. Last May, the 

People's Political Council passed a resolution recommending the reduction and eventual abandonment of the practice for it was said to foster a habit of reliance on aid among students. The Ministry of Education, responding to this resolution, decided last fall to limit the number of government financed students among college freshmen and first year middle school students to 20 per cent of their total number. To promote self-reliance, government allowances are granted to deserving students as scholarships.

According to a statement made public last month by the Ministry of Education, there are 76,978 college and 44,471 high school students receiving full subsidy and 12,181 college and 12,765 high school students receiving partial subsidy. The total of subsidized students is 146,395. Each student under subsidy is given a monthly ration of 23 shih sheng (about 36 lbs.) of rice or 46 shih chin (about 50 lbs.) of flour together with a cash grant equal to one-seventh of the basic living allowance of a government employee, which is readjusted every three months according to the costof-living index. The students in the Northeast now draw a special cash grant amounting to Ch$3,000,000 monthly, and those in the Nanking-Shanghai area, Ch$400,000 monthly. Among those receiving educational aid are war veterans, orphans, children of honored soldiers, and students whose homes are in frontier provinces or in Communist-held territory. 

For the first six months of 1948, 13 per cent of the national budget has been allocated for educational and cultural purposes, as against only 3.2 per cent last year. This greatly increased appropriation is an attempt to conform with the Chinese Constitution which specifies that expenditure for educational, scientific and cultural activities shall not be less than 15 per cent of the national budget. One-fourth of the budget of the Ministry of Education, amounting to Ch$730,000.- 

000,000 goes to the support of students, a heavy burden on the national treasury.

Referring to the demand that all students be supported by the Government, a spokesman of the Ministry of Education declared on February 27 that it was both financially impossible and unreasonable for the Government to support students of the entire nation. 

He said this was unprecedented in any country. If carried out, no funds would be available for primary and mass education which are urgently needed in China.

Students Abroad and Cultural Relations 

In the past decades, China sent a fairly large num ber of students to study abroad. Between 1929 to 

1934, a total of 4,334 students studied in foreign coun tries, with 830 of them in the United States. The number of government-supported students in foreign countries was largest in 1934. After the outbreak of war, the number was greatly reduced, 92 in 1938, 65 in 1939 and 86 in 1940.

Between 1940 and 1945, 263 students left China to study abroad, 109 of them being government scholarship students and 154 private scholarship or selfsupporting students. The distribution of the 263 students was as follows: government scholarship students-United States, 59; England, 40; and India, 10; private-fund students-United States, 125; England, 4; 

Canada, 3; India, 17; Thailand, 5; and Batavia, 1.

In 1943, a number of scholarships and fellowships was offered by American and British universities and organizations. To select candidates, the Ministry of 

Education sponsored an examination in December, 1944. Out of 1,824 applicants 195 were selected.

Since V-J Day, several examinations for selecting students to study abroad have been held. In July, 1946, for instance, an examination for governmentsupported students going abroad was held in Nanking and other eight cities, at which 148 students qualified out of 3,409 applicants. Another examination for selfsupporting students going abroad was held in nine districts, qualifying 1,216 students out of 2,279 applicants. 

Dr. John Leighton Stuart, American Ambassador to 

China, announced on December 17 the establishment of the United States Educational Foundation in China to administer cultural exchange between China and the United States as authorized by the Fulbright Bill, which provides US$20,000,000 to be spent in the next 20 years in China's educational field. Among the possible projects are the dispatching of American professors to China, the granting of scholarships to students in American-sponsored institutions and the granting of research funds to specified institutions.

APRIL 1, 1948 

China's Enormous Relief Task 

Among its numerous urgent tasks, the Chinese Government has a huge undertaking of providing social relief to its afflicted people. It is now engaged in a complex displaced persons problem the size of which dwarfs postwar population displacement problems elsewhere. According to the Chinese Ministry of

Social Affairs, some 33,000,000 people north of the Yangtze River are fleeing from Communist-held areas and the battle zones to seek refuge in Government controlled towns and cities. An additional 15,000,000 persons are reported to have been displaced by famines and floods.

The war refugees are mostly peasants and their families escaping Communist oppression and the Red land-redistribution policy which paralyzes China's agricultural economy. In a statement issued on Jan. 21 by 

Dr. Hollington K. Tong, Director of the Chinese Government Information Office, it was disclosed that "the Communists are turning to murder, torture, `arson, religious persecution, and wholesale population displacement in their desperate attempt to break the spirit of the Chinese people. They are leaving a trail of blood and destruction which is turning large areas in North China into a horror chamber. Not even the 

Japanese, at their worst, touched a lower depth of sadistic terrorism than is now being witnessed in some parts of the Red-dominated regions. The facts concerning this awful condition have long been concealed to the outside world by the smoke screen of propaganda which the Communists have thrown up. 

The truth is now becoming clear. Civilization is under threat of a complete breakdown in the regions under Communist control. The Communist armies are drawing a red line of savagery, behind which terror-stricken populations are suffering.”

In spite of the Chinese peasants' well-known attachment to their land, their plight under Communist domination has become so unbearable that they readily uproot themselves to leave the turbulent countryside for urban centers under Government protection. 

The fact that millions of refugees are streaming into the Government-controlled territories from Communist-held districts tends to disprove the Communist claim that popular support is with them. According to the Ministry of Social Affairs, the Chinese Communists have made homeless the following estimated 

Shantung Honan

Hopei Anhwei Shansi Northeast

Hupeh Kiangsu Shensi

Suiyuan Kansu Jehol

Chahar 

Ningsia 

5,070,000 4,800,000 4,400,000 4,380,000 3,600,000 3,000,000 2,900,000 2,260,000 910,000 480,000 470,000 415,000 350,000 33,000

The current Chinese displaced persons problem is particularly serious because the refugees form a shifting population, unable to settle down. They move as the Communist tide advances or recedes. In some places, like in northern Kiangsu and Honan, towns and villages have changed hands time and again. This floating population is homeless, penniless, and demoralized. They are removed from productive employment and have to depend on the Government and society for support-a crushing burden upon the meager resources of both.

Relief Funds Appropriated 

Large sums of money have been appropriated by the Government for the care of the most destitute of

the refugees. Altogether Ch$231,000,000,000 (2.5 per cent of the original 1947 national budget) were distributed by the Government in the war devastated areas in 1947. Half of this sum, Ch$116,872,540,000, was distributed by the Ministry of Social Affairs, the other half by the military and provincial authorities for emergency relief. The Ministry reported¹ that it gave out Ch$23,000,000,000 in the Northeast where fighting was the severest. Shantung received over 

Ch$11,000,000,000. The four provinces of Honan, Shansi, Anhwei, and Kiangsu each got Ch$9,000,000,- 000. Hopei received Ch$8,000,000,000. The three provinces of Hupeh, Shensi, and Suiyuan each had over Ch$4,000,000,000. Other affected provinces were given their shares. The six special municipalities- 

Nanking, Peiping, Tientsin, Sian, Tsingtao, and

Hankow-where large numbers of refugees gathered, were given special funds to take care of the victims. 

Peiping alone got Ch$2,000,000,000.

Besides cash relief, the Government distributed in 

1947 some 210,110 tons of flour, 50,000 bales of clothing, and 15,000 piculs (each picul is 110 pounds) of salt to war refugees from Communist-held areas who needed immediate relief. Agricultural and personal loans granted to rehabilitate refugees by Government banks, especially the Farmers Bank, in 1947 amounted to Ch$64,890,000,000.

Meanwhile, civic organizations and public-spirited citizens also helped in aiding the roving victims. In 

Nanking, the city's Refugee Comfort Committee, a private organization, alone gave more than Ch$100,- 000,000, in addition to food and clothing, to the refugees who crowded the city's water front in early 1947. 

Students in the city launched a movement to donate one bowl of rice out of their own rations. Newspapers collected old clothes from their readers. The same movements are going on in all cities and towns where refugees congregate. However, the needs are far from being adequately met.

Winter Relief Costs 

For winter relief in early 1948 a total of more than Ch$1,000,000,000,000, comprising Government appropriations and public donations, was spent. The Government appropriated a winter relief fund of Ch$20,- 

000,000,000 and $800,000,000 Northeast currency (Ch$9,200,000,000) besides 10,000 tons of foodstuffs and 600 tons of cloth. The total value of the foodstuffs was estimated at Ch$200,000,000,000 and the cloth Ch$720,000,000,000. In addition, the Executive Yuan appropriated Ch$50,000,000,000 to cover shipping and operational costs of the winter relief program. 

The Government relief funds and goods were turned over to the provinces and municipalities according to the following list,2 drawn up after careful consultation with civil leaders and study of the needs. of the different localities.

Charity Organizations 

240,000

Mr. Ku Cheng-kang, Minister of Social Affairs, stressed that the Government relief funds and goods were far below the actual urgent needs. Therefore, a call for help was issued by the Government to the different cities and public bodies throughout the country. The sums raised by local governments and public bodies in response to the call far exceeded the Government appropriations. Shanghai alone produced Ch$60,000,000,000 to care for the needy cases in and around the city as well as neighboring towns. 

Nanking, the National Capital, raised Ch$10,000,000,- 000. The various provinces responded altogether with Ch$13,000,000,000.

Six Relief Zones 

The Ministry of Social Affairs has divided the country into six relief areas to facilitate its work of helping victims of war and natural calamities. The first area is composed of Kiangsu, Anhwei, Nanking, and Shanghai; the second Hupeh and Honan; the third Shantung and Tsingtao; the fourth Hopei, Chahar, Shansi, Peiping, and Tientsin; the fifth the Northeast; and the sixth the Southeast. Each relief area has a director to handle all relief work sponsored by the Ministry as well as to coordinate private relief activities in his territory. Each area has a supervisory group made up of local civilian leaders to oversee the distribution of the relief funds and goods and to advise on general

relief policy. Besides Government cash grants, the 

Ministry expects to have at its disposal thousands of tons of relief goods formerly handled by the UNRRA and the Chinese National Relief and Rehabilitation 

Administration. 

Beginning early 1948, the Ministry expects to receive American relief supplies under the US$30,000,- 000 American Foreign Relief Bill, and the US$18,000,- 

000 United States Emergency Foreign Aid Bill. The 

Chinese Government plans to distribute such relief supplies under a new rationing system to be enforced in the leading cities, with the cooperation of American experts and representatives. Such supplies will greatly relieve the demand for foodstuffs and other goods in the big cities, making it possible thereby for the Chinese Government to divert its food and other supplies to interior cities and villages to meet local demands. 

For the relief of children, the Ministry hopes to be able to make use of the US$3,500,000, China's share of the United Nations Children's Emergency Relief 

Fund. This money will be used exclusively for child welfare and the support of refugee children in China. 

CNRRA and Pre-V-J Day Displaced Persons 

During the war with Japan, some 40,000,000 Chinese war victims were forced to leave their homes and take to the road. After V-J Day, those with homes in South China and along the Yangtze River mostly returned to their native cities or towns. The CNRRA³ provided free transportation and cash allowances for 

1,400,000 home-going refugees before the end of July in 1947. Another 30,000 overseas Chinese were returned to the South Seas countries and islands with the help of CNRRA. Most of the displaced persons, however, returned home at their own expense. A considerable number of people remained in their adopted towns in interior China where they had established themselves during the anti-Japanese war. Still others who came from North China and areas now under Communist occupation find it impossible to return to their homes without risking their property and lives. These people, together with the new arrivals from Red-dominated areas, constitute the large number of displaced persons now seeking aid. 

Before its closing, the CNRRA provided jobs for some 10,000,000 war refugees through its public works program which included water conservancy projects and rehabilitation of railways and highways. More than 200,000 refugees were engaged and supported by 

CNRRA to repair 20,000 kilometers of highways in the three central China provinces of Hunan, Kwangsi, and Hupeh. CNRRA's roving medical corps treated millions of patients, mostly refugees, and gave millions of anti-epidemic vaccinations and injections in 1945-7. 

With the closing of CNRRA at the end of 1947, its 

3 For activities of CNRRA, see Two Years of Relief and Rehabilitation 

(in Chinese), published by Chinese Government Information Office, Nanking, 1947. Also China Handbook, 1937-1945, published by Macmillan 

Co., New York, 1947. unfinished relief activities have mostly been absorbed by the Ministry of Social Affairs.ª 

Ministry Directs Relief Work 

The Ministry's most important function during the present national emergency is the undertaking and direction of relief programs. Social relief is a longestablished institution in China. Each city has its own public and private relief institutions, permanent or temporary. At the end of 1947, there were 4,180 relief organizations registered with the Ministry. They included general relief institutions, old people's homes, children's homes, infants' homes, women's homes, and institutions for the crippled and blind, as well as relief workshops and free clinics. There were also 1,887 child welfare centers and 1,718 workers' welfare centers, registered with the Ministry. The 

Ministry extends financial and technical assistance to these institutions whenever necessary. It supports 133 at present, 28 of which are general relief organizations, 67 child welfare centers, and 24 workers' welfare centers. It runs 14 of the country's 622 registered free employment service centers which help locate jobs for the unemployed.

While the Ministry exercises the power of general supervision over all relief and welfare institutions in the country, actual oversight over local organizations, public and private, is largely entrusted to the local people. For this purpose, the Ministry has encouraged the formation of local social relief leagues in different provinces and major cities by public bodies and private citizens. Such bodies take charge of the solicitation and distribution of funds, Government grants or private donations, and the supervision of the activities of relief institutions in their respective areas. By the middle of 1947, a total of 629 of such local social relief leagues was in existence, as compared with 235 in 1945. For guidance, the Ministry promulgated and enforced laws and regulations which include the Social Relief Law and laws governing child welfare centers and employment offices. 

Since its organization in 1940, the Ministry has devoted considerable attention to experimentation and the establishment of social welfare demonstration centers. The most important of the Ministry's 37 social welfare experimental institutions, big and small, throughout the country is the Chungking Welfare Experimental Institution established in 1942. It has three homes (for the aged, children, and crippled respectively), and two hospitals (for general medical service and maternity cases respectively), and one workshop. An average of 500 people is accommodated in the institution which serves as an experimental station of relief methods. Data and findings of such institutions are passed on to their affiliated organizations for reference and study. 

For activities of the Ministry of Social Affairs, see Social Welfare (in Chinese) published by Chinese Government Information Office, Nanking, 1947.

The Ministry's Nanking Social Welfare Experi mental Institution was established in the winter of 1946. It has a women's home and a workshop. It has also a social service station doing rural social welfare work in one of Nanking's suburbs. 

A special rehabilitation center was established by the Ministry in Nanking in 1947 to help wounded soldiers and civilians. This institution emphasizes the vocational training of the crippled and maintains a workshop for manufacturing artificial limbs. 

Child Welfare Work Emphasized 

The Ministry had under its direct control 28 children's homes caring for 12,640 children by the end of 1946. Nine of these institutions were formerly orphanages established or subsidized by the National Relief Commission, which was dissolved in 1944, and 19 of them were institutions maintained by the National Association for Refugee Children organized by Madame Chiang Kai-shek during the war to take care of the nation's displaced younger generation. 

During the war against Japan, nearly 100 homes for refugee children were maintained by different child welfare organizations and at the height of their activities had more than 66,000 children under their roof. The most active of these was the National Association for Refugee Children established in Hankow in 1938. The Association had 14 regional branches supporting 61 institutions, with the help of Government grants and public contributions, both Chinese and foreign. The latter included the donations from the United Service to China and its predecessor in the United States. The number of the Association's children's homes was gradually reduced in the latter part of the war as a result of the general stabilization of the war situation. It had 19 homes when the war ended.

In its eight years of history, the Association cared for 29,849 children-21,045 boys and 8,804 girls. The children were given primary school education in the homes and later sent to higher schools or to take up apprenticeships upon graduation. In eight years, 6,389 of them left the war orphanages for different schools, including 104 for universities and colleges. Another 

2,382 became apprentices. The Association was dissolved in 1945, and its subordinate institutions were turned over to the Ministry of Social Affairs. 

The National Relief Commission maintained at first 24 children's homes in interior provinces of China. 

By the end of 1944, the number was reduced to 19, accommodating 10,853 children. Reorganization un- 

For wartime child welfare work, see China Handbook, 1937-1945, published by Macmillan Co., New York, 1947. der the Ministry of Social Affairs further reduced the number.

The China Wartime Child Relief Association, founded by a group of Government leaders, including Mr. Ku Cheng-kang, in 1938, had six orphanages under its management. They had at one time more than 

5,000 children under their care. The number of insti tutions under the Association, however, was later reduced to three and their inmates to 800. The orphanages were transferred to under the Ministry of Social Affairs at the conclusion of the war. 

The National Child Welfare Association is the old est of the Chinese child welfare associations and is still operating. It was established by Dr. H. H. Kung, former vice-president of the Executive Yuan, in Shanghai before the war. It had seven institutions caring for 1,958 children by the end of 1944. Most of the orphanages have since, however, closed down and their functions transferred to organizations under the 

Ministry of Social Affairs. 

The Ministry's 28 homes graduated 1,117 children in 1946. The graduates secured Government scholarships to enroll in middle schools.

Special Relief Levy for 1948 

To help defray the huge relief outlays, the Government decided in January to raise Ch$10,000,000,000,- 000 through a special relief levy on the rich. The levy is not a tax; public opinion will be used to enforce it. 

The nation is divided into 11 districts and each district has a committee composed of Government officials and local leaders. The quotas assigned to the different districts are: (1) Shanghai, Ch$5,500,000,- 

000,000; (2) Canton, Ch$1,000,000,000,000; Nanking,

Ch$800,000,000,000; Chengtu, Ch$800,000,000,000; 

Kunming, Ch$600,000,000,000; Sian, Ch$150,000,000,- 

000; Tientsin, Ch$400,000,000,000; Hankow, Ch$200,- 

000,000,000; Chungking, Ch$200,000,000,000; Amoy, Ch$300,000,000,000; and Tsingtao, Ch$50,000,000,000.

Levies will be collected from individuals with personal property worth more than Ch$5,000,000,000 and corporations with assets worth more than Ch$10,000,- 000,000. For individuals, the levy will be 10 per cent of their property and that on corporations 5 per cent. 

It can be paid either in cash or in the form of goods such as food, cloth, gold, silver, foreign exchange, real estate, stocks or bonds. A special committee is to be established for the evaluation of such articles deposited with the Central Trust or with any Government bank in areas where there are no branch offices of the Central Trust. The entire fund-raising program is expected to be concluded by June.

APRIL 15, 1948

APR 2 3 1948 

VARD LAW LIBRARY 

No 7

National Assembly Elects President 

In Nanking, capital of China, the first National Assembly under the new Chinese Constitution is meeting to elect the first constitutional President and Vice- 

President. The Assembly was inaugurated on March 

29, the Martyrs' Day (in commemoration of the revolutionary martyrs who sacrificed their lives in an unsuccessful uprising on that day in 1911), and is expected to last a few weeks.

The Chinese Constitution was adopted on December 25, 1946.¹ It was promulgated on New Year's Day, 1947, and went into effect on December 25 of the same year. The general election for delegates to the National Assembly was held during a three-day period beginning November 21, 1947. Approximately onehalf of the nation's estimated 250,000,000 qualified voters went to the polls.

Assembly Representation 

According to the Constitution and the Election Law, there shall be in all 3,045 delegates. They are divided as follows: 

Regional (Provinces and Municipalities)... 2,177 

Mongolia 17 57 Tibet 40 

Racial Groups in Border Regions. 

34 

Chinese Nationals Residing Abroad.. 

65 

Women's Organizations 

168 

Inland Citizens with Special Habit............... 

Farmers' Guild 

134 

Labor Unions 

126 

Fishermen's Union 10 

Business Groups 

44 

Industrial and Mining Groups.. 

24 

Educational Groups 

90 Occupational Groups 59 1 For more details of the new Constitution, please see China Information 

Bulletin, Vol. I, No. 1, dated Jan. 15, 1948.

The election took place in all the areas under Government control. In general, voting was relatively heavier in urban areas than in rural districts, in sections where the intellectuals are numerically superior than in areas of the poor and the uneducated. Being the first in its more than 4,000 years' history, the general election was not conducted flawlessly. As Minister of Interior, Chang Li-sheng, who concurrently served as Director-General of the General Electoral Office, put it, the first general election was not expected to perform miracles, but China did hope that it had demonstrated the nation's determination to achieve political democracy.

The Minister further stated: "The Communist rebellion, which drives millions of people into exile, of course, was a tremendous handicap to the election. (Minister Chang estimated then that 500 of the country's 2,019 counties are either directly or indirectly facing Communist menace.) Also, a person economically hard-pressed simply will not put aside his jobs to go to the polls."

Three political parties and non-partisans competed in the elections. The three political parties were the 

Kuomintang, the Young China Party, and the Democratic Socialist Party, all of which are represented in the State Council, the highest organ of the National Government. While the Kuomintang had its beginning in 1892 with the organization of Hsing Chung Hui (Regenerate China Society), its predecessor, the other two parties were founded during and after the 1920's. It was generally conceded that all the parties had not been sufficiently organized to arouse partisan enthusiasm such as prevailed in western countries during elections.

Elected candidates were reported by various local electoral offices. Results of the election of delegates to the National Assembly from occupational groups were

not reported until as late as February 29. When the 

Assembly opened on March 29, 1,629 delegates had registered. Three days later, the number increased to 

2,061 with additional delegates on the way. The total number was expected to reach 2,670, which is more than enough to form a two-thirds quorum required by the Constitution.

Inter-party Agreement 

According to an inter-party agreement, the Kuomintang promised to assist candidates of the two minority parties to get elected. The Young China Party produced 387 candidates, while the Democratic Socialist Party put up 453. The number of candidates the former hoped that the Kuomintang would help get elected was 300; the latter, 260. In accordance with this agreement, many elected Kuomintang delegates gave up their seats in favor of the candidates of the two parties.

Some 200 Kuomintang members, who ran without party sanction, got themselves elected as "independents." The group at first persisted in retaining their seats, but when President Chiang Kai-shek, as Director General of the Kuomintang, personally intervened in favor of the minority parties' candidates, all except a little more than 50 yielded on the day the Assembly was convened. 

Answering critics charging "managed election," Carsun Chang, leader of the Democratic Socialist Party, said in a letter to the editor of the New York Times2 that "it is no surprise to my party, with its short political experience, that these independents have taken the place of our nominees. What is surprising is that even

Chen Li-fu, who has the best and most efficient organization in the Kuomintang, also lost out to the independents. All of which shows that, rudimentary as it is, there is such a thing as public opinion in China, and, what is more important, this public opinion refuses to be molded by organized interests and groups. 

I am both surprised and encouraged by the emergence of this unknown quantity. This does not mean that I do not have my quarrel with the Kuomintang, because many of these independents are still Kuomintang people. But the important thing is that, though they belong to the Kuomintang, they were elected by the people rather than forced upon them by the organized groups."

Chinese Women Represented 

Chinese women are well represented in the Assembly. According to the Constitution and the Election 

Law, there shall be 299 women delegates, of whom 168 are representatives of local and national women's organizations; 74, occupational groups; 40, regional delegates; 6, Mongolian leagues and banners; 6, overseas Chinese; 4, Tibet; and 1, the southwestern province of Yunnan. 

2 The letter appeared in the New York Times on March 21, 1948.

Up to the time the Assembly opened, 223 of the elected women delegates had reported. The number consisted of 40 regional delegates; 1 from Yunnan; 90 from local women's organizations; 20 from national women's organizations; and 72 from occupational groups. The occupational groups have the following professional classifications: traders, miners, and industrial workers; educators and teachers; journalists, lawyers, chemists, accountants, herb doctors, medical practitioners, nurses, obstetricians, and agricultural workers.

Among those elected were: Miss Chen Chi-yi, regional delegate of Nanking and deputy secretarygeneral of the Women's Advisory Council of the New 

Life Movement Association; Mrs. Ma Chao-chun, mem ber of the Kuomintang Central Executive Committee and chairman of the Nanking Chapter of the Red Cross Society; Mrs. Li Tsung-jen, social worker of 

North China; and Mrs. Hsiung Hsi-ling of the Educational Group.

Functions of Assembly 

Article 27 of the Constitution defines the functions and powers of the National Assembly as follows: 

(1) Election of the President and Vice-President; (2) Recall of the President and Vice-President; (3) Amendment of the Constitution; and (4) Ratification of amendments to the Constitution proposed by the Legislative Yuan.

It stipulates that "with respect to the exercise of the powers of initiative and referendum, besides what is stipulated in the preceding third and fourth sections, the National Assembly shall institute measures pertaining thereto and enforce them, after the said two powers shall have been exercised in one-half of the hsien (counties) and municipalities of the whole country."

When the Assembly held its first regular session on 

April 6, after seven preparatory meetings, a movement was initiated by some delegates to expand the functions of the Assembly.

It was stated in one item on the Assembly's agenda that the scope of the proposals submitted by any delegate must be within that of Article 27 of the Constitution as outlined above. Lin Pin, jurist and member of the Legislative Yuan, proposed that it be amended to include discussions of proposals that can help eliminate obstacles to constitutionalism. Kuo Hung-chun, a delegate representing newspapermen, asked that the Assembly's powers be enlarged to include legislation on the Communist rebellion and national reconstruction.

Outside the Assembly, discussions were prevalent on the wisdom or otherwise of amending the Constitution right away. The Nanking Ta Kang Pao, a Kuomintang organ, wrote:

"Those in favor of introducing amendment measures now maintain that they have the constitutional right to do so any time after it is enforced and that the Constitution, since it is a compromise between many divergent partisan opinions, has defects which have made alterations not only necessary but urgent. "Holders of the opposite view have advanced arguments equally plausible. For one thing, the Constitution was enforced only three months ago with its merits unproven. For another, it should not be changed so often because doing so might cause distrust of the people in this fundamental law of the nation. "Furthermore, most of the delegates hold important positions in their respective fields and they cannot stay away too long from their offices. Since deliberating on constitutional amendments is a time-consuming affair, it will be better to put it off till some other time. "On the strength of these arguments, much as we agree with those who are in favor of amending the Constitution, we hardly think it is advisable to do so now."

As presently constituted, the Assembly's powers are limited to those of election and amendment, and, as such, the term of office of the Assembly delegates will coincide with that of the President.

Presidential Candidates 

According to the Constitution, "any citizen of the

Republic of China having attained to the age of forty years is eligible to the office of the President or the Vice-President," and "the term of office of the President and the Vice-President shall be six years. They may be elected for a second term." It is stipulated that the National Assembly shall be summoned by the President to meet ninety days prior to the date of expiration of the term of each presidency. 

The convocation of the first Assembly under the Constitution was widely hailed as a political milestone in Chinese history. Despite the two presidential elections, respectively, in 1913 and 1923, which took place under the former Peking Government in China with the Parliament functioning as the Chinese electoral college, the election of the constitutional President and Vice-President is unprecedented in China.

Pre-election campaigning by candidates was not energetic by any western standard. But political observers were generally impressed by the manner with which vice-presidential campaigns were being conconducted. The Chinese people have long been known as retiring and reserved, but in campaigning for the second highest office, the competitors, casting modesty to the four winds, came out with assertions of their own accomplishments, a practice regarded as unseemly in former times. All, however, refrained from belaboring or criticizing their competitors. 

Election Procedures 

In order to be a presidential candidate, one has to secure the nomination of at least 100 Assembly delegates. A candidate need not be a delegate to the Assembly.

Voting is by secret ballot. Each Assembly delegate may vote for only one candidate. The balloting for the presidency precedes the selection of a vice-president. To win, presidential and vice-presidential candidates must secure the votes of more than half of the total number of the Assembly delegates. If the first ballot does not give a majority to any one candidate, a second ballot follows to elect one from the three who secured the highest number of votes in the first ballot. A third ballot may be cast if necessary. If the third ballot still fails to produce a clear majority, a fourth vote may be called for on the two candidates who secured the highest number in the last preceding ballot. In the event of a tie, balloting continues until one obtains a majority. The Assembly chairman proclaims the successful candidates. 

3 For a discussion of Chinese Parliament and Administration during the earlier years of the Republic, see Modern China, A Political Study, by Sih-gung Shen, published by the Oxford University Press, 1919. * President Chiang Kai-shek stated in Nanking on April 4 that he would not run for the presidency and would rather devote himself to the unfinished task of suppressing the Communist rebellion and bringing to fruition the national reconstruction program. Thus the only presidential candidate known at that time was Chu Cheng, the 72-year old President of the Judicial Yuan. In announcing his candidacy for the highest post of the nation on March 31, he explained that his participation in the race was only "auxiliary" in nature and he earnestly hoped that President Chiang would be re-elected. Of the five vice-presidential contestants, three are civilians and the remaining two, high-ranking military officers. Yu Yu-jen, President of the Control Yuan, and Mo Teh-hui, a non-partisan State Councillor, hail from Shensi and the Northeast, respectively, while the other three come from provinces below the Yangtze valley: Gen. Cheng Chien being the native son of Hunan; Gen. Li Tsung-jen, of Kwangsi; and 

Dr. Sun Fo, the incumbent Vice-President, of Kwangtung. 

P. P. C. Bridge to Constitutionalism 

With the convocation of the National Assembly and the implementation of the Constitution, the People's Political Council went out of existence after ten years of useful and valuable service as a bridge to constitutionalism.

The Council first came into being in 1938 as a result of a resolution adopted by the Extraordinary National Congress of the Kuomintang which provided that "the Government should set up an organ for the participation by the people in public affairs, whereby the service of all persons of outstanding virtues and of great wisdom may be utilized in the formation of state policies." Since then, four People's Political Councils came into being which held a total of 13 plenary sessions in ten years. The Councils adopted altogether 

2,231 resolutions, of which 49 were on general subjects, 200 on war and national defense, 107 on foreign affairs, 795 on internal affairs, 745 on finance and economics, 325 on education and cultural affairs, and 10 on Council procedures.

The P.P.C. was at first authorized to deliberate on policies of the Government, to make recommendations to the Government, to hear reports from the Government, and to make interpellations. The second Council was granted the added power in 1941 to conduct investigations on matters entrusted to it by the Government. In 1945 the Government gave the Council the right to examine and debate on the National Budget before its adoption. While the resolutions of the P.P.C. were not binding on the Government as its functions were advisory, most of the 2,231 resolutions adopted by the four Councils were accepted by the Government and enforced after due review by the 

Supreme National Defense Council, China's highest wartime policy-making organ, and following the dissolution of the Supreme National Defense Council, by the National Government.

Beginning with the second Council, regional representatives were elected by provincial and municipal assemblies on regional and occupational bases, thus giving the Council a partially representative nature. 

The first P.P.C. was entirely appointed by the Government.

The first P.P.C. had 200 members, with 88 of them nominated on a regional basis, 6 from Mongolia and 

Tibet, 6 from overseas Chinese groups, and 100 from people who had served more than three years in various important cultural and economic organizations. 

All shades of political thought in the country were represented. The Chinese Communist Party had seven members in the Council, and most of them attended the five plenary sessions of the first P.P.C. between 

1938 and 1940, the first one held in Hankow and the other four in Chungking, China's wartime capital.

The first plenary session met in July, 1938, and adopted resolutions favoring the Program of Armed Resistance and National Reconstruction and the establishment of local provisional representative bodies for popular participation in Government. The second plenary session took place in October-November, 1938, in Chungking. It pledged full support to continued armed resistance against Japanese aggression. 

The third session, held in February, 1939, denounced the traitor Wang Ching-wei in no uncertain terms.

It also voted the advocation of National Spiritual Mobilization to strengthen the nation's morale for war. The fourth session took place in September, 1939, which mapped out the plan for the development of Szechwan and Sikang as a war base. It petitioned the 

Government to call a National Assembly to adopt a 

Constitution. The fifth session, held in April, 1940, denounced the puppet organization in Nanking and discussed exhaustively the May 5 Draft Constitution. 

In 1941 the second P.P.C. was formed with 240 members, 90 of whom were elected by the provincial and municipal assemblies. It held two plenary sessions. 

The first one was held in March, 1941. The Communist members, before the session, presented the Council with a number of demands as a condition of their participation. The Council felt that acceptance of demands from any group would set a harmful precedent in the Council and refused to consider the Communist demands. Since then, the Communists boycotted the P.P.C. The second session met in November, 1941, to reaffirm the Chinese national demand for the recovery of the Northeast. It resolved to ask the

Government to convene the National Assembly immediately after the war.

The third P.P.C. was organized in 1942 with 240 members, 164 of them elected. It held three plenary sessions. The first October session recommended to the Government price control measures and formed a 

Promotion Committee for Economic Mobilization. The second session took place in September, 1943. It established the Promotion Committee for the Implementation of Constitutionalism. The Committee was instrumental in promoting studies of the Draft Constitution and the protection of human rights in China. The third session met in September, 1944, to recommend measures of military reform and to hear reports on Government-Communist negotiations from both Government and Communist representatives.

In 1945 the fourth P.P.C. came into existence with 

290 members, 199 of them elected. This Council held three plenary sessions, the last one in Nanking, China's capital. The first session, July, 1945, was marked by resolutions on the convocation of the National Assembly and reports by the Council's delegates who had made a special trip to Yenan, the then Communist headquarters, in their unsuccessful attempt to win Communist cooperation. The second session was held in March, 1946. It organized investigation groups to tour areas recovered from the Japanese and made proposals to the Government on the administration of the recovered areas. The third session, held in Nanking in May, 1947, devoted most of its time to the restoration of internal peace. It urged the Communists to participate in the discussions of the Council. The request, however, was flatly rejected by the Communists. 

The People's Political Council was partially representative in its organization and advisory in its function. It was not a representative body like the National 

Assembly and the Legislative Yuan. Nevertheless, in its ten years of history, the Council discharged with distinction its trust in supporting the war of resistance and promoting democracy and constitutionalism.

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y.

MAY 1, 1948 

Assembly Hears Cabinet Reports 

Ten days after the National Assembly opened in Nanking on March 29, the more than 2,000 delegates voted to hear Government reports. Led by President 

Chiang Kai-shek, who reviewed the important economic and military situation on April 9, Cabinet Ministers followed one another in outlining to the Assembly the state of affairs in their respective domains. 

By April 14, eight Ministers had completed their reports. They were: General Pai Chung-hsi, Minister of National Defense; O. K. Yui, Minister of Finance;

Chen Chi-tien, Minister of Economic Affairs; Yu Tawei, Minister of Communications; Chang Li-sheng, Minister of Interior; Wang Shih-chieh, Minister of 

Foreign Affairs; Ku Cheng-kang, Minister of Social 

Affairs; and Chu Chia-hua, Minister of Education.

Printed below are summaries of the reports presented to the Assembly by President Chiang and the Ministers concerned.

President's Review 

President Chiang Kai-shek reported:

Communist propaganda and pessimistic foreign press reports notwithstanding, China is in no danger of an economic collapse nor is the Government's military campaign against the Communist rebels going to result in a debacle. The Chinese currency is adequately backed. The 

Government has in the Central Bank of China vaults gold and silver bullion valued at US$100,000,000, sum which alone is enough to cover the less than 70 trillion Chinese dollar notes in circulation. (This is calculated on the basis of US$1 to Ch$600,000). In addition, the Government has US$180,000,000 of foreign exchange, and assets estimated at US$400,000,000 in the China Textile Corporation, the China Merchants' Steam Navigation Company, in factories operated by the National Resources Commission, former enemy and puppet properties, and Japanese reparations. All these assets may be sold at any time and the proceeds used to stabilize the currency. 

No. 8

Reports of China's impending economic collapse are propagandistic fabrications put out by the Communists. China's crisis is more psychological, accentuated by Communist subversion. As a result, some people lost self-confidence and are at a loss as to how they can help themselves. Others lead a luxurious but wasteful life.

The military situation is not so dark and grave as the Communists have portrayed it. The Communists will not gain control of China, be it in six or sixty years.

Out of its concern over the welfare of the people, the Government has deployed troops to protect cities and towns from Communist ravage in all areas contested by the Communists, with the inevitable result that troops are spread thin, thus giving the Communists opportunities to choose their targets. 

In view of this, the Government is compelled to revamp its military strategy. It has now decided to combat the Communists with new tactics of fighting battles at times and places of its own choosing. 

Despite tactical errors in the past, the Government has succeeded in wresting almost all cities of strategic value or political importance from the Communists. 

With the capture of Yenan, the Communists have been deprived of their political nerve-center and their forces, since then, have been operating like roving bands. Of cities of strategic value, including, in the order of importance, Chinhsien, Mukden, Changchun, and Harbin outside the Great Wall, and Kalgan and Chefoo inside the Great Wall, all but Harbin are securely in the hands of the Government. 

In the Northeast, the Government lost in the past year seven divisions, composed mostly of ill-trained former puppet forces. But the Government field armies remain intact. So long as the Government holds 

Chinhsien, Mukden, and Changchun, the Northeast will be China's. In North China, the situation has

been greatly stabilized since General Fu Tso-yi took command of all Government forces last October.

Foreign Relations 

Wang Shih-chieh, Minister of Foreign Affairs, reported: 

1. World Peace: The most confirmed optimist can not deny that the current conditions in the world are serious. There are, admittedly, some points of dif ference between the world today and the world on the eve of the second world war. One of these differences consists of the fact that today all the major powers do not desire war; indeed, they may all have some abhorrence of war. Such abhorrence of and disinclination to war do not, however, lighten the gravity of the world situation. This is so because states that do not desire war are not all working for peace. Therein lies the fundamental danger.

More than two years after the cessation of hostilities of the second world war, the United Nations have not reached a common policy with regard to Germany and Japan, thereby leaving peace treaties with them still unconcluded. Moreover, the United Nations Organization has yet to develop into an organ with powerful sanctions able to preserve peace. That the situation is serious cannot be gainsaid. 

Under these grave conditions, China must continue to exert all efforts to contribute to the realization of any proposal for buttressing the structure of the United Nations. In order to strengthen the United 

Nations, we are firmly of the opinion that, firstly, we must devise ways to restrict abuse of the veto power and, secondly, to establish as soon as possible, in accordance with the Charter, an armed force of the 

United Nations.

We will endeavor to see that these two aims be attained without recourse to the amendment of the Charter, for not only is the process of amending the Charter a difficult one, but it may be fraught with danger. Should the only choice left to us be the collapse of the United Nations or amendment of its Charter, we may have to support the latter. 

2. Relations with United States: Sino-American friendship has both a long history and a solid basis. 

During the last four years, American opinion has been critical of us. Much of this adverse opinion consists of legitimate criticism, but some of it is the result of malicious Communist propaganda. The recent decision to continue aid to China by the American Congress and Administration has again demonstrated the fundamental soundness of Sino-American friendship.

Since V-J Day, the United States has afforded us, among other things, two major kinds of assistance: (1) She has helped our Government in repatriating two to three million Japanese, both military and civilian. Had it not been for this action, there would be additional obstruction in our present campaign of Communist suppression. (2) She helped to transport to the Northeast and North China large contingents of our troops to accept the surrender of Japanese troops.

Our policy toward the United States of America is to maintain and to further the traditional friendship and cooperation that have so long characterized our relations.

The current Aid-to-China Program, part of which may be devoted to military procurement and the larger part of which is for economic assistance, aims mainly to help us to achieve economic recovery. Funds thus available will be used for the triple purpose of: (1) balancing our international payments; (2) obviating the necessity of further inflationary note issue; and (3) initiating certain selected rehabilitation projects. 

Simultaneously, the Government will put into execu tion Premier Chang Chun's Ten-Point Self-Help Pro gram so that revenue may be increased, exports may be encouraged, and expenditures may be cut. 

3. Relations with U.S.S.R. and Others: China and 

Soviet Russia have an extended common frontier. 

China likes nothing better than the maintenance of perpetual peace and friendliness between our two countries. With this end in view, the Sino-Soviet 

Treaty of August 14, 1945, was concluded. Under this treaty, the U.S.S.R. has assumed the following important obligations vis-a-vis China: (1) Except in Port

Arthur, no Soviet troops shall be stationed at any part of China's Northeastern provinces. (2) The Soviet 

Government shall afford moral and material assistance only to the National Government of China; conversely, it shall not afford any assistance to Chinese Communists who are rising in rebellion against the 

National Government. (3) The Soviet Government shall not interfere in the affairs of Sinkiang province. (4) The Soviet Government shall respect the independence of Outer Mongolia as the condition for China's recognition of Outer Mongolian independence.

Should the Soviet Government observe her obligations under the treaty, Sino-Soviet relations cannot but be peaceful and amiable. If Sino-Soviet relations are not all that should be desired, the fault does not lie so much with the treaty itself as with its nonfulfillment. Our Government will persevere in insisting on strict observance of the provisions of the treaty. 

With reference to Great Britain, France, and other countries, our Government will continue to promote friendly relations so that, on large common issues facing us, we may have whole-hearted cooperation and work together for the peace of the world. 

It is a source of gratification to us that after World War II a good number of Asiatic nations achieved their independence. Toward these new states, we shall strive for understanding and cooperation. India has had long cultural contact with us. We value espe cially cooperative efforts between the Indian Government and ourselves. Once their domestic conditions become stabilized, India and China will be in a position to contribute effectively toward the promotion of world peace. With a combined population of 800 million, India and China, even without military might, may be expected to make an invaluable joint contribution toward world peace. 

4. The Problem of Japan: Our policy toward Japan

has not been changed since V-J Day. We exact no revenge; we brook no coddling. In military matters, we favor strict control. In the coming peace treaty with Japan, adequate guarantee must be provided to prevent a resurgence of aggression. Economically, we do not oppose any policy that will lead to Japan's recovery. Our policy is to support the growth of a new, democratic Japan. Our desire is neither for revival of militarism in Japan nor for letting her be turned into a hotbed of Communism. 

On February 13 last, the Far Eastern Commission passed a resolution for the prohibition of Japanese military activities and the disposal of Japanese military installations. Before the conclusion of the peace treaty with Japan, China will insist upon thorough execution of the terms of the resolution. When such a peace treaty is to be concluded, China will insist upon the establishment of an organ of joint control. 

5. The Protection of Overseas Chinese: In the days of unequal treaties, our treaties with other countries provided only for privileges to be enjoyed by alien nationals in China but ignored the rights which shall be given to Chinese residing in other countries. The starting point in the protection of overseas Chinese, therefore, was to conclude with other countries new treaties which provided for fair treatment of overseas Chinese.

During the last two years or so, our Government has negotiated for and signed new treaties on the twin basis of equality and reciprocity with Great Britain, the United States, Canada, Belgium, Norway, Sweden, the Netherlands, France, Denmark, and Portugal. In

Siam, French Indo-China, the Philippines, and a number of Latin-American countries where there reside large numbers of our people, new treaties of this nature have been concluded.

Our Government will continue its efforts along these lines in regard to other countries with whom negotiations of such treaties are pending. 

Military Affairs

General Pai Chung-hsi, Minister of National Defense, reported:

To smash the Communist rebellion, it is necessary to coordinate military with political and economic measures and to mobilize all local manpower and resources.

By engaging the Communists in open battle, we can defeat them. However, we shall never be able to wipe out their latent strength unless we get the full cooperation of the local populace. The people are ready to help the Government fight the Communists. But the Central Government in the past has not taken full advantage of them either in giving the provincial and hsien (county) authorities power to mobilize the local forces or exploiting the local resources. 

The strategy of the Communists in Central China is to occupy a large tract of territory, and then by conscripting able-bodied men and by requisitioning food, expand and strengthen their forces. Invariably, they employ numerically superior armies to tackle small 

Government garrisons.

Since the adoption last month of the total war plan by the governors of the six provinces in Central China, remarkable progress has been made in the campaign against the Communists. The plan provides for political, economic, and military coordination in the war areas. The local militia forces in west Honan have been reorganized into two powerful armies totalling some 120,000 men. They were successful in the battles of Hsihsiakou and Chengping. 

Following the governors' conference, the local governments were vested with more power. Hitherto, there was too much red tape, and financially the local authorities were too weak to undertake any large-scale project to fight the Communists. With the organization of self-defense forces throughout the Central China provinces for garrison duties, National troops will be given a freer hand in seeking battles with the Communists.

A large arsenal is being set up somewhere in Central China for the production of small arms and ammunition for these militia units. The arsenal will be in full operation within four months.

Financial Policies Minister of Finance O. K. Yui reported: 

The ultimate objective of all China's financial measures and policies is free enterprise, and the nation's inflation is not so bad as has been painted. The present total amount of currency in circulation is between $60,000 and $70,000 billion Chinese dollars.

The Government has been trying to increase its income by overhauling its old revenue system rather than by raising the rate of taxation or creating new taxes. It is also the Government's policy to effect a more equitable distribution of wealth through taxation. The progressive scale of the income tax is an example of this policy.

China advocates free international trade, but because of the unstable state of her industries, she has been forced to curtail foreign trade. Imports have to be regulated in order to give industries a chance to develop.

The Government's concrete measures to stabilize the commodity prices and to increase its industrial output includes close supervision over financial activities of all commercial banks. Idle capital is controlled as far as possible. There are long-range, low-interest industrial, agricultural, and export loans. The Foreign Exchange Equalization Board controls the importation of essential goods and assists the nation's foreign trade. 

To combat smuggling activities in South China, which have been extensive due to the existence of two foreign ports, anti-smuggling agreements have been signed with the Government of Hongkong and the 

Portuguese authorities in Macao. 

Over 22,000 smuggling cases were discovered by the

Chinese customs in all ports in 1947, the goods seized being valued at over $310 billion Chinese dollars. Up to the end of February of this year, over 2,900 smuggling cases were discovered and over $100 billion worth of goods seized.

Economic Affairs 

Minister of Economic Affairs Chen Chi-tien reported: 

Communism is the fundamental cause of the economic instability. The shortage of materials and the decreased production of essentials have resulted from the wide-spread destruction perpetrated by the Communists.

The main task of the Ministry is to rehabilitate the country's coal mines and other industries and to promote the nation's foreign trade.

The Shanghai-Nanking area, formerly completely dependent on the Northeast and North China for its coal supply, was short of coal last year because of the rebellion. As a result of a coal production conference called by the Ministry last August, the area today is almost 80 per cent self-sufficient in coal. The textile industry is short of cotton and other necessary materials. At a textile conference held last year under the auspices of the Ministry, plans were made to double the nation's textile output.

Communications Minister of Communications Yu Ta-wei reported: 

China today has about 13,000 kilometers (1 kilometer equals .6214 mile) out of 35,000 kilometers of railways in operation. When the Chinese Government moved into the Northeast after V-J Day, it found only 

200 kilometers out of 11,000 kilometers of railways in use. It repaired 3,000 kilometers and put them back into service, but Communist destruction has left only about 600 operative kilometers.

The China Merchants' Steam Navigation Co. has cleared all of its foreign debts and now belongs entirely to the Chinese people.

Internal Affairs Minister of Interior Chang Li-sheng reported: 

Although not all of China enjoys local autonomy, at least 80 per cent of her provinces and counties do have representative bodies functioning alongside governmental organs in an advisory and supervisory capacity. They have acted as liaison bodies in the execution of Government measures and have kept a constant check on the local officials, thus preventing the latter from abusing their powers.

The Government has carried out reforms guaranteeing the people their civil rights. For one thing, press censorship has been abolished and the publications law has been liberalized.

Communist rebellion and lack of funds have hampered Government efforts at improving general conditions. The greater part of China, except for the larger cities, needs schools and public health facilities. The people are too poor to undertake anything themselves. 

Out of a total of 773 Legislative Yuan members, 574 have been duly elected by the people, while 223 Control Yuan members, representing more than twothirds of the total membership, have been chosen by the provincial and municipal councils. The election of Control Yuan members has been successfully conducted, but the election of National Assembly delegates and Legislative Yuan members was by no means perfect.

In the general elections, many difficulties were encountered. The rigidity of the electoral law has not helped its universal applications, and because of the prevailing unstable economic condition, popular interest lacked spontaneity. Then there was the lack of experience on the part of the electioneering officials. 

In conducting the first elections, the Ministry was guided by two fundamental principles: inter-party collaboration must be maintained and electoral laws must be observed.

Social Affairs Minister of Social Affairs Ku Cheng-kang reported: 

Although there are over 5,000,000 organized laborers in China today, there has never been a strike serious enough to threaten the national economy. Liberal

Government policies toward labor have prevented the Communists from gaining control of the unions. 

The Ministry has done everything possible to insure the laborers a livelihood and to encourage them to participate in politics. In this way, they know they are able to speak for themselves. There are over a thousand labor representatives today who are members of municipal and provincial councils. In the 

National Assembly are over 200 workers representing labor unions. 

Since the termination of UNRRA and CNRRA, a total of Ch$5 trillions has been expended by the Ministry for direct relief to rehabilitate refugees. Part of these funds is provided by the Chinese Government; the rest, from the sale of UNRRA and CNRRA relief supplies and from the United States post-UNRRA aid. Education 

Minister of Education, Chu Chia-hua, reported:

Expenditures for educational, cultural, and scientific purposes, now amounting to Ch$28 billions, constitute 12.8 per cent of the national budget, and the amount is expected to be increased to 15 per cent as specified in the Constitution.

In the last decade, the scholastic standard has been raised by schools of all classes. The number of universities has been increased from 108 in the prewar days to 207, with the total enrollment raised from 

41,609 in 1937 to the present 148,000. The number of middle schools and primary schools has been proportionately increased. 

The Ministry is now concentrating on the promotion of fundamental education to reduce gradually the high percentage of illiteracy. To achieve its purpose, a total of Ch$8 billions has been appropriated, and it will be equitably distributed among the provinces and municipalities under Government control.
