MAY 2 1 1948 

Published Twice Monthly by Chinese News Service, An Agency of the Chinese GovernmeWARD LAW LIBRARY 

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y. Nanking 

MAY 15, 1948

China's Capital 

Nanking, where China's National Assembly met recently to elect the nation's Chief Executives, is situated on the south bank of the Yangtze River in the section of China's greatest natural wealth at a point about 400 kilometers from the mouth of the river. 

A 310-kilometer railway links it to Shanghai. The city wall of Nanking measures some 34.23 kilometers in length and encloses an area of 41.3 square kilometers.

The whole municipality takes in about 477.845 square kilometers. The port of the capital is called Hsiakwan and is situated outside the city wall in the immediate north part of the city. On the opposite bank of the 

Yangtze is Pukow, the terminus of the Tientsin-Pukow Railway.

According to the latest census, the population of Nanking reached 1,118,734 by the end of January, 1948. The city is planned to accommodate 2,000,000 residents.

The physical appearance of Nanking is picturesque. 

It is fronted by the Yangtze and flanked by hills 400 to 1500 feet high. Some of these hills such as the 

Purple, Shih Tzu (Lion's), Wu Lung, (Black Dragon) and Yu Hua (Descending Flowers) are of historic interest. The famous Tsin Huai River runs through the city. Throughout the city are lakes and scenic spots.

Among the better known scenic spots are Tsin Huai 

River, the Hsuan-Wu Lake, the Lake "Sans Souci,"

Chi Ming Sze (Cock's Crow Temple), the Drum 

Tower, etc. People fond of rowing spend pleasant hours on the Tsin Huai River and Hsuan Wu Lake.

All these places are usually frequented by travellers, especially in the spring and summer. 

Ancient Capitals 

In the course of China's long history, several cities had served as the capital. Sian, the present capital of 

Shensi, was the capital on several occasions. It reached * One kilometer = .6214 mile.

No. 9 

its prime as the capital of the Tang dynasty (618-907). 

Under the name of Chang An, it was the capital of the 

Empire from 206 B.C. to 605 A.D. 

Loyang in western Honan was another prominent capital of China. It first became the capital of East 

Chou (B.C. 720-711) and then that of Eastern or late 

Han (A.D. 25).

The Sui dynasty first made Sian its capital and after 

904 A.D. moved to Loyang.

Peking, now called Peiping, was the capital of China for about 700 years under various names. It became the capital of the Chinese Empire when the Mongols overran China and founded the Yuan dynasty. With the exception of the reign of the first emperor of the Ming dynasty, Peking was the capital of the nation through the Mongol, Ming and Manchu dynasties, until the Revolution of 1911.

Dr. Sun's Choice 

Since they were capitals at one time or another, all these cities have historical significance. But they are out of place as China's political center today. Sun Yatsen, Father of the Republic of China, made it clear in his will that Nanking should be the capital of China. 

In his opinion, Nanking is peculiarly adaptable as the site for the national capital because there may be found "high mountains, deep water and a vast level plain." 1

In order to make Nanking a modern capital, Dr. 

Sun laid down plans for its construction and development. He proposed that a large wharf be built along 

Nanking's waterfront to accommodate ocean-going vessels and that Pukow, on the opposite side of the Yangtze River, be made a great terminus of all railways. converging from the great northern plain to the valley 

1 Sun Yat-sen, The International Development of China, Ministry of 

Information of the Republic of China, Chungking, 1945, p. 43.

of the Yangtze. Moreover, Dr. Sun set forth a scheme for connecting Nanking with cities of strategic and economic importance by a railway network.2 

Nanking's History

Historically, Nanking has been a place of splendor and stateliness. The city was first constructed during the Chun-Chiu (Spring and Autumn) Period, 722-479 

B.C. It became the capital of Wu, East Tsin, Sung, Chi, Liang and Chen, known as the "Six Dynasties," which succeeded each other in rapid succession between the years 222 and 581 A.D. 

When Chu Yuan-chang, the conqueror of the Mongols, came into power in 1368, he set up his capital in Nanking, where he took the reigning name of Hung 

Wu for himself and Ming or "Brightness" for his dynasty. During his reign, Hung Wu embarked upon the construction of a large city on magnificent lines. 

The present city wall of Nanking was built by Hung Wu. The ancient tomb of the first Ming emperor with its rows and rows of giant, gray, stone statues and animals guarding the approaches is still extant at the foot of the Purple Mountain. It presents a curious contrast to Dr. Sun's massive, modern, marble mausoleum nearby.

In 1848, the Tai Ping Rebellion against the Manchus started with Hung Hsiu Chuen as the leader.

In 1852 the rebels moved northward and took several towns. Early in 1853 they captured Wuchang, and in 

March of that year Nanking fell into their hands. They proclaimed Nanking the capital. 

Seat of National Government 

In 1927, Nanking was made capital by the National 

Government. On November 1, 1928, the Nanking City

Planning Bureau was organized. This Bureau was charged with the task of devising a concrete and comprehensive plan for building up the capital city. On 

December 31, 1929, a report together with detailed recommendations was submitted by the Bureau to the 

Government for consideration.3

Commenting on the new capital, a prominent American scholar once remarked:

Nanking as a capital proved an advantage to the Nationalist Government. It had historic associations and it was removed from the galling presence of the armed legation quarter in 

Peking (now renamed Peiping). More important still, it was the most populous and prosperous region of China. A government with headquarters there had a better chance of controlling the nation than if it had been off on the periphery, at Canton or Peiping." 

2 Ibid., pp. 43-44; p. 94 et seq. "4 For a detailed account, see Tyau, Two Years of Nationalist China, Kelly and Walsh, Shanghai, 1930, p. 380 et seq.

Latourette, The Chinese, Their History and Culture, The Macmillan Co., New York, 1941 (Second Edition Revised, Two Volumes in One), pp. 437-438.

Government's Return After War 

In December, 1937, this seat of the National Government fell into Japanese hands. For the next eight years, Chungking, 1,200 miles above the Yangtze, was the wartime capital.

On May 5, 1946, the Government officially returned to Nanking. The removal of Government personnel and families, archives, faculty and students of schools and national universities and their dependents back to the capital was a tremendous job. A United Office of 

Party, Political and Military Organizations, established for the purpose, mobilized 850 buses and trucks, 83 steamships, 32 lighters, hundreds of junks and 15 airplanes in May, and by the end of October, 1946, a period of six months, 596,024 Government workers and 121,217 tons of public effects were successfully transported to Nanking and the lower Yangtze. This joint office fixed travelling and shipping priorities.

The Highway Administration of the Ministry of 

Communications kept six highway routes open to traffic between Chungking and Nanking. A total of 

238,131 persons returned to the capital via these highways. Ships plying between the two capitals carried 

203,024 persons and 84,497 tons of cargoes, while junks moved 44,588 passengers and 32,551 tons of cargoes. 

Aircrafts carried 110,281 persons and 4,169 tons of cargoes back to the original seat of the Government. 

Nanking's Rivals as Capital 

Prior to the Government's return to the original lower Yangtze seat, there was considerable debate on the question of suitability of Nanking, Peiping, Sian,

Loyang and other cities as China's national capital, particularly in newspaper and magazine columns. The discussion was carried into the National Assembly which met in Nanking in November-December, 1946, to discuss the Draft Constitution. 

Those who championed Peiping as the Government seat claimed that to safeguard the Northeast (Manchuria), bolster national defense and prevent the resurgence of aggression, Peiping should be the choice. 

Besides being a beautiful city of great historical significance and a communications and cultural center, the supporters said that Peiping was close to the Northeast and Mongolia and had great possibilities as a heavy industry center in the future. It was stressed that Peiping's propinquity to trouble centers made it desirable as the national capital, but those who advocated Nanking argued that in modern national defense, propinquity was of little importance. 

The controversy continued throughout the monthand-a-half-long session of the Assembly. The seat of the Government was originally stipulated in Article 7 of the Draft Constitution. The stipulation had to be removed owing to the spirited but unresolved disputations.

Communication Facilities 

Besides river communications, two railways serve Nanking: the Shanghai-Nanking Railway and the 

Tientsin-Pukow Railway. The capital is linked with 

Hangchow, Chekiang's provincial capital, and Wuhu, rice-producing center in Anhwei, by railways or highways.

Like all the Chinese cities in the postwar era, communications within the city constitute a daily problem. 

Nanking is large and sprawling, and the demand for transportation is extremely heavy. The vehicles on 

Nanking's streets include motor cars, trucks, buses, rickshaws, pedicabs, jeeps, and an amazing assortment of hybrids, such as horse-driven automobile chassis and motorcycle-pulled carriages on rubber-tired wheels. 

There are no trolleys in the city. While the vehicles of the Municipal Bus Company and the privatelyowned Kiangnan Bus Company carry a big load of the city's commuters, Nanking's 15-kilometer little railway -the Chinese equivalent of occidental local elevated trains and subways-plays a very important part. Some 

20,000 passengers patronize the trains each day. 

The little railway is 40 years old. The make-up of the three trains which are kept running during the sixteen-hour schedule is assorted. Behind the doughty little engine may be small freight cars with benches, open coal cars without benches and a strange looking rear coach, which can be described as a duplex cheesebox. There are no class distinctions on the railway. 

Each passenger pays a flat fare for any distance.

There are five train stops between the north and south terminals. The trains leave every hour, and the trip one way takes about sixty minutes. The trains are always on time. To maintain this record is by no means easy since the trains traverse through the heart of the city. The shrill whistle, warning the obstructionists, can be clearly heard in sections of Nanking along the line.

The little railway is operated by the Nanking Municipal Government.

Structure of Municipality 

Nanking being the capital of China, its government qualifies as that of a special municipality which comes under the direct supervision of the Executive Yuan (Cabinet).5

The Municipal Government of Nanking was formally inaugurated on June 1, 1927. Under the Mayor are a secretariat consisting of a chief secretary and a number of secretaries; four counsellors; the bureau chiefs; a number of technical experts; and chiefs of the sections. 

According to the Chinese law, a special municipality must be: (1) seat of the Government; or (2) its population exceeding one million; or (3) created under special circumstances. 

When first established, the municipality had the fol lowing bureaus: (1) Bureau of Finance (2) Bureau of Public Works; (3) Bureau of Public Safety; (4) Bureau of Education; (5) Bureau of Public Health. 

At the end of the same year the Land Office was established. In 1929 the National Government took over the Bureau of Public Safety and placed it under the control of the Ministry of Interior. In 1932 the 

Bureau of Education was amalgamated with the Bureau of Social Affairs, the Land Office with the Bureau of Finance, and the municipal government itself took direct charge of public health.

The municipal administrative council is composed of the Mayor, the counsellors and the bureau directors and section chiefs. In the city there exists a representative assembly. The territorial units within the city are the boroughs and wards, each of which has its executive, representative and supervisory organs.

Mayor Names Nanking's Needs 

Heading the municipality is Mayor Shen Yi, a German trained engineer (D. Eng., Dresden University,

Germany, 1925). In an annual report to the municipal council in March this year, the Mayor said that the most urgent needs for the 1,300,000 residents of Nanking were: houses, improvement in the rationing systems, extension of social relief activities and the improvement of the city's sewage system. 

According to the Mayor's report in February, 1947,

Nanking's housing shortage was serious. He disclosed that before the war there were 62,500 dwelling houses in the city. During the war, 4,600 of them were destroyed. Meanwhile, the population reached the million mark, which was the same as before the war. 

Despite general financial stringency, the Bureau of Public Works managed to pave 15 kilometers of highways with asphalt and to repair 374 streets and 10 bridges during 1947.

Due to the lack of maintenance and repair, the roads in the city were in bad shape following the war. After the surrender of the Japanese, roads measuring 68.59 kilometers in length have been repaired. Thirteen thoroughfares measuring 19.24 kilometers in length have been surfaced with asphalt. Others measuring 

5.46 kilometers have been laid with cement. Certain roads which were too narrow for traffic have been widened.

Besides repairs to the Hsiakwan wharves, the municipality has completed 74 kilometers of dikes.

Nanking had during 1947 a maximum water supply of some 60,000 metric tons daily. The Mayor pointed out that this was still inadequate to meet the city's needs. Three generators are expected to arrive to reinforce the city's power plant. 

The Civil Administration Bureau, founded last 

April, has successfully completed census-taking, organ-

ized the Pao-Chia (small administrative units) system, and helped in the conscription of 1,000 soldiers. In land administration, the Mayor reported, the municipality has begun its three-fold task of clearance of land deeds, survey of land and the reassessment of land values, both within city limits and in the suburbs. 

More Schools Opened 

In the educational field, Nanking was kept abreast with the increase in population. The enrollment of students has been on the increase in the past two years. 

A total of 122,767 college, secondary and primary school students were registered for the fall term of 

1947. Of this number, 9,089 were students of 11 universities and technical colleges; 27,945, students of secondary schools; and 84,733, students of primary schools. 

The number of municipal primary schools in Nanking was increased from 67 shortly after the war to 149, in addition to 36 private primary schools, and that of the municipal secondary schools from 5 to 12. The number of private secondary schools rose from 10 to 32. 

Task of Rehabilitation 

How heavy the task of rehabilitating the Nanking municipality after the Japanese surrender may be gauged from the fact that in the three months between 

February and April in 1946, some 24,000 tons of debris were moved out of the city. 

Despite extensive war devastation, Nanking still remains the foundation for city-building, which was solidly laid in the decade from the establishment of the capital in 1927 to the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese War in 1937. 

Nanking Before 1937

In those ten years of peaceful development, many magnificent Government buildings featuring the traditional Chinese architectural lines of drooping eaves were erected, and 48 main thoroughfares measuring 402 kilometers in length were constructed. Streets of lesser importance and roads connecting the various districts and outlying villages were also completed in that period. For the improvement of the city's sewage system, a total of 26,722 meters of sewers was laid. 

In flood prevention work, the Nanking municipality undertook the dredging of the river and the building of new dikes.

In the field of education, the municipality established the following schools before 1937: 

3 secondary schools; 1 normal school; 1 junior vocational school; 2 schools for the children of revolutionary martyrs; 79 primary schools; I school for the blind and the dumb; 132 free schools; 45 other normal and primary schools.

The municipal government also maintained 3 mass education institutes, 1 city library and 40 reading rooms.

In the field of public health, the municipality managed the following: 

(1) Hospitals and Health Stations. A municipal hospital which was built in 1936; an isolation hospital erected in 1933; a hospital for opium addicts completed in 1934; clinics for social diseases. Eighteen health stations functioned in the city proper and in the suburbs. These stations gave free medical treatment to the public. Maternity cases were handled free for those unable to pay. 

(2) Prevention of Diseases. The Public Health De partment provided the general public with vaccinations against small pox and with inoculations against cholera, typhoid and diphtheria. Measures were taken to exterminate germ-carrying insects so as to check malaria, cholera and other contagious diseases. 

Under the city's direction were the following relief institutions: Home for the Aged; Home for the Infirm; 

Home for the Destitute Women; schools for men without training; and orphanages.

To meet the urgent demand for housing resulting from the rapid increase in the city's population, the municipality embarked upon an extensive housebuilding project. It completed the two residential sections and put up apartment buildings and houses in seven separate districts before the Japanese invasion. 

Construction activities were extended to the improvement and laying out of public parks. A special office was created by city authorities to supervise the public parks and the trees along the main thoroughfares and streets.

Famous Nanking Products 

Nanking is known for its production of rich, velvety brocade. Nanking brocade was widely used for court dresses in the imperial days. This hand-woven fabric made of locally-grown silk is still the best material for elaborate embroidery.

Nanking farms produce rice in good quantity and quality. Its hilly land grows giant-size peanuts. The lakes and ponds dotting the municipality serve as good breeding places for ducks, which are a famed food in the Chinese capital. The city's pickled vegetables and salted duck-eggs are rated as delicacies for the Chinese breakfast table.

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y.

JUNE 1, 1948 

No. 10

President's Inaugural Address* 

The National Assembly has, in accordance with the Constitution, elected me President of the Republic of 

China. Beginning today, I am a public servant of the state and of our people. For twenty years I have been at the helm of the state. I greatly regret that because of the prolonged campaigns, first against the Communists and later against Japan, the goals originally set for reconstruction have not been attained. Henceforth, we should endeavor to introduce democracy, to undertake reconstruction and to achieve progress in the midst of renewed military operations against the rebels.

In assuming office today, uppermost in my mind are the difficulties encountered by Dr. Sun Yat-sen and our Revolutionary martyrs in establishing the Republic, the circumstances in which our nation finds itself today, and also the hopes entertained by our people at large for the future. I am fully aware of the heavy responsibilities that are now mine. 

For forty years I have dedicated myself to the af fairs of the state. My only concern has been consistently one of how best I could be of service. Nothing, therefore, was farther away from my mind than to become President of the Republic.

Support of the People 

No nation is ever built by one man, nor in a day. 

Since the people of our nation have seen fit to entrust me with these responsibilities, I hope they will also give me the support necessary to do what is expected of me.

I wish to avail myself of this opportunity to impart to our people what in my opinion are the paramount needs of our country and whither our Government and people should direct their common efforts: 

The adoption of constitutional rule is an unprecedented event in the history of the Republic. It has come at the end of a long period of trials and tribulations, during which our early Revolutionary martyrs and our heroes in the recent war have laid down their lives. Hence, it is incumbent on everyone of us to cherish what we have thus newly accomplished. 

It is for the sake of national tranquility and our people's welfare that the constitutional regime is being inaugurated. If the nation should still fail to advance toward unity, independence and progress and if the status of our people should remain unimproved as before, then constitutionalism will have lost much of its meaning.

First of all, now that the Constitution has taken effect, it is up to us to see that it is actually enforced and does not remain on paper. The enforcement, therefore, is as much the duty of the Government as that of the people as a whole. We must respect the 

Constitution, for this is the way to foster a law-abiding spirit.

Democracy a Way of Life 

Meanwhile, it is imperative that our people all understand what democracy really is and learn to live accordingly. It should be realized that democracy is 

President Chiang Kai-shek's inaugural address given in Nanking on May 20 shortly after his induction as the first President of the Republic of China under the new Constitution.

a way of life, and it finds expression not only in politics but also in economic and social activities. 

Citizens of a democratic country do not forsake their rights nor do they evade their obligations. It is democratic for a minority group to obey the majority.

However, under no circumstances should the majority usurp the rights of the minority. Conversely, no minority should ever dictate to the majority. Everyone should have self-respect. He should have the chance to express his views, but he should also be prepared to accept criticisms and to make personal sacrifices in the interest of the common good. 

Allegiance to the Constitution

In my new office, I shall endeavor to set an example for the nation by pledging my complete allegiance to the Constitution. It is my hope that by so doing I shall succeed in inculcating in our people democratic habits and thus lead our country on the road to true democracy. This is especially important at this juncture when our constitutional rule is being launched. 

In discharging our offices, both I and my colleagues in the Government are determined to stay within the bounds set by the Constitution, thereby laying a good foundation for democracy in China.

Our Constitution was adopted with a view to solidifying our sovereignty and protecting the rights of our people. What our country needs most today is unity, liberty and progress. By rising in armed rebellion and by upsetting social order, the Communists have become an obstacle to national unity and an enemy to liberty and progress. We must muster the nation's strength to eradicate these anti-democratic forces which have been creating disunity, terror and destruction. 

Following the formation of our constitutional Government, further steps must be taken to improve and strengthen our military affairs so as to bring the campaign against the rebels to a successful conclusion at an early date. This is necessary in order to deliver our people in the Communist areas from indescribable sufferings and to remove the present threat to our very existence as a nation.

Ways of Removing Communist Menace 

At the same time, we must coordinate our political, economic, educational and social activities with our military efforts against the rebels. The present military campaign was not brought on by friction between political parties. Much less was it caused by conflicting interests. It is a war between democracy and totalitarianism, between forces of unity and those of disunity, between patriots and traitors, between libertyloving people and oppressors, between those who want to see China continue as an independent nation and those who seek to subjugate her.

In effect, this is an all-out war on the part of the Chinese people to save their nation from collapse. 

I firmly believe that the strength which comes from the people through self-awakening is what it takes to defeat the rebels, however ferocious they may be. 

Likewise, it is my conviction that comprehensive political, economic and social reforms are among the most effective ways to remove the Communist menace once and for all.

It is for the purpose of safeguarding the foundations of our Republic and protecting the people's welfare that I am now calling on all freedom-loving and patriotic Chinese to close their ranks and rally behind the campaign against the rebels. This is the way to shorten the period of hostilities and to hasten the restoration of peace and order throughout the country. Then and only then will there be domestic unity and peace.

Our country hardly has had time to recover from the ravages of eight years of war of resistance against Japan when the Communist conspiracy suddenly took on even more serious proportions. As a result, plans for demobilization, rehabilitation and reconstruction were upset with serious effects on all aspects of our national life.

Administrative, Legal Reforms 

Today our nation is confronted with a serious crisis. That we have political and social shortcomings is undeniable. These shortcomings should be promptly remedied. However, any reforms, to be effective, must be enacted through institutions and laws and must meet the needs of the majority of the people. Further more, there should be a differentiation of what is fundamental, what is tributary, and what must be implemented by concrete measures of enforcement. 

Following the commencement of constitutional rule, improvements have been effected in our institutions. 

Relations between the government and the people have, as a result, become much closer. As I have been entrusted by the people with the Presidency, I shall grasp this new opportunity with the greatest sincerity and determination and see to it that the Government takes necessary steps to effect the needed administrative reforms.

In my opinion, the principal task of the new Government is to purge itself of corrupt officials, to enforce discipline, and to uproot all practices of officials using their positions to serve personal interests. On the other hand, ways must be found for the new Government to draft worthy and able citizens into service as a way of invigorating the Administration and thereby raising its efficiency.

A system of post-performance examination must be enforced so that awards and penalties will fall where

they are due. Efforts must be made to ascertain the real needs of the people. Making-believe and procrastination, which have been prevalent in the officialdom, must be done away with. The people must be relieved of extra-legal levies and exactions. Above all, laws and orders should be simplified, powers and responsibilities must be clearly demarcated and law-enforcing organs must be unified. All duplicating and impractical laws and regulations should be removed from the statute book. In short, we must enhance the dignity of law and order and afford protection to legitimate rights. All citizens must be equal before the law, and there should also be equal opportunity for all citizens to perform their duties and to develop their individual abilities and potentialities.

Principle of People's Livelihood 

In economic affairs, we must practice the principle of people's livelihood as a means of raising the standard of living of the people as a whole. Provisions for national economy and social security in the chapter on fundamental national policies in the new Constitution are based on Dr. Sun Yat-sen's teachings. Consequently, we do not need to look elsewhere today for ways of solving China's economic and social problems. 

Following the establishment of the new Government, I and my colleagues shall exert ourselves with this in view.

On one hand, we should take effective steps to stabilize our currency, balance our national budget, regulate credit and banking and improve means of transportation in order to force the commodity prices to level off and to save our people from their plight. On the other, we should steadily effect land reforms, protect tenant farmers, extend farm credit loans, all for the purpose of equalizing land ownership. We should also eliminate speculation and tax excessive profits. 

Meanwhile, we should extend cooperatives, encourage agricultural and industrial production, protect legitimate business enterprises and welcome foreign investment. Thus more goods will become available to the people.

It has been our consistent view that the two principal goals of the Three People's Principles are political freedom and economic equality. Among the first tasks to which the new Government should apply itself is to move toward them through adopting concrete plans and employing practical and definite steps. We hold it to be self-evident that the interests of the state are also the interests of the great majority of the people. 

This will be the new Government's guiding policy in domestic affairs. 

Support the United Nations

As to the new Government's foreign policy, I propose to explain it in three parts: 

First, concerning the United Nations. Following its establishment, the new Government's first task, insofar as foreign policy is concerned, is to continue to support the United Nations and to strengthen it. 

Article 141 of our Constitution clearly prescribes China's obligations vis-a-vis the United Nations. 

We fought against the Axis powers for eight years and made heavy sacrifices because we had hoped that the ideal of world peace as signified by the United Nations would become a reality. It is true that the 

United Nations is as yet not powerful enough to maintain world peace. Because of the numerous setbacks the United Nations has encountered during the past two years, many have become dubious about its future. In fact, some have even begun to question whether the UN is worth having. On the contrary, because of the existence of such misgivings, we should redouble our efforts to sustain and strengthen it. 

Insofar as China is concerned, we did not bear the principal responsibility for the failure of the League of Nations. However, if the United Nations should unfortunately fail too, we cannot evade our share of the responsibility to mankind and to posterity. This is because China was one of its sponsor nations and has been a permanent member in its Security Council. 

Policy of International Cooperation 

Second, concerning international cooperation in general. Since the surrender of Germany and Japan, relations the Allied Powers have deteriorated.

among In the place of cooperation, there is today mutual suspicion. The Chinese people are greatly disturbed over this state of affairs. China's policy, however, has been one of peace and cooperation. In dealing with any issue requiring international cooperation, we shall never be guilty of self-seeking or of neglecting the world's need for peace, prosperity and progress. 

Economically speaking, our industries and commerce cannot yet compare with those of other countries. 

However, this shall not deter us from participating in international trade and tariff agreements.

In political matters, though we still have an armed rebellion at home and, though the general educational standards of the nation are still comparatively low, we have not hesitated to participate in the Freedom of Information Conference and other projects calling for international cooperation in the social and cultural fields.

We have regarded peace and cooperation as two of the highest ideals in international relations since the

end of the recent war. The Chinese people, both those at home and others residing abroad, have maintained happy and cordial relations with the peoples of other nations. In the spirit of equality and reciprocity, they have constantly endeavored to promote friendship and mutual respect.

It is true that for our economic recovery we shall need international assistance. But we fully realize that only through self-help could we make international aid really effective. Following the establishment of the new Government, we shall continue to adhere to our policy of international cooperation, and shall carry out a program of self-help at the same time. 

Magnanimity Toward Japan 

Third, concerning China's attitude toward the conclusion of peace treaties. At the time of the Japanese surrender, I declared that China would not seek revenge for what Japan had inflicted on the Chinese people. It has been my belief that, either toward Germany or toward Japan, members of the United Nations should one and all adopt a policy of magnanimity. This should not be construed as a sign of weakness. 

On the contrary, reasonable magnanimity points the road to the attainment of our lofty ideals. 

In connection with Japan, we believe two points deserve attention. One, the Allied powers should do their best to foster the growth of truly democratic forces in Japan, so that there could be a genuine change in that nation's political and social systems and in the thoughts of the Japanese people with a view to uprooting Japanese militarism. Whether our policy of magnanimity will prove fruitful depends on whether our efforts are realistic. Two, China has no excessive demands to make of Japan. After having suffered from Japanese aggression for more than eight years, China cannot but ask the other powers to recognize that she is entitled to a special position when the Japanese peace terms are to be decided.

The three points mentioned represent the major policies in our external relations. As Dr. Sun Yat-sen has pointed out to us, the Chinese people should discharge their duties and enjoy their rights as befitting at civilized nation. This means that internationally we should seek to strengthen ourselves by our own efforts and externally to secure equality for China in the community of nations and at the same time to offer cooperation to the other nations. In this spirit, our country shall make its contributions toward upholding international righteousness and the preservation of world peace.

Turning Point in Nation's History 

In assuming office today, I realize that at home we have yet to suppress an armed rebellion and to stabilize our economy, and that in the world at large solid foundations for peace have yet to be laid. I cannot deny that the path before China is beset with troubles. Much less can I deny that the task of rebuilding our nation is a difficult one. Our basic conditions for democracy are still deficient. But the forces of the times have already pushed us onward on the road to constitutional democracy. No matter how many obstacles may block our way, we have to move forward. 

My experience, after having been in the service of the nation for the last forty years, has led me to be lieve firmly in Dr. Sun Yat-sen's axiom: "Success will come to any measure that conforms with the natural laws, that follows that course of human understanding, that keeps in step with the world trends, that answers the people's needs, and that has been discovered by men of super-intelligence." Therefore, with the utmost sincerity and determination, I am accepting this sacred charge of saving the country from peril, rebuilding it and fostering the growth of democracy in the country. It is my hope that leaders in all walks of life, civilians and soldiers alike, will demonstrate unity of purpose and faithfully perform their new duties. In particular, I hope they will give me and my colleagues in the new Government faithful counsel and assistance so that the Republic's unity, independ ence, liberty and equality may rest on a solid foundation. I shall always make the people's interest and the state's welfare my own. I shall always welcome counsel, and I shall loyally discharge my duties so that the task before us all can be successfully accomplished. 

This is a great turning point in the history of our nation. Personal success or failure, nay even life or death, no longer enter into my consideration. My only wish is to serve the state and people, for this has been my lifelong ambition.

HARVARD LAW LIBRAR NEW YORK, N. Y.

Published Twice Monthly by Chinese News Service, An Agency of the Chinese Government 

30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y. 

JUNE 15, 1948 

No. 11 

China's Efforts at Opium Suppression

Ever since the Opium War a century ago the Chinese Government has been endeavoring to eradicate the production of and suppress the evil of consuming narcotic drugs. The effort of the Chinese Government was particularly successful in the late Twenties and early Thirties-during the decade before the out break of the Second World War. When the Japanese occupied first the Northeastern Provinces and then the Northern Provinces in the course of the war, they deliberately increased the production and encouraged the consumption of opium in those areas. Consequently today the menace of opium has become greater than ever before.

Since the end of the war the Chinese Government has redoubled its effort to terminate the menace of opium. The work is being done by the Narcotics Commission under the Ministry of the Interior. The 

Commission has established fourteen branch offices throughout the country. Under these offices there are 

494 inspection units and 362 opium clinics. 

It is realized that narcotic drugs are not only a national problem, but an international problem of a very serious nature. This is why the United Nations, as soon as it came into being, established under the Economic and Social Council a Commission on Narcotic Drugs, to which the powers and functions formerly exercised by the League of Nations under international conventions on narcotic drugs were transferred. China has been a member of the Commission since its inception.

The Commission held its third session from May 3 to 21 recently at Lake Success. Dr. C. L. Hsia, China's alternate representative on the Security Council, served as Chinese member of the Commission. 

General Conditions in China

According to the reports of the Ministry of Interior, the production of opium in China was being reduced greatly. In 1947 the Government discovered a total of 719,390 mow (1/6 of an acre) of land throughout the provinces on which opium was produced. As a result the Government has eradicated 

1,576,000 opium poppies and turned 719,390 mow of land to the production of food stuff. 

Also in the same year the Government arrested 18,780 persons who smoked opium, 1,965 persons who sold opium, and 65 persons who manufactured opium alkaloids.

There is a great deal of illicit traffic in opium. In 

1947 the Government reported 1,395 cases of illicit traffic, involving 2,080 persons.

Illicit traffic in narcotic drugs has been going on not only inside China, but also between China and foreign countries. In 1947 the Chinese Customs reported 156 cases of illicit traffic in narcotic drugs which originated in several countries. 

Control and Limitation of Opium 

One of the most important matters before the Commission on Narcotic Drugs was the calling of an international conference on the control and limitation of the production of raw opium. Some preparatory work has already been done by the League of Nations and the Government of the United States. 

Speaking before the recent session of the Commission on Narcotic Drugs at Lake Success on two separate occasions, Dr. Hsia urged the calling at the earliest possible time of an international conference for the control and limitation of raw opium as an essential step toward world control and regulation of narcotic drugs.

Pending the calling of such a conference, the Chinese delegate appealed for an interim commodity agreement between the main opium producing and

consuming countries to limit the production and trade of opium in a way consistent with the world's legitimate needs. To hasten the operation of this interim measure, he proposed that the agreement be drawn up and executed in such a form as to preclude the necessity for protracted legislative action. As a basis for discussion by the Conference, Dr. Hsia suggested that the Governments submit information that would present a complete picture of the opium situation of the entire world.

On May 17 the Chinese delegate proposed that recommendations be forwarded to the Economic and Social Council for a definite plan. 

Recommendation by Narcotic Commission 

The Commission was in general in favor of the suggestion of the Chinese member, and instructed the Secretary-General to initiate studies for the purpose of reaching an interim commodity agreement limiting the production of raw opium. The Commission decided to recommend the adoption by the Economic and Social Council of the following resolution: 

"The Economic and Social Council, 

"Requests the Secretary-General to initiate studies and inquiries on the desirability of convening a conference of the opium-producing countries and of countries using opium in the manufacture of drugs for medical and scientific needs for the purpose of reaching an interim commodity agreement limiting the production and exports of opium to these needs, pending the adoption of an international convention on limitation of raw materials used in the manufacture of narcotic drugs. "The results of these studies and inquiries should be submitted to the next session of the 

Commission on Narcotic Drugs." Instrument of Genocide 

Dr. P. C. Chang, China's representative on the Economic and Social Council, suggested that the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Genocide, which was under consideration by the United Nations, should include narcotic drugs as an instrument of genocide and make the use of such instrument a crime under international law.

The attention of the Commission on Narcotic Drugs was called to the fact that during the Second World War the Japanese built a huge opium extraction plant in Mukden, which could process some 400 tons of opium annually, producing fifty tons of heroin-at least fifty times the legitimate requirements of the world. This quantity, according to some medical authorities, would be enough to administer lethal doses to 200,000,000 to 400,000,000 persons.

In view of the seriousness of this crime, the Chinese and Polish representatives on the Commission on Narcotic Drugs proposed resolutions which would request the Economic and Social Council to include narcotic drugs as an instrument of genocide in the proposed Convention on Genocide. The resolution adopted by the Commission, which is a compromise of the Chinese and Polish proposals, reads as follows: 

"The Commission on Narcotic Drugs, 

"Having considered document E/CN.7/W.28, submitted by the representative of the United States, giving details of the factory for the manufacture of narcotic drugs built by the Japanese authorities in Mukden during the Japanese occupation of Mukden, and taking particularly into account the manufacturing capacity of that factory, which in the opinion of an expert consulted by the United States representative was 50,000 kilos of heroin annually, an amount which represents 50 times the amount of the annual legitimate needs of the world for this drug; "Having been profoundly shocked by the fact that the Japanese occupation authorities in Northeastern China utilized narcotic drugs during the recent war for the purpose of undermining the resistance and impairing the physical and mental well-being of the Chinese people; "Considering that narcotic drugs constituted and may constitute in the future a powerful instrument of the most hideous crime against mankind; "Recommends that the Economic and Social

Council ensure that the use of narcotics as an instrument of committing a crime of this nature be covered by the proposed Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Genocide."

Imported in the 8th Century 

Although opium first found its way into China in the 8th century, opium smoking became a habit in the country only in the 18th century. Following repeated but unsuccessful efforts at uprooting the evil, the National Government, soon after its establishment in 1927 in Nanking, initiated a policy of “absolute suppression," prohibiting unconditionally the cultivation, transportation, sale, and use of opium. 

Laws and regulations for the arrest and punishment of the offenders were promulgated. June 3rd was named Opium Suppression Day. 

In the absence of marked progress, the policy of "absolute suppression" was changed to one of “gradual suppression" in 1935. Generalissimio Chiang Kaishek, chairman of the National Military Council, assumed the concurrent post of Director-General of

Opium Suppression, and the administrative responsibility fell upon the newly created Central Commission for Opium Suppression under the Military Council.

Under the new policy, the Government allowed two years for the suppression of narcotic drugs and six years, opium. The production, transportation, sale, and consumption were regulated and controlled by the Government. All laws and regulations pertain- 

Opium Suppression, by Kan Nai-kuang, in the Chinese Year Book, 

1937 issue.

ing to opium suppression were revised in order to conform with the time-table.

Poppy Cultivation Suppressed 

The new policy brought about gratifying results. 

Progress was particularly noted in the suppression of poppy cultivation. Absolute suppression was achieved one year after the enforcement of the new policy in the following twelve provinces: Honan, Hupeh, Anhwei, Kiangsi, Fukien, Kiangsu, Chekiang, Hopei,

Shantung, Chahar, Shansi, and Hunan. In the following five provinces, poppy was not being cultivated: 

Kwangtung, Kwangsi, Chinghai, Sikang, and Sinkiang.

The campaign of suppressing poppy cultivation therefore narrowed down to activities in the seven provinces of Szechwan, Yunnan, Kweichow, Shensi, Kansu, Suiyuan, and Ninghsia. In two years, poppy cultivation was eliminated in 144 out of 148 counties in Szechwan, and in 85 out of 129 counties in Yunnan.

Szechwan and Yunnan were the two largest opium growing provinces in China. In Kweichow, complete suppression was achieved in the same period in 35 out of 81 counties; in Shensi, 73 out of 91 counties; and in 

Kansu, 32 out of 67 counties.

There were altogether seventeen opium growing counties in Suiyuan before 1935. Following the enforcement of the new policy, the authorities divided each of the seventeen counties into five sections for gradual elimination. At the rate of stamping out one section each year, the elimination of poppy growing in Suiyuan was to be completed in 1939. 

In Ninghsia, 298,900 mow of poppy growing land in seven counties in 1934 was reduced to 142,800 mow in 1936. The remaining area was to be eliminated between 1937 and 1939.

Interior Ministry Took Over 

Following the outbreak of the war in 1937, Generalissimo Chiang relinquished the concurrent post of Director-General of Opium Suppression. The ad ministration passed from the National Military Council to the Ministry of Interior. Between 1937 and 

1939 the six provinces, Kansu, Ninghsia, Szechwan, Kweichow, Shensi, and Yunnan reported complete elimination of poppy growing. According to a 1935 statistic, areas of poppy cultivation in these six provinces aggregated 2,700,000 mow. Instead of raising poppy, this huge farm area began to yield foodstuffs, cotton, tea, tung oil trees, tobacco, and other agricultural products. In Shensi alone, foodstuffs and cotton, rather than poppy, became the produce of over 500,000 mow of cultivated land.

The spreading of fighting, coupled with Japan's narcotic policy in occupied areas, which openly encouraged the production, transportation, sale, and consumption of opium, seriously undermined the program of opium suppression in Free China. 

Special commissioners charged with the specific duty of detecting poppy cultivation were sent to the following provinces: Honan, Hupeh, Anhwei, Kiangsi,

Hunan, Fukien, Szechwan, Shensi, Kansu, and Yun- In provinces and districts where no special commissioners were deputed, the provincial authorities and magistrates were held responsible. 

Registration of Smokers

To ascertain the amount of opium supply neces sary for the treatment of opium addiction, the Government began the registration of opium smokers in 

1935. As of March, 1937, the number of registered smokers totaled 3,628,162. By the end of 1937, the number reached 4,160,285. 

To each of the registered smokers, a permit was given which enabled him to obtain his prescribed amount from the designated retailer. According to the regulations, one-fifth of the registered smokers were summoned each year for treatment. Those below the age of 40, or less addicted, were to be summoned first. By this method, it was calculated that all the registered smokers would be cured before 1940.

Smokers were permitted to take voluntary treatment in anti-opium hospitals and clinics. Failure to do so would result in compulsory treatment. Cured addicts were subject to periodic medical examinations. 

To accommodate addicts, the Government ordered all provincial governments and municipalities in 1936 to provide hospitals. It was stipulated that in any one place where registered addicts numbered 10,000, an anti-opium hospital of not less than 100 beds must be installed. If the number of addicts should fall below 2,500, then an anti-opium clinic with a proportionate number of beds must be maintained. 

In addition to the hospitalization facilities, the Government ordered the provision of vocational training for cured addicts. Meanwhile, relief measures were devised for the destitute cases.

Large scale migration resulting from widespread hostilities rendered the records of a large number of registered addicts obsolete. In 1940, the target year originally set for curing all registered smokers, the Ministry of the Interior instructed the provincial and municipal authorities to take adequate measures, in spite of wartime handicaps, to press the campaign to completion. Addicts who could not be cured before the conclusion of the campaign were held personally responsible for seeking voluntary treatment. Meanwhile, medical examination of cured addicts was intensified to prevent relapse. 

In 1937, opium-smoking offenders arrested numbered 

35,075; in 1938, 7,064; in 1939, 7,068; in 1940, 20,415; in 1941, 20,421; in 1942, 8,621; in 1943, 2,897; in 1944, 2,918; in 1945, 2,617.*

Transportation of Opium 

During the period of gradual elimination, the Opium Suppression Supervisory Bureau was established at Hankow to take complete charge of the transportation and distribution of the necessary supply of the drug for registered smokers. The Bureau had under it suboffices located at various important 

* Pamphlet in Chinese_on Opium Suppression by the Chinese Govern ment Information Office, July, 1947.

centers. Preventive forces were organized to help achieve effective control of the widely scattered opium producing areas, to prevent the producers from selling their produce to unscrupulous merchants, and to check narcotics smuggling to and from abroad. The preventive forces were made up of provincial and municipal police, the Customs guards, the postal authorities and the preventive officers and patrols of the railways. In addition, the Bureau maintained a special force known as the Preventive Department to check smuggling.

For better supervision and control of opium transportation, the Bureau specified certain routes the movements of such cargo must adhere to. Raw opium was sent to specified storage centers from where it was distributed to dealers who sold to the registered addicts on the presentation of permits.

The Opium Suppression Supervisory Bureau was abolished at the end of 1940.

Suppression Campaign After War 

Following the conclusion of the six-year "gradual suppression" campaign, the Government reverted to the "absolute suppression" policy. Relentless efforts were continued in Free China in the detection of poppy growing, in examining cured addicts, and in general, in clamping down on the evil.

The close of the war found the Government confronted with an alarming situation in areas formerly occupied by the Japanese. Here, anti-opium hospitals and clinics were ordered established, and all responsible officials were instructed to suppress poppy cultivation in areas under their respective administration. 

A six-month period was set for all addicts to be rid of their habit.

In conformity with the Government's post-war twoyear program for the eradication of the scourge in all China, provincial and municipal governments listed opium suppression as one of their principal administrative tasks.

In 1946, 272 cases of poppy cultivation were prosecuted in twelve different provinces and a total of 

662,925.20 mow of poppy fields were converted to food-producing land.

Up to June, 1947, a total of 111 anti-opium hospitals and clinics were functioning in eleven provinces and four municipalities in addition to 417 other hospitals which also offered anti-opium treatment. 

Of the 192,820 registered opium smokers in eight provinces and four municipalities, 85,822 were cured of their addiction. 

Red Rebellion Hinders Drive 

Opium suppression in the Northeast and North

China, which served as the nerve center of the Japanese narcotic policy, was, however, stymied almost from the outset by the widespread Communist military operations. Except for such cities as Peiping,

Tientsin, Tsingtao, and a few counties in Hopei, where the use of opium was sharply curtailed, the Government's anti-opium campaign there had to be suspended.

The Government effort has suffered a further blow as a result of the rebel's encouragement of poppy cultivation in territories under their control. To cope with this situation, the Government promulgated a set of regulations providing that, within ten months after the recapture of any Communist-occupied area, effects of the Communist-promoted drug policy should be wiped out. Poppy fields are to be summarily suppressed and the farmers are to be encouraged to produce foodstuffs instead. Severe punishment is to be meted out to those who disregard the ruling. 

In the case of addiction, the Government regulations provide that anti-opium hospitals and clinics be set up within the first month of the recapture of any Communist area. All smokers are to be registered and they are to be cured of their habit within six months of their registration.

Wang Yi-lin, a spokesman of the Ministry of Interior, charged that, in the Communist occupied areas, poppy cultivation has been widely encouraged. The drug is generally smuggled into Government areas in exchange for essential materials. Poppy cultivation was particularly prevalent in western Shantung. A survey of the recovered areas shows that some 20 per cent of the former Communist occupied areas grew poppies. The Communists were reported to have collected an average of 80 per cent of the yield as tax. 

Despite this heavy tax, many farmers were willing to cultivate poppy because of the high profit and light labor.

The effect of the Communist drug policy has been widespread. The spokesman cited one case in Peiping where the police discovered on March 14 of this year a big narcotics processing plant. The twelve persons arrested all confessed that they had been receiving raw opium from the Communists. The manufactured narcotics were sold in Taiyuan, Sian or Shanghai for gold with which they bought telegraph, i.e., communication or automobile parts to be sold to the 

Communists at a great profit. 

Reiterates Government Policy

On the occasion of the Opium Suppression Day on 

June 3 this year, President Chiang Kai-shek reiterated the Government's determination to rid China of the evil. He severely denounced the drug policy which the Japanese promoted during their occupation of the 

Northeast and North China and which the Communists are continuing. He called upon the Government authorities and the entire citizenry to fight the evil. 

According to the Opium Suppression Commission, of the 365,924 registered opium smokers in eighteen provinces and municipalities, 218,959 were completing their treatment at the end of 1947.

JULY 1, 1948 

Water Conservancy and Flood Control 

Torrential rain, rising waters, and seasonal typhoon are once more taking their heavy toll of men, property and crops in several parts of China, according to recent reports. In Hunan, 11 counties around the 

Tungting Lake have been inundated. Rice-producing land flooded is estimated at 1,800 square kilometers (1 square kilometer equals to .3861 square mile or 

247.104 acres), affecting the livelihood of more than 

3,000,000 people. In Kwangtung, hundreds have lost their lives and thousands have been rendered homeless by rain storms and the swollen East, North and West Rivers. In the other coastal province of Fukien, over a thousand people have perished and 200,000 have been deprived of their property and residence when the Min River rose violently to overrun thickly populated parts of Foochow, the provincial capital. In some provinces traversed by the unpredictable Yellow River, watch is being vigilantly kept for signs of overflowing and the weakening in the long dykes. 

Besides directing local authorities in extending emergency relief in the afflicted parts of the country, the Government has, at tremendous cost, steadily pursued a long-range program of flood control and water conservancy like sealing breaches in the dykes, erecting and reinforcing dams and sea embankments, dredging, reforestation, etc. The Chinese Communists have jeopardized and slowed down several projects but progress has been made in spite of their obstruction and subversion. 1 

Water Conservancy Since V-J Day ¹ 

Immediately after the V-J Day, China embarked on a program of repairing and rebuilding dykes that had been neglected during the long war years. Funds for these undertakings were provided by the Government out of rehabilitation and relief appropriations. The 

UNRRA and CNRRA supplied machines and materials, as well as food for the laborers. As a result, all necessary repairs on the major dykes along the Yellow 

1 Hsueh Tu-pi: "Water Conservancy," The China Magazine, October, 1947, pp. 44-49. 

No. 12 

River, the Yangtze River, the Hwai River, the Pearl

River and the sections of the Grand Canal, together with the rebuilding of sea embankments along the Kiangsu and Chekiang coasts, were completed before the high water season in 1946. Meanwhile, at Choukiaow on the Shan River in Central Honan, and on the Yangtze banks near Shashih in Hupeh, the danger of impending dyke breaks and floods was averted by timely prevention measures.2

Yellow River Returned to Its Old Course 3 The most important water conservancy project undertaken in China after V-J Day is the closing of the Yellow River breach at Huayuankou in Honan.

The project involved more than 27,000 workers and the use of 3,704,653 cubic meters (1 cubic meter equals to 35.314 cubic feet) of earth and stone.

After flooding the fertile Honan-Anhwei-Kiangsu plain for nearly 9 years, the Yellow River was finally pushed back to its pre-1938 course via the northern Shantung flatland to the Gulf of Chihli. The breach of the river dyke at Huayuankou in eastern Honan was closed on March 14, 1947, eight years and nine months after its collapse in June, 1938, as a result of an engagement fought in that vicinity between the 

Chinese and Japanese forces.

The sealing of the Yellow River gap prevented the further inundation of nearly 2,800 square kilometers of fertile farmland in eastern Honan, northern Anhwei, and northern Kiangsu, one of China's most important wheat belts. It freed some 6,100,000 Chinese farmers from the scourge of flood and destruction. 

It enabled the return of some 1,000,000 displaced persons to their homes which for years were covered by the waters of the Yellow River. It saved the Hwai 

Water Conservancy Administration (in Chinese), published by Government Information Office, Nanking, China in Dec. 1947, pp. 14-21. 

See (a) Chinese News Service Release SA: 333 April 16, 1947; (b) Closing of the Yellow River Dyke (in Chinese), published by Government Information Office, Nanking, China, July, 1947; 

(c) Oliver J. Todd: "Taming 'Flood Dragons' Along China's 

Hwang Ho," National Geographic Magazine, February, 1942; (d) "China's Sorrow," Life, July 1, 1946.

River, the northern Kiangsu section of the Grand Canal and even the mighty Yangtze from the disastrous effect of accumulating Yellow River silt. 

The huge project, undertaken jointly by the Chi nese National Water Conservancy Commission, UNRRA, CNRRA, and the Honan Provincial Gov ernment, started on March 1, 1946, and cost the Chinese National Treasury some 650 billion Chinese dollars, not counting contributions made by the local Honan people and material and equipment supplied by the UNRRA. Twenty American experts, under the leadership of Oliver J. Todd of California, helped in the direction of the work. For a year and 14 days, thousands upon thousands of Chinese farmers fought the turbulent stream with both time-honored Chinese methods and new scientific American technique to return it to its old course, and won. Huayuankou Breach 

The Huayuankou breach measured about 1.2 kilometers (1 kilometer equals to 0.621 mile) wide when the work started. Six main barriers or dams were erected to stem the rushing current, while the western section of the dyke was being extended to meet the eastern section. The barriers were completed in June, 1946, only to be swept away by the summer flood in 

August. Failure stalked the second attempt, making 

6 months' hard work and the expenditure of 28 billion Chinese dollars a total loss.

The project was started all over again, on a modified pattern, in the following winter when water was low. 

Again, there was no success. Finally, the foreign experts discarded orthodox western methods and adopted the old Chinese way of holding back the waters by blocking it. Where the new dyke had not been washed away, rocks and stones were heaped up, leaving a reduced gap to be sealed later. At the same time, methods of building the dams were also revised to meet the peculiar Yellow River current. 

When all that remained to be done was the sealing of the gap in February, 1947, more than 27,000 workers were mobilized. Work was rushed day and night for a whole month. Wires were strung up to to provide a suspension bridge for workers. A wooden bridge was built to facilitate transportation of material. The sealing was done with massive piles of stones in huge willow baskets, tied together with ropes and wires before they were dropped into the river. Huge iron cages, imported from the United States and filled with rocks and stones, were then lowered into the gap. 

The breach was reduced to ten feet on March 12 and completely closed on the afternoon of March 14. 

Communist Obstruction

The closing of the Yellow River dyke breach would have been completed earlier had not the Chinese Communists injected into this purely technical water conservancy project military and political implications. 

The Communists, who had as one of their main bases the Hopei-Shantung plain through which the Yellow River's pre-1938 course flowed, did not want the river to come back for military reasons. A mighty river like the Yellow River would constitute a water barrier cutting the Communist base into two if it should return to its old course. Such a barrier would also mean a great military handicap for the highly mobile Communist troops. Therefore, the Communists tried every means to delay the gap-sealing work at Huayuankou. 

At first they used a political alibi saying that there were some 500,000 farmers settled in the Yellow River bed since 1938. Accepting this argument, the 

Government promised to pay a total of 15 billion Chinese dollars to facilitate the resettlement of these farmers. The Communists, however, held up the distribution of the relief money and the removal of the farmers, accusing the Government meanwhile of try ing to displace these people. 

Seeing that the Government was determined to return the river to its old channel, freeing thereby sev eral million people from the menace of flood and destruction, the Communists resorted to force to stop the Huayuankou gap-closing project. In the winter of 

1946-1947, installations north of Huayuankou were destroyed by Communist raiders while material and equipment were repeatedly plundered and workers. killed or kidnapped by Communist armed groups.

Despite Communist threats and obstructions, however, the Honan farmers collaborated heartily in putting the river back to its old course. 

Yellow River Basin

Embracing a total drainage basin of 756,000 square kilometers, the Yellow River travels 4,000 kilometers from west to east through nine provinces and affects the livelihood of 140,000,000 people, nearly one-fourth of China's population. To harness effectively the mischievous river, the National Government has a project to find a fundamental “cure” for the river. Even when the work of the breach-sealing was going on at Huay. uankou, a group of Chinese and American engineers, under the leadership of the American expert, John L. Savage, were making ground and aerial surveys along the river. It was their task to map out a concrete program to change "China's Sorrow" to "China's Blessing."

The sections of Yellow River in Honan, Hopei, and 

Shantung Provinces add up to approximately 1,400 kilometers. Repairing of the dykes along these sections of the river was to have been completed before the closing of the gap at Huayuankou. Unfortunately, the projects were repeatedly disrupted by the Chinese Communists. Constructions which have been completed since V-J Day in these three provinces despite 

Communist interference are as follows: (1) In Honan, more than 5,000,000 cubic meters of dyke works were put up by the end of June, 1947. On the river's south bank, about 70% of repair work has thus far been completed. (2) In Hopei, about 1,500,000 cubic meters were built on both banks of the Yellow River before the end of June, 1947. More than 90% of the con struction works on the south bank was completed. (3) In Shantung, about 1,000,000 cubic meters were constructed before the end of May, 1947, with more than 90% of the urgent repairs in western Shantung completed by August of the same year.

In a joint endeavor to prevent further floods, the 

Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry has reforested 

43 square kilometers of land formerly inundated by the Yellow River, 36 square kilometers in Honan and the rest in Anhwei. The project was made possible by the UNRRA contribution of Ch$642,300,000 and 600 tons of wheat for feeding the workers. The project began in April, 1947 and was completed before the end of the same year. The Ministry plans in 4 years to complete the forestation of the entire area reclaimed from the Yellow River.

Work Along the Yangtze 4 

The Yangtze River is the largest river in China, which runs from west to east more than 6,000 kilometers and waters an area of 1,959,000 square kilometers, approximately one-sixth of China. The river affects the livelihood of one-half of China's population. Since 1931, China has made special efforts along the river to control flood and reclaim land. Up to the outbreak of Sino-Japanese War in 1937, 2,872 kilometers of dykes had been built along the river which steadily deteriorated during the Japanese occupation. 

Immediately after the V-J Day, a program of dykerepairing along the Yangtze River was worked out by the National Water Conservancy Commission. The project involved about 16,000 square kilometers of farmland in the Yangtze valley in the provinces of Hunan, Kiangsi, Anhwei and Kiangsu and the city of Nanking. The yearly production of foodstuff in the region was about 2,500,000 tons. As a result of the program of flood prevention and land reclamation, the yearly production in the area was expected to increase by 1,650,000 tons. Under this program a total of 2,840 kilometers of dykes was to be built in four provinces and one municipality. 

Dykes in Hupeh Province

Considerable work has also been done along the Yangtze River and its tributary, the Han River, in Hupeh. In this province, there are a total of 5,311 kilometers of dykes along the two waterways. These bulwarks protect 50 cities, 48,000 square kilometers of farmland and 10,000,000 people. Dyke-repairing in 

Hupeh started immediately after the V-J Day, with 5,900,000 cubic meters of earthwork constructed in 

1946 and additional 3,100,000 cubic meters completed in 1947.

Among the most important projects undertaken in Hupeh were the closing of the dyke at Chuchiawan and the reinforcement of the dykes at Chihchiayuan, Chaochiate and Mienchow. In August, 1945 when

Hupeh was still under Japanese occupation, the dyke at Chuchiawan of Kunganhsien in southern Hupeh collapsed. As a result, 21,000 homes in the area were washed away, with 22,600 people drowned and 267,000 people made homeless. An area of 425 square kilometers with approximately 300 square kilometers of farmland was inundated. Repair work started on January 4, 1946 and was completed on May 25, 1946. 

4 Repairing Work of Dykes Along the Rivers of Yangtze, Ruan and Pai and Their Tributaries (in Chinese), published by Government Information 

Office, Nanking, China in November, 1947.

It cost the Government about Ch$135,000,000 but it stood up well in July, 1946 when the water level of the river registered a new high. 

The dyke of Chihchiayuan, 30 kilometers east of

Shashih in southern Hupeh, is the only one which protects the districts of Kiangning, Chienli, Mienyang and Hanyang. In the flood season of 1946, a part of the dyke collapsed, resulting in a breach of 400 meters wide. Fortunately, the breach was plugged immediately by emergency units and the dyke was reinforced after the flood season was over. The reinforcing job began on February 26, 1947 and was completed on 

June 25, 1947, costing Ch$469,441,990.

At Chaochiate in Kingmen in western Hupeh, dyke reconstruction started on February 19, 1947 and was finished on June 15, 1947. It cost Ch$410,797,600.

The Mienchow dyke in southern Hupeh was reinforced with stone and bamboo between March 1 and 

June 28, 1947, costing Ch$299,699,874.5

The YVA Plan 6 

During the war, Dr. John Lucian Savage of the

United States Bureau of Reclamation was invited by the Chinese Government to investigate the hydroelectric potentiality of the Yangtze River. After a thorough survey, Dr. Savage was convinced that the 

Yangtze gorges were one of the best sites for hydraulic power projects, and he conceived the well-known 

YVA (Yangtze Valley Administration) plan. 

Dr. Savage chose Pingshanpah, some 15 kilometers west of Ichang, as the site of a dam which is the key structure of the Yangtze gorge project. When completed, the dam will rise 228 meters in the Yangtze and create a 402-kilometer reservoir to control gorges the devastating Yangtze floods and irrigate 41,700 square kilometers of farmland. It would permit oceangoing ships to travel all the way to Chungking. It would have a lock or ship-hoist 167 meters high, whereas the biggest ship lock of today lifts a vessel a maximum of 15 or 18 meters. The envisaged 96 turbogenerators in the 24 tunnels drilled into solid rock in the gorges would be capable of generating 10,560,000 kilowatts of electricity, almost four times as much power as that generated by TVA, five times that of Grand Coulee and eight times of Boulder. If the plan is realized, not only Hankow and Wanhsien, but cities as far as Chungking and Nanking would enjoy cheap supplies of electricity. In the power distribution area live 

140,000,000 people, as many as the total population of the United States. It will be certainly a "Super TVA." 8 

Because of financial difficulties, the Yangtze Valley Administration has been temporarily suspended but not abandoned, according to a statement by Dr. Wong 

Wen-hao, then Chairman of the National Resources 

Commission, on December 16, 1947.9

Repairing Work on Dykes Along The Rivers of Yangtze, Ruan and Pai and Their Tributaries, pp. 9-28.

Yungting, Taching, Tzeyah, Pai 10

Under Japanese occupation, flood control in Hopei was entirely neglected during the war years. To remedy the situation, the Dyke Reconstruction Engineering Bureau was established in Hopei by the Government in 1946.

The dam at Lukouchiao, which diverts a part of the Yungting River waters from its upper reaches into the Shouching River, was built in the Ching Dynasty at the turn of the century. A part of the dam was washed in the autumn of 1945 and the breach away was widened in the spring of 1946. In June the breach was sealed after 20 days of hard exertions.

The flow of Yungting River slows down considerably south of Lukouchiao, but it is entirely held by dykes on both banks because the river bed is 3 or 4 meters above the surrounding land level. Should the dykes collapse, it would be extremely difficult to push the river back to its original course. During the war, the dykes collapsed at Liangkehchuang and two other places in 1939 and 1942, flooding more than 420 square kilometers of land. Rebuilding has been under way for two years but the breaches have not yet been entirely closed. The repairing project involved the construction of 2,700,000 cubic meters of earth-work. 

The Yungting and Pai Rivers always threaten the city of Tientsin. To protect the municipality, an embankment was built in 1924 in the area south and west of the city. The embankment was damaged in 

1939, immersing most of Tientsin in water. Repairing of the section along the Tientsin-Pukow Railway started in June, 1946 and was completed in August, 1946; and the section from Tientsin-Pukow Railway to South Canal was begun in April, 1947 and completed in June of the same year.

The Hopei plain west of Tientsin is frequently submerged by waters of Taching and Tzeyah. Taching flows from west to east and Tzeyah from south to north. They meet at a point west of Tientsin. In 1939, the Chienli Dyke of Taching and the East and West 

Dykes of Tzeyah collapsed, flooding more than 2,400 square kilometers. After the V-J Day, the Government drew up plans for the rebuilding of the following: (1) 77 kilometers of dykes on the east bank of Tzeyah 

River; (2) 30 kilometers on the west bank of Tzeyah; (3) and 51 kilometers on the south bank of Taching

River. On account of the Communist uprisings, however, only the 77 kilometers on the east bank of Tzeyah were completed in 1947. 

Dyke Construction in Kwangtung

Recently it was reported that plans had been made for spending about US$300,000 for the rebuilding of 22 dykes that protect rich rice fields extending 354 kilometers from Canton along the courses of the Pearl, East and North Rivers. The dykes involved protect land that produces most of the cereal grain grown in 

10 Repairing Work on Dykes Along The Rivers of Yangtze, Ruan and 

Pai and Their Tributaries, pp. 29-36. 

Kwangtung. Floods last year washed away many of the dams, causing starvation. The loss in rice output was almost double the normal 500,000-ton yearly rice deficit of this thickly populated province.¹¹

The flood of last year was Kwangtung's worst in 20 years caused by a month of torrential downpour. 

Altogether 1,375 square kilometers of farmland were deluged and 500,000 people lost their homes.12 

In 1946 and 1947, flood control work covered more than 300 kilometers of dykes along the Pearl River and Hankiang with approximately 4,000,000 cubic meters of earthwork and 30,000 cubic meters of stonework constructed. 

Hwai River and Grand Canal 13

Dyke-repairing along the Hwai River covered approximately 1,000 kilometers from Hunghokow to

Swangkoucheng in northern Kiangsu. The work was started in April, 1946 but was interrupted by the overflowing of the Yellow River.14 Work resumed in November and by July, 1947, 14,800,000 cubic meters of earth-work were constructed .In the same period, 309,- 

578 cubic meters of silt and sand were dredged from the Hwai River and its tributaries. 

In the provinces of Kiangsu and Shantung, repairs on dykes along the Grand Canal covered about 940 kilometers from Tsining, Shantung to Kwahchow, Kiangsu. In the section from Yangchow to Kwahchow in Kiangsu, which is 18 kilometers long, 15,680 cubic meters of earth-work were built between May, 1946 and April, 1947. Along the section from Yangchow to 

Kwaiying in northern Kiangsu, 1,017,608 cubic meters were erected between February and June, 1947. Along the Grand Canal section from Taierhchuang to Hanchuang in southern Shantung, work started in March, 1947 and about 300,000 cubic meters of earthwork were completed by the end of the following June. 

Sea Embankments15

The sea embankments along Chekiang and Kiangsu coast were reinforced in 1946 and 1947. The urgent repairs of the sections of Hanghsien-Haining, Haiyen- 

Pinghu, and Shaohsin-Hsiaoshan in northern Chekiang started in April and were completed in September, 1946. In the areas of Hanghsien, Shaohsien and Hsiaoshan in northern Chekiang, and

Ninghai in eastern Chekiang, six projects of embankment construction were completed in the period from October, 1946 to July, 1947. In southern Kiangsu, rebuilding embankments in the districts of Paoshan, Sungkiang, Taichang and Shangshu started in July, 1946. More than 5,000 meters of embankments were completed by the end of 1946. Another 3,314 meters were to be built in 1947.

CHINA INFORMATION BULLETIN
