JULY 15, 1948 Premier Wong's Administrative Program Mr. Chairman and Members of the Legislative Yuan: 

1 I am here today ¹ to present the administrative program of the Executive Yuan.

First of all, let me point out that our major tasks at this juncture being the implementation of constitutional democracy and the suppression of the Communist rebellion, the Government should give equal attention to both. 

My appointment as President of the first Executive

Yuan under the new Constitution has made me deeply aware of the grave situation confronting the nation and of the earnest hopes which our people at home and our friends abroad have reposed in my administration. I shall naturally give all that is in me to fulfill my duties.

Today our people as a whole yearn for reforms. 

I shall, with utmost resolve, strive to foster a youthful spirit in all government levels. I shall not cringe or dilly-dally. I shall earnestly work for the establishment of democratic institutions in accordance with the Constitution and other pertinent laws. Guided by the 

Three People's Principles and the fundamental national policies stipulated in the Constitution, I shall follow the dictates of the people in my service to the country. I shall formulate plans and enforce them accordingly.

Suppress Communists 

The campaign to crush the rebels and to restore peace and order in the country is of special significance at this moment. Under normal conditions, political parties are not banned in countries where constitutional rule prevails. But in no instance is a political party ever allowed to exceed legal bounds. It cer tainly cannot possess arms, cause local disturbances, dispossess the people or undermine general security. 

¹ Dr. Wong Wen-hao, President of the Executive Yuan, presented his administrative program to the Legislative Yuan on June 11, 1948.

No. 13 

Unfortunately, the Communists in our country have turned out to be a bane to the people and a menace to the state. It is the bounden duty of the Government to suppress them without remorse. A fuller realization of the seriousness of the situation will add power to our campaign against the rebels, thereby ensuring its successful conclusion at an earlier date. For this purpose, we must concentrate our minds and muster our strength, for only thus can concrete measures be really effective. Those bearing upon military needs naturally come first.

Build Up Combat Power 

Militarily, our main course of action will be to increase the combat power of our troops in the Northeast, in the North and in Central China, maintain an adequate living standard throughout the rank and file, tighten their discipline, boost their morale, and heighten their confidence in victory. The point must be driven home that crushing the Communists is the prerequisite for national salvation and reconstruction. 

Simultaneously, local defense forces will be strengthened to fight side by side with the regular troops so as to whittle down the Communists' main force. In this way the victorious completion of the campaign may be hastened. Mopping-up operations will also be intensified against roving Communist rebels to restore stability in various localities. All men in the fighting services should consider it their supreme duty to put down the rebellion quickly. 

Improve Conscription, Local Defense 

Furthermore, positive steps will be taken to improve the system of conscription. The importance of the present campaign will be given wider publicity. New recruits will be better treated and benefits provided for their dependents by law will be extended. Draft irregularities will be curbed. All these have one com-

mon purpose: to guarantee a steady supply of manpower for the armed forces. 

Regarding local defense forces, the Government will enlarge the peace preservation corps in various provinces, increase their pay and furnish them with more arms and ammunition. Intensive efforts will be made to organize and train the militia units, and to impress them of their duty to protect the lives and property of the people. The Government will also give them maximum assistance in the repair, manufacture and purchase of arms. Ex-servicemen will also be encouraged to take part in such efforts.

Simplify Mobilization Laws Mobilization laws and orders should be unified and simplified. In combat areas as well as in places recovered from the Communists, the total war plan must be put into effect in the interest of unity of authority. 

This will make possible full coordination of military, political and economic activities against the rebels. 

It is the hope of our people that the campaign will be brought to a speedy conclusion. It behooves the 

Government, therefore, to pursue its set course with still greater determination.

In the administration of civil affairs, the objectives to seek will be the establishment of an honest and efficient machinery so that it can give effective support to the military efforts against the rebels. Of primary importance is the defraying of military expenditures. 

Normally the national budget should be balanced but during this campaign necessary military outlays must not be curtailed.

Every care and discretion, of course, will be exercised to make sure that every item is actually spent for the purpose meant. The Government will also see to it that all such appropriations will be promptly made to all army units and military institutions and training schools.

Army Requirements Large 

Next, we come to the question of army clothing and provisions. The Government will do its utmost to supply cotton yarn and cloth for the making of uniforms and will try to forestall any shortage. The need for military provisions is large. As our own food production usually is not enough, food constitutes the first item in American aid. To take care of the army's needs the Government has resorted to requisitioning food and compulsory borrowing. It, however, has done so only with great reluctance. Being fully aware of the trouble it is causing the people in making food collections, the Government will call upon competent authorities to issue strict orders to all their subordi. nate organs and agents that there must be no malprac tice and misconduct. In view of their onerous burden, the people should be given every facility in fulfilling their delivery quotas.

Meanwhile, it is the hope of the Government that all of our people will appreciate the importance of army food especially during the present campaign and contribute freely so that this year's food collection goals may be met.

Sound Local Administrations 

Local administrative affairs not only are of immediate concern to the populace but have a far-reaching effect upon the rebel-suppression campaign. Particularly at this moment, we should deliberate on such urgent matters as how civil affairs in war-affected provinces are to mesh with the punitive operations and how provinces away from combat areas are to achieve a higher degree of self-government. The functions and powers of the provincial governments must be extended considerably so that each province can look after its own defense. Since magistrates form the backbone of the civil administration at the county level, they should be carefully selected, be given higher pay and be assured of specified tenure of office so that they can really promote the interests of the people. It is hoped, too, that the system of pao and chia, the lowest administrative units, will be further improved. 

To push the implementation of constitutional rule, the General Principles for Local Self-Government should be enforced at an early date. Even before the 

Constitution was adopted, these principles had been submitted by the Executive Yuan to the State Council for consideration. They are now in the hands of the 

Legislative Yuan. While aiming at instituting local self-government, these principles take into account the need of unifying all administrative decrees. Consequently they have a great deal to do with the political organization of the country.

Receipts and Expenditures 

Next in importance only to military affairs is the matter of finances. The budget for all Government receipts and expenditures for the second half of the year has been prepared by the financial organs and will soon be submitted to the Legislative Yuan. It will have two parts, ordinary and extraordinary. The former will comprise all expenditures for such purposes as are suggested by its very name while the latter will be composed chiefly of expenses necessitated by the military campaign. To cover the emergency outlays of the punitive operations alone, revenue must be increased as much as possible. This is also the way to slow down inflation and prevent further aggravation of the people's hardships. Hence, customs duties, salt tax, commodity tax, direct tax, et cetera, must be thoroughly readjusted. Attention will be paid so that the tax burden will fall equitably on the people at large. 

Where legislative powers are involved, special bills will be drafted and presented to the Legislative Yuan for deliberation and adoption. Actions in this connection, it is hoped, will meet with the sympathetic consideration of members of the Legislative Yuan so that there may be instituted a taxation system fully in keeping with the needs of the day. Besides, the Government will also discreetly use all state-owned materials to augment the source of revenue. 

Tax the Rich

Taxation during the present campaign should be guided by the principle of taxing those who are able

to pay. The richest should pay the most. The way to do this is to enforce the regulations limiting the use of only one name by an individual and to register the people's property. On the basis of this registration, all such levies as property tax, inheritance tax and income tax can be collected. Thus the national coffer will be further replenished without increasing the burden of the people as a whole. Moreover, whatever surplus funds are available could be used to help and improve the livelihood of the needy and to narrow the disparity of wealth in the country. 

Financially, the ways open to us are two: opening up new sources of revenue and reducing outlays. In exploring new sources of revenue we must revamp the taxation systems.

Augment Tax Returns 

First, objectives in tax collection should be specified, and the procedure simplified. Non-essential personnel should be cut down to a minimum while the efficiency of tax-collecting agencies should be heightened. 

Second, since at present the urban populace pay far less in taxes and levies than people in the rural areas, the financial organs concerned should carefully readjust the tax scales in accordance with the actual earning power of the people. 

Third, at a time when commodity prices keep rising the tax rates must be revised periodically. The number of levies must be fixed. Only thus can tax returns be increased. 

Fourth, overlapping tax-collecting agencies must either be amalgamated or abolished. Rigid measures are to be taken particularly against those organs which are spending far more than they are collecting, We shall strive hard to attain the goals set forth above. Concrete measures will be announced from time to time.

Reduce Unessential Spending 

Now we come to the question of national expenditures. During the period of our campaign military outlays naturally dominate the budget. Such spending is admittedly necessary; we must make sure there is no waste. Besides, we must also cut down payments for non-productive purposes. Even appropriations for reconstruction projects must be made only on the basis of their actual progress. In a word, the Government will and should exercise economy in order to reduce unnecessary spending. 1 In recent years there has been a great increase in the number of government employees. Repeated attempts have been made to reduce the government personnel without, however, much result. Those on the payroll find their pay not enough to live on. Yet the aggregate outlay constitutes a drain on the national treasury. And they cannot all be paid adequately as to do so would have taken the entire national revenue. 

This abnormal state of affairs cannot be corrected overnight. Nevertheless, it must be improved by sincere and concerted efforts. It is hoped that all Government organs will recognize the difficulties facing our nation, study their respective duties so that all necessary staff members are properly employed, and all superfluous personnel must be paid off. Thus working efficiency can generally be increased. 

Mere words, we must all realize, will not help in carrying out these methods of opening up new sources of revenue and cutting down expenditures. It is necessary that we proceed with the greatest determination and perseverance.

Names Financial Problems 

In our endeavor to reorganize our finances, we should pay special attention to certain fundamental problems. Let me enumerate the more important ones. 

1. How to effect currency reform so that the new currency backed by an adequate reserve can enjoy public confidence; 

2. How to improve the Government's credit stand-· ing so that bonds can be issued; 

3. How to divert idle capital into productive channels so that it will cease to be a cause of market disturbances; 

4. How to use American aid so that it can effectively meet the pressing needs of the nation and help lay a foundation for national recovery.

All these are keys to the alleviation of the present national crisis. Therefore, the authorities concerned should have the benefit of services of specialists and experts in formulating plans to be carried out step by step at opportune moments.

In short, the Government's financial measures aim first at building a firm basis and secondly at enforcing necessary reforms. It will make determined and persevering efforts towards attaining these two objectives. 

In the meantime, it is hoped that to be successful its endeavors will have the sympathy and support of members of the Legislative Yuan.

We should put special emphasis on three points, namely, (1) monetary and foreign exchange control; (2) export and import control, and (3) stabilization of commodity prices. In fact, all three are inter-related and inter-dependent. Overall planning and coordinated activities are necessary before any good can be accomplished. Following are the concrete objectives in the overall planning:

Six Corrective Measures 

First, the development of our export trade. While continued assistance should be given to the export of all items which constituted our pre-war exports, special encouragement should be accorded such products as cotton yarn and cloth, cement and sugar which have found a place on the export list since the end of the war. In the acquisition of foreign exchange from the 

Government, producers of exportable goods should receive priority within prescribed limits. The newly

revised foreign exchange measures allow reasonable profits to export trade.

Second, encouragement to overseas Chinese remittances. In pre-war years such remittances played a major part in balancing our international payments. 

They have decreased steadily in recent years because of the unfavorable rates of exchange. Now that the exchange rules have been modified an increase in such remittances can be expected. The Government will do its part for their encouragement. During the present crisis, it is hoped overseas Chinese will give increasing aid to their fatherland. 

Third, the prevention of smuggling and the exodus of capital. This is necessary in order to increase the 

Government's income and reduce the outflow of national assets.

Fourth, the securing of critical materials and daily essential articles. The system of rice and flour rationing, in force in five leading cities with considerable success, will be extended gradually both in its scope and in its items. As a further step toward price stabilization, cooperative enterprises will be promoted. 

Fifth, the development of transportation facilities and the reasonable adjustment of freight charges. The rates of public utilities will be fixed in relation to the general commodity index, and steps will be taken to slow down their upward trend.

Sixth, the promotion of agricultural and industrial production. Our aim is production under as near normal conditions as possible in order to achieve a gradual stability of prices. 

Outlines Basic Economic Improvements 

From the fundamental economic standpoint, three things call for prior consideration. They are (1) industrial production and reconstruction of communications; (2) a more equitable distribution of land, and (3) agricultural improvement. Let us take up these points one by one.

During the war of resistance against Japan a fiveyear program for post-war reconstruction was mapped out. At present this program cannot be carried out because of the Communist rebellion and unstable conditions in various parts of the country. It has to be revised in accordance with the needs of the Government campaign for the suppression of the rebellion. 

Plans have to be worked out for various regions of the country because of their changes in conditions. The 

Northeast, for instance, produced a large quantity of coal last summer and, together with the resumption of its iron and steel industries, also produced some rolling stock and locomotives. This output was later disrupted because of Communist attacks. At present, despite the serious military situation there, the production of bituminous coal and electric power in the neighborhood of Mukden is going on as usual. 

In the North many important coal mines have since fallen into Communist hands. But still in operation. are the coal mines at Kailan, the iron and steel works in Peiping, Tientsin and Tangshan, cotton mills and paper factories in Tientsin, textile mills and rubber factories in Tsingtao. All these industries are carrying on without interruption and will be given necessary replacements. Press reports to the effect that the 

Government intends to remove a large number of industrial plants from the North to the South are groundless.

Railroads and tele-communications facilities in various parts of the country have been repeatedly destroyed by the Communists but whenever and wherever circumstances permit services have been conscientiously maintained. In the South, the coal mines south of the Huai River and in eastern China are all turning out large outputs despite the trying conditions under which they are operating. In the Northeast and in the 

North, our men in charge of communications and production were attacked, many captured or killed by the rebels. But wherever possible our men are sticking to their posts.

Today the Government forces are pushing northward from the south, just as during the war against the Japanese, Government forces drove eastward from their bases in the west. As during the last war every effort was made to strengthen bases in the interior and to recover lost territories in the east, so in the present campaign against the rebels the Government has to build up areas in the south so that its troops can score victories in the north. In both cases the emphasis was dictated by actual needs, and this is a fact that should be clear to all.

To Build Up the South 

It is, therefore, in the south where the Government will undertake large reconstruction projects. In concrete terms, the Canton-Hankow and the Chekiang- Kiangsi railways and their feeder lines will be improved and brought up to their pre-war standard. 

Traffic on these lines, though since resumed, is at best still haphazard, due chiefly to the hasty rehabilitation work that was effected. Construction work on the 

Chengtu-Chungking Railway, an important rail line in the hinterland, should be accelerated. Most of the coal deposits in the provinces of Hunan and Kiangsi have yet to be tapped. Then production in the exist ing coal mines, which has been meager because of lack of capital investment, will be increased. The industrial output of Taiwan, though large, can be increased. Besides, equal attention will be given to the building up of industries in the coastal provinces. 

Develop Interior Provinces 

A good foundation for industrial and mining enterprises has already been laid in the interior provinces and this initial advantage will be maintained by all means. In the development of the interior, priority will be given to power industry so as to provide the necessary stimulus to other industries. The 

Government will also aid in the expansion of iron and

steel, petroleum, textile, sugar, paper-making, electric engineering industries.

As the textile industry is essential to the people's livelihood, it is only logical that both the Government and private enterprises should be coordinated for its expansion. Unfortunately, the main cotton production centers are in areas infested with Communists. Hence only a limited amount of cotton can be collected through purchase. The deficiency is such that it can hardly be made up by American cotton provided under the U. S. China-aid program. The growth of our textile industry is therefore handicapped. But the Government, in view of the importance of the industry, which at present is not only supplying the home market, but is producing for export, will do everything possible to keep it going and to increase its production.

All measures outlined are designed to step up industrial and mining production and to improve the transportation system. In all its undertakings in this connection, the Government will proceed with dispatch and in accordance with plans. Good results may be expected within two to three years.

To Push Land Redistribution 

The equalization of land ownership is the core of 

Dr. Sun Yat-sen's Principle of People's Livelihood. 

This principle has been tardy in its implementation because of the need of careful planning. Even so, the 25 per cent reduction in land rental which the Government decreed after V-J Day has substantially lightened the burden of tenant farmers. For the disposition of land in the various pacification areas, a set of regulations has been promulgated by the Government. In fact, serious efforts at land redistribution are being made by the local authorities in the 

North. The initial steps have been taken, but they need to be followed up and extended to other areas. 

To carry out reforms at a time like this calls for determination of the first order. I wish to announce that the Government is determined to push the land redistribution program through as one of its major tasks. Concrete measures of enforcement are as follows: 

(1) The Regulations Governing the Disposition of 

Land in the Pacification Areas will be used as a reference. Their advantages and disadvantages which have become manifest following their enforcement in the northern provinces will be taken into account.

Meanwhile, Government organs in charge of land administration will authorize the local authorities in various pacification areas and in places where the landlord-tenant relationship has reached a critical stage to give land reform their immediate attention. 

The same organs will be asked to map out plans to protect owner-farmers. In accordance with equitable rules decided upon by the Government, land not tilled by their owners will be distributed among non-owners who do the actual tilling, so that the land problem will be solved once and for all. (2) In areas other than the ones mentioned above, land registration should be completed within a specified period of time. To restrict excessive land ownership, a ceiling will be imposed on the size of land that one can have with anything in excess to be sold to farmers at reasonable prices. Pending the sale of such excessive lands, a progressive tax will be put on their owners as a means of discouraging large land ownership. (3) The 25 per cent reduction in land rental should be strictly enforced so as to better the lot of the tenant-farmers.

All these measures will be vigorously carried out within a definite period of time, with a view to benefitting the farmers at an early date.

Rebels' Land Reform Propaganda 

In their propaganda the Communist rebels have given considerable lip service to land reform. Actually the idea did not originate with them. Often those who went along with the Communists became landlords overnight, while those who opposed them were totally dispossessed. The Communists have merely used land redistribution as a reward for the faithful and penalty for the intractable. They have made only a negligible attempt at land redistribution in its real sense. On the other hand the Government's land reform measures represent its sincere desire to bring about equalization in land ownership and to improve the farmers' living standard. Since its objective is impeccable, its programs will produce wholesome results.

Rural Improvements Emphasized 

Next in importance is agricultural improvement. 

Although a farming country for thousands of years, China today faces a serious food shortage. The export of products such as tea, silk, tung oil, hog bristles, sugar and cooking oil has dropped because their qualities have deteriorated. Corrective measures must be taken immediately since the economic well-being of the nation is at stake.

Among other undertakings contemplated by the Government are the construction of more water conservancy projects to provide irrigation, the erection of more chemical fertilizer plants to turn out an adequate supply of fertilizers for domestic needs. The 

Government will promote mechanical farming and agricultural cooperatives. One thing I wish to emphasize here is that water conservancy works not only cover irrigation projects, but also the dredging of rivers and harbors with a view to improving water transportation. These all have a vital bearing on the future of our nation. Hence, the Government will work hard for their materialization. 

The rehabilitation of the former Yellow River flooded areas will be carried out under a special bureau set up for the purpose. Fortunately we have on hand a large amount of equipment, and rehabilitation

will begin as soon as southern Honan is cleared of Communists. It is part of the project to put thousands of refugees to work on reclaiming large spans of wasteland. As to the cooperatives the Government will foster their growth through financial aid and other forms of assistance.

The Three People's Principles of nationalism, democracy and people's livelihood constitute the foundation on which our nation stands. At this time we immediately should carry out the one on People's Livelihood. Its important points are: (1) The equalization of land ownership, with particular emphasis on land reform; (2) restriction of private capital through the institution of capital levy and inheritance tax, and (3) the development of state capital through the expansion of state-owned productive enterprises and communications systems. Only thus can our national economy have a new lease of life. 

To Support Democracy, World Peace 

Regarding foreign affairs, detailed directives were contained in President Chiang Kai-shek's inaugural address. These may be summarized as follows: (1) Sincere support to the United Nations charter, strengthening of the United Nations, upholding of justice, so as to lay a firm basis for world peace. (2) Maintenance of friendly relations with all countries of the world, especially with the United States which has helped China not only during the war but also since the end of the war in numerous ways, with a view toward joint endeavors in the promotion of democracy and world peace. (3) Concerning Japan, China will not seek revenge but insists that she be completely demilitarized according to international agreements and be rendered incapable of becoming an aggressor nation again. As to the requirements for a peaceful Japanese economy,

China will be agreeable to its recovery at an early date. (4) In relations with countries in other parts of the Far East, China hopes that cordial relations will be established with them, and that Chinese nationals living in their midst will find it possible to carry on with their daily pursuits in security and contribute their share toward local economic recovery. Among the new nations India stands out by virtue of her vast territory and large population and statesmanship, and it is hoped that China and India will work closely together in the interest of world peace. 

To Justify American Aid, Goodwill

Recently, the United States decided to extend material assistance to China. Under the U. S. China-aid program, food, cotton, petroleum, fertilizer and other important materials are provided not only to meet our immediate needs, but also to help us balance our international payments and to slow down our inflation. Meanwhile, the United States has also made available to us loans and credits for the purpose of increasing our production and extending our communications facilities. This gives us a new opportunity in effecting economic recovery. It is our policy to use aid properly in conjunction with a self-help program such as outlined in the ten points adopted by the last cabinet. These ten points are both pertinent and practical so they should be enforced in earnest. 

In short, on our part we must take all necessary steps to justify the goodwill of a friendly power. 

Sound Education Urged 

As regards education and culture, special emphasis will be laid on the coordination of school education with needs of society. Mass education will be promoted, and scientific research will be encouraged. The Communists have tried time and again in recent months to spread propaganda among the students by twisting facts and telling downright falsehoods. Something must be done about this matter at once. The students themselves are not to blame. It is up to the education authorities to show them the truth and give them whatever guidance they may need.

Expenditures for educational and cultural purposes will be increased to 15 per cent of the national budget in accordance with stipulations of the Constitution. 

Besides, there are refugees waiting to be succored, frontier affairs to tackle and the welfare of overseas Chinese to look after so that their link with their fatherland will become closer than before. These and many other important matters will be dealt with in their order of importance. It is essential that the Government be determined to act and to effect needed reforms. This is the only way to achieve progress. 

Legislative Support Asked

In the performance of our duties, I and my colleagues shall depend on the Legislative Yuan for its support in solving the problems of major importance and in mapping out concrete plans. Given cooperation of the legislature, this administrative program will be assured of smooth execution. I shall report to you from time to time on the activities of my administration. Whenever it is necessary for me to make proposals, I shall do so in utmost sincerity and candor. 

Your understanding will be what I need most. I hope you all appreciate the seriousness of our national crisis and the danger of delay in our efforts to save the situation. I hope you will make known your important decisions and you will co-operate with me and my colleagues in the executive branch of the Government in order to ensure the success of our campaign against the rebels and to enhance the economic well-being of the people. This is what I am asking of you.

Vol. 1 

AUGUST 1, 1948 

No. 14

The Canton - Hankow Railway 

High on China's self-help program, with American aid, is the complete rehabilitation and early improvement of the vital south-north communication line linking the Yangtze River and the Pearl River, the 

1,378-kilometer (855 miles) railway and branches under the Canton-Hankow Railway Administration. 

A feat of improvisation and patch-work which astonished even the most hardened observers in China was the restoration of traffic on this extensively damaged and badly torn Chinese railway in six months in 1946. 

With the cooperation of the UNRRA, Chinese engineers went to work with whatever material they could lay hands on and directed ingeniously an army of trained and largely untrained workmen. At one time 

50,000 men were placed on the job. Several hundred men had lost their lives and the Chinese Government had spent approximately US$25,000,000 before limited traffic became once more possible. 

Running through Kwangtung, Hunan and Hupeh, the three sections of the Canton-Hankow Railway did not operate as a unit until less than a year before the Japanese attacked. In the first year of China's Resistance, the Japanese air force raided this supply and distribution line more than 600 times and dropped over 5,000 bombs. When the enemy campaigned in 

1944 to seize the entire railroad, Chinese defenders were compelled to block tunnels, blow up bridges, remove rail ties and level road beds. The demolition was thorough enough to discourage the Japanese from ever attempting to restore or make use of it.

The service this patched-up railway has been called upon to perform since it reopened to traffic in July, 1946, has taxed its unrestored capacity to the limit. 

Because of weak make-shift structures and inadequate signal systems, accidents have occurred, costing many lives and loss of property.¹ However, the role the 

1A passenger train which jumped the tracks and plunged into a river at Yingteh, north of Canton, on July 10, 1947, resulted in more than 200 deaths. The accident was later found due to the collapse of the floodweakened approach to a railway bridge over the river. railroad has to play in hastening the rebuilding and development of South China and the Yangtze valley is becoming increasingly obvious, a fact emphasized by the high priority the Chinese Government has allotted it.

Fund Applied For 

An application for US$22,000,000 from the Economic Cooperation Administration's China aid fund has been filed by the Canton-Hankow Railway for the overhaul and improvement of this important communication system. According to the railway authorities, the fund will be used for the purchase of 540 kilometers (335 miles) of standard gauge rails, 70 passenger coaches and 500 freight cars. Other needs of the railway amounting to US$39,300,000, according to a 1946 estimate based on a survey by an American firm, will be met locally. 

Sections of this south-north artery were thoroughly destroyed during the war with Japan. The major bridges and tunnels were demolished and in many places the road bed was completely levelled. Little was left of the life-line connecting Canton on the South China Sea coast and Hankow on the central Yangtze River when the war ended. 

Traffic Resumed in 1946 

The Chinese National Government started on the rehabilitation of the Canton-Hankow Railway2 in 

January, 1946. Six months later, in July, traffic resumed on the entire line. With the exception of some locomotives and cars supplied by the UNRRA, the line was restored mainly through a combination of ingenuity and persistence, the use of hand tools and muscles and the rails and spikes removed from nearby branch lines. It was a hodgepodge of a railway when it reopened to traffic in the summer of 1946. It is still a patched-up railway today. Not until the arrival of 

2 For the destruction and repair of the Canton-Hankow Railway, see pamphlet in Chinese on the Canton-Hankow Railway issued by the Chinese Government Information Office, Nanking, 1947.

American rails, steel bridges, locomotives, cars, and traffic signal equipment can the queer assortment now in use be replaced to make it the first-class railroad it should be in the Chinese transportation and communication network.

History of the Railway The Canton-Hankow Railway is relatively new in 

China. The entire line was completed and opened to traffic in September, 1936, less than a year before the war broke out. The construction of the line, however, had a long history. It was among the first rail lines planned by the late Manchu Dynasty at the end of the last century. The project of connecting Central 

China and South China was first drawn up in 1896. 

Plans were completed that year and negotiations began with an American firm for a construction loan of US$40,000,000. Contracts were signed in 1900 and actual construction work began the following year on the 49-kilometer (30 miles) Canton-Samshui branch line which was completed in 1903. 

The American firm, however, soon lost interest in the enterprise. The Canton-Samshui line and the construction rights of the Canton-Hankow Railway were sold to the Chinese in 1905 at the cost of US$6,- 

750,000, the money being raised by the Kwangtung,

Hunan and Hupeh Provinces, through whose territory the line was to traverse. The nationalization of all major railways in the country in 1913 brought the line under the direct control of the Ministry of Com munications of the Chinese Government. The Canton- 

Samshui branch line, which had been under the control of the Kwangtung Provincal Government, was turned over to the National Government in 1929. 

Built in Sections 

The first section of the trunk line of the Canton- Hankow Railway built under Chinese direction was the 224-kilometer (139 miles) segment between Canton and Kukong in northern Kwangtung. Its construction started in 1905 and it was completed in 1916 after a period of inactivity before and after the Revolution of 1911.

The northern section from Wuchang (across the 

Yangtze River from Hankow) in Hupeh to Chuchow in central Hunan totalled 417 kilometers (258 miles).

Its building was financed by a consortium composed of American, British, French and German banking interests on the eve of the Revolution. The Revolution and World War I interrupted again and again the construction of this section and it was not completed until 1918. 

South of Canton, the 179-kilometer (110 miles)

Canton-Kowloon Railway connects Canton and Hong kong. The 142-kilometer (88 miles) section between 

Canton and Samchun on the Kwangtung-Kowloon border was built between 1907-1911 by the Chinese, and the 35-kilometer (22 miles) section between Samchun and Kowloon at the same time by the British. 

The Canton-Kowloon line was linked with the Canton-Hankow line in August, 1937, soon after China's war with Japan broke out.

Mid-Section Building Delayed 

The middle section between Chuchow and Kukong, 454 kilometers (282 miles) long, was left unfinished for many years. The difficult terrain on the Hunan- 

Kwangtung border and the unsettled conditions of the country before 1928 made systematic construction work on the central sections hard. It was not until 

1930, after the establishment of the National Government in 1928, when plans for the completion of the entire Canton-Hankow Railway were mapped out, that work resumed. The estimated cost of constructing this section was Ch$38,330,000 not including im ported materials estimated at £1,660,000. To meet these payments, £1,660,000 was made available in London and £1,240,000 in China, both from the matured portion of the British share of the Boxer Funds.³ 

Serves Rich Provinces 

The Canton-Hankow Railway is the only southnorth railroad south of the Yangtze River. It traverses the three rich provinces of Kwangtung, Hunan and 

Hupeh. Hunan is known as China's rice bowl. It has an annual surplus in rice production. Kwangtung, its neighboring province, does not produce enough food for its 30,000,000 people. Before the completion of the Canton-Hankow Railway, Kwangtung imported large quantities of rice from Siam and Indo-China. 

Since the opening of the railroad, however, Hunan rice has been made available to the population of Kwangtung. 

The lake regions in central Hunan are also famous for their agricultural products. Of more importance is the Hupeh cotton output which is second only to that of Kiangsu. Hupeh cotton now supplies Hunan and Kwangtung mills. Hupeh and Hunan tea and tung oil and other agricultural products have an easy outlet to domestic and foreign markets because of the 

Canton-Hankow Railway. 

Southern Hunan's strategic minerals like tungsten, antimony, manganese, zinc and lead are distributed by this line, and lumber produced there constitutes a major item of the railroad's freight. Kwangtung's fruits, salt, fish and other sea foods are being transported by the rail link to new markets in central China.

Hunan is South China's main coal producer. It is vital for the economic advancement of both Hunan and Kwangtung to have easy access to Hunan coal. 

Before the war, the National Resources Commission planned to establish an industrial center in the 

Hsiangtan-Chuchow area in central Hunan. Preliminary work had already started when the war broke out and whatever was completed had to be destroyed or abandoned. Construction is now resumed under the Commission to develop the area into a main industrial base in South China. Coal production alone in the Hsiangtan-Chuchow area is calculated at 500,- 000 tons a month. Lines branching off the Canton- 

Hankow Railway reach the mines. The trunk railroad 

3 Chang. Kai-ngau, China's Struggle for Railway Development, pp. 77. 

78, New York, 1943.

will bring coal to supply planned factories, power plants and other fuel consumers in the three provinces. 

Connects Leading Cities 

The railroad connects several of China's leading cities. Its northern terminal is the Central China trading center of Hankow, where connections can be made with North China via the Peiping-Hankow Rail. way and with the shipping lines on the Yangtze River serving both East China and West China.

Hankow is known as China's Chicago. It is in Hankow where the products of West China and Central China gather before being shipped to other parts of 

China and to overseas markets. Sea-going ships can reach Hankow throughout the year. Formerly an inland port city with British, French and Japanese concessions, Hankow has been under complete Chinese administration since the end of the unequal treaties. 

It has a population of 750,000 and, with its sister cities of Wuchang, capital of Hupeh Province, and Hangyang, where Central China's largest iron works is situated, the Wuhan cities constitute the political, cultural, and economic nerve center of the Central 

China provinces.

The southern terminal of the railroad is Canton, South China's leading port city on the estuary of the 

Pearl River. The three headwaters of the river, the 

East River draining eastern Kwangtung, the North 

River from northern Kwangtung, and the West River traversing Kwangsi and Kwangtung, bring to Canton products along their courses. Manufactured goods from Canton move on these rivers to interior Kwangtung and Kwangsi. Canton was one of the oldest sea ports for China's foreign trade. The city has now a population of 1,500,000 and is the commercial center of the southern provinces. 

From Canton northward, the railroad reaches Hengyang in southern Hunan. Hengyang captured worldwide fame in the summer of 1944 when a Chinese garrison held out 48 days against heavy Japanese attacks.

Northward along the Hsiang River, the railroad leads to Changsha, Hunan's capital. The city lies near the Tungting Lake, China's largest inland water. It is the center of the province's rice market. The city is also renowned for embroidered coats and bedspreads. 

But its world-wide fame during the war was obtained through the repulse of the Japanese three times just outside of its gates.

Axis of South China Railways 

In the South China transportation network the railroad plays an important role. It is linked with the 

Peiping-Hankow Railway leading into North China.

It serves East China and West China through shipping links on the Yangtze River. It is connected with seagoing navigation lines on the South China coast. At 

Chuchow it meets the Chekiang-Kiangsi-Hunan Railway. From Chuchow one may travel all the way by rail from Hunan to Shanghai and Nanking through Hangchow, capital of Chekiang, or to the central Yangtze trading town, Kiukiang, in Kiangsi through Nanchang, Kiangsi's capital. At Hengyang, the Canton-Hankow Railway crosses the Hunan-Kwangsi Railway to Kweilin, capital of Kwangsi, where an extension southward leads to Nanning and Chengnankwan on the China-Indo-China border. Another extension from Kweilin leads northward to Kweiyang, capital of Kweichow, where projected lines will eventually take the traveller by rail to Chungking and Chengtu in Szechwan. At Hsiangtan in central Hunan, another line stems from the Canton-Hankow Railway westward to Hsinhwa on the Hunan-Kweichow border from where a line will in time be extended westward to Kweiyang and thence to Yunnan where rail transportation to Indo-China is already available. The Canton- 

Hankow Railway is actually the axis from which the entire South China system of railways reaches out in various directions. 

Has Important Military Value 

The railway's military value, though not conspicuous in the blueprint when it was first planned, was fully demonstrated soon after its completion. After the war with Japan began in July, 1937, the Canton- Hankow Railway, by linking up with the Canton- Kowloon Railway, conveyed huge quantities of military supplies and equipments to the different active fronts. It was the only line which had access to a sea port that was not under Japanese control or threat. 

Supplies coming from the outside world were landed in Hongkong and moved into China via the Canton- Kowloon line and then freighted northward to Hankow for distribution. The railway was also instrumental in moving hundreds of thousands of troops from Kwangtung, Kwangsi, and Hunan to the East 

China and North China fronts.

Because of its strategic value, the Japanese made repeated efforts to put the railroad out of commission in the early stage of war. Up to June, 1938, in less than one year, the Japanese had raided the railway 684 times, dropping 5,538 bombs. "The average interval between air raids," said a spokesman of the Ministry of Communications, "was about 18 hours on the Canton-Hankow section and 46 hours on the Canton-Kowloon section." The line was finally cut by the Japanese ground forces in October, 1938.

While the Japanese main force was driving up the Yangtze River toward Hankow, the northern terminus of the Canton-Hankow Railway, in the summer of 1938, another Japanese column made a surprise landing at Bias Bay in Kwangtung on October 12, 1938, and drove toward Canton. Ten days later, Canton fell. 

With the loss of the city, the Chinese section of the 

Canton-Kowloon line and the 65-kilometer (40 miles) section of the Canton-Hankow line south of Yuentan also fell into the hands of the enemy. All the way from Yuentan to Kukong, a distance of 160 kilometers (100 miles), the Chinese removed the rails and destroyed the tracks. The northern section between 

Wuchang and Huangshiachieh, 268 kilometers (166 miles) long, was lost to the Japanese with the fall of the Wuhan cities. Between Huangshiachieh and Chu- 

China Information Committee News Release, No. 236, September 17, 1938, Chungking. ww

chow, a distance of 149 kilometers (92 miles), the

Chinese removed all rails and destroyed all bridges.5 

Only the 454-kilometer (282 miles) section of the railroad between Chuchow and Kukong was left in operation from 1938 to 1944 when a Japanese drive captured the entire railway.

Channel of War Supplies 

Before the Japanese occupation of the northern and southern sections of the Canton-Hankow Railway in 1938, the line had transported 700,000 tons of war supplies. During the period of the heaviest traffic, 140 trains were running simultaneously on the line's single track. Even in the periods of relatively light traffic, 80 trains were in simultaneous operation." 

The Chinese demolished most of the Canton-Hankow Railway in 1944. Of the line's 135 major bridges, 98 were totally put out of commission. Another 171 of its 676 smaller bridges were also destroyed. Some 

300 kilometers of rail in the middle section of the line were removed in the face of the advancing Japanese. Also removed were 1,000,000 railroad ties and 

1,000,000 square meters of road bed. Nine of the 22 tunnels were blocked. All the plants, shops, station buildings and godowns along the line were destroyed. 

Traffic signals were removed. The Chinese plan was to deny the Japanese the use of this railway for at least six months. The Japanese never succeeded in making use of the torn railway before the war concluded. 

Repair By Chinese Engineers 

Rehabilitation of the railway was accomplished in 

January to July, 1946, by Chinese engineers, most of them trained in United States universities, working under Tu Cheng-yuan, a Cornell man and the managing director of the road. Old rails were collected along the line. In the end 23 different kinds of rails, from light 55-pounders to the United States standard 110pounders, were laid down by six rail-laying brigades working on different sections of the railroad. Side tracks were ruthlessly stripped for ties, rails, and installations for use on the main route. Using blacksmith shop facilities and rudimentary tools, cars were pulled from scrap heaps and pounded and bolted back into usefulness. The shop workers even made their own bolts and nuts.

When the entire railroad was reopened to traffic in 

July, 1946, the Canton-Hankow Railway engineers had rebuilt bridges with a total length of 8,400 meters (5.3 miles), laid 400 kilometers (250 miles) of tracks, replaced 790,000 railroad ties, added 340,000 cubic meters of stone, repaired 1,030,000 square meters of road bed and 15,000 cubic meters of embankment. 

Carrying Capacity in 1947

The line had in July, 1946, a total of 82 locomotives, 146 passenger coaches and 1,753 freight cars. Many of 

5 Chang: p. 210. 

Chang: p. 210. them were salvaged from wrecks and were constantly in need of repair. But efficient use of the vehicles on hand made it possible for the railroad to improve its services. It increased its passenger service from 713,917 paid passengers in January of 1947 to 1,066,292 in

May of the same year. Its six leading stations alone served 296,000 passengers in January, 1948. In January, 1947, the railroad transported 24,134 tons of commercial freight. The amount was increased to 39,745 tons in May of 1947. In January, 1948, the freight carried rose to over 170,000 tons. The time for its express passenger service between Canton and Hankow was clipped from 55 hours to 45 hours and recently to 40 hours, as compared with 44 hours before the war.

With some further improvement, the management of the railway hopes to operate before the end of the year at least 28 trains every 24 hours with a minimum freight service of 2,000 tons a day and a top speed of 

70 kilometers (44 miles) per hour for its passenger trains.

The Railway's Needs 

The railroad still needs 370 locomotives, 1,500 passenger coaches and 3,000 freight cars to meet the foreseeable transportation demands. It also needs 500 kilometers (315 miles) of standard rails, 1,100,000 railroad ties, and 26,000 tons of other materials to make it a really useful railway. On the top-priority list is an electric signal system, together with ten telephone exchanges and 800 telephones and some 300 tons of telephone wires, to serve the entire length of the railroad, a "must" for a single-track railroad.

The railroad solved its fuel problem with the completion of a branch line to the Kaokang Coal Mines in western Kiangsi. The mines have a daily output of 5,000 tons of coal. Another feeder line from Pingshih in northern Kwangtung to the Kouyahtung Coal

Mines will be completed before the end of the year. 

The Management 

Tu Cheng-yuan, the railroad's managing director, is one of China's best-known railroad builders. He is a great believer of American methods. He is trying to standardize the railway equipment along American lines. Even the Australian locomotives used on the line were built according to American specifications. 

Mr. Tu also hopes to get American advisers to help introduce American systems of engineering and management. His urgent need, however, is American materials to replace improvised bridges and makeshift rails, ties, signal systems, plants, locomotives and cars that do not meet the regular standard. What has been done on the railroad is only a stop-gap. It needs permanent repairs soon to make it the all-important south-north artery south of the Yangtze River worthy of its position in the Chinese transportation system.

AUGUST 15, 1948 

No. 15

The Chekiang - Kiangsi Railway 

For the renovation of the 1,004-kilometer (624 miles) Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway, an application for

US$10,000,000 has been made with the Economic Cooperation Administration's China-aid fund by the Chinese railway authorities. Built before the war by 

Chinese engineers mainly with Chinese capital, and hastily put together after the war under great hardships, the line is the most important east-west artery in China south of the Yangtze River. It links the ricegrowing province of Hunan with the scenic city of Hangchow, capital of Chekiang, where rail connections are available to Shanghai and Nanking. In Hunan, it meets the south-north Canton-Hankow Railway.

The Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway was proposed by 

Chang Ching-kiang, Governor of Chekiang,¹ in 1929.

His purpose was to provide an easy outlet for western Chekiang products, to bring Kiangsi grains into Chekiang cheaply, and to hasten the economic development of the province.

Planning and survey of the line began in March, 1929. The first section to be built was the Hangchow- 

Lanki section of 195 kilometers (121 miles). In order to limit the required amount of capital to the minimum, Governor Chang decided to use light rails at the initial stage of the railroad. His idea was: Get the trains running first; improvement comes afterwards. 

To finance the construction of the Hangchow-Lanki section, Governor Chang secured a credit of pre-war Ch$3,600,000 from Chinese banks guaranteed by an issue of provincial reconstruction bonds. Construction began on February 20, 1930, and the section was completed on March 6, 1932. The line was of standard gauge. Its rails were the 35-pound light type, and its wagons were limited to a maximum capacity of 15 tons.

The construction of the second section between For the story of the construction and financing of the Chekiang- 

Kiangsi Failway, see Chang Kai-ngau: China's Struggle for Railway 

Development, pp. 78-80, John Day, New York, 1943.

Lanki and Yushan, 164 kilometers (102 miles), began on November 28, 1932. At Governor Chang's suggestion, the Chekiang Provincial Government sold the Hangchow Power Plant and secured a further loan of 

Ch$1,900,000 from the Bank of China and Ch$1,200,- 

000 from four local Hangchow banks to finance the construction of this section. An additional £140,000 was secured from the British Boxer Indemnity Fund for the purchase of railroad materials from abroad. 

Work was completed in December, 1933, and through traffic from Hangchow to Yushan began on January 

1, 1934.

Extended to Nanchang 

Plans subsequently were made to extend the railroad further west to Nanchang, capital of Kiangsi. 

However, even with the ready support of the Chinese banks, the Chekiang Provincial Government found the project too heavy a drain on its resources. Therefore, it approached the Ministry of Railways of the 

National Government and the Kiangsi Provincial 

Government for collaboration. The three parties, after negotiations, formed the Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway Company under the auspices of the Ministry of Railways. 

To finance the construction of the 292-kilometer (181 miles) Yushan-Nanchang section, the Ministry of Railways issued bonds to the amount of Ch$12,- 000,000, secured on the profits of all Government railroads. The Kiangsi Province issued bonds to an equal amount, secured on the provincial surtax on salt. 

Both issues were handed over to the Chinese banks as security for a loan of Ch$8,000,000 and a like amount for credit on materials. The Bank of China, acting as representative of the Chinese Banking Syndicate, entered into an agreement with Otto Wolff of Cologne, Germany, to procure railroad materials on credit.

With funds and materials secured, the construction of the Yushan-Nanchang section began in November, 1934, and was completed in January, 1936. The rails

of this section were of the 63-pound type used on all 

Chinese national railways.

After the completion of the Yushan-Nanchang section, the Ministry of Railways decided to extend the line further westward to Pingsiang in western Kiangsi to connect it with the Chuchow-Pingsiang section, belonging at that time to the Canton-Hankow Railway Administration. The Ministry issued bonds to the amount of Ch$27,000,000 with which it secured a loan of Ch$22,000,000 to finance the new project of building the 263-kilometer (163 miles) section, which was completed on September 10, 1937.

Meanwhile, the Ministry ordered the Chekiang- Kiangsi Railway Company to have the light rails used on the Hangchow-Yushan section changed to the regular 63-pounders. The rails were supplied by the Vitko vice Mines, Steel and Iron Works Corporation of 

Czechoslovakia. The cost was Chinese Customs Gold

Units 2,331,443 (at the time equivalent to approximately US$1,400,000).

The Czech rails reached China between the months of February and March, 1937.

The Ministry of Railways also ordered that the 90kilometer (56 miles) Pingsiang-Chuchow line be transferred from the Canton-Hankow Railway to the 

Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway. The transfer completed the Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway, an east-west trunk line of 1,004 kilometers (624 miles).

To connect the line with the Shanghai-Hangchow Railway, a double-decker steel bridge of 1,390 meters (1,520 yards) was built across the Chientang River at Hangchow. The bridge was not completed until November, 1937, when China's Resistance against Japan was already in its fourth month. 

Aids Military Effort 

Chinese troops from the southern and southwestern provinces were transported over this line to the Shanghai-Nanking battlefields. In reverse, refugees, dismantled factories, and equipments and materials were evacuated to the interior. Part of the railroad equipment was moved from the Shanghai-Hangchow and Shanghai-Nanking Railways to the Chekiang- Kiangsi line. At one point during that time, the line had 107 locomotives, 160 passenger coaches, and 1,500 wagons as against its original 47 locomotives and 66 passenger coaches.

The rapid advance of the Japanese invaders, however, made it necessary for the railroad authorities to destroy the Chientang River Bridge in early December, 1937 (only one month after its completion), and then the section from Hangchow to Mienchi. In June, 1938, the section from Mienchi to Chuki was also destroyed. This section, however, was soon restored when the war situation in Chekiang stabilized. 

The Japanese occupation of Nanchang, capital of Kiangsi, in March, 1939, cut the Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway into two sections. East of Nanchang, only the 

435-kilometer (270 miles) section between Chuki and Tengchiapu was in operation. To the west, the entire line was destroyed and rails totalling 340 kilometers (211 miles) were removed to Hunan for the construction of new lines into Kwangsi. 

From the destruction of the Chientang River Bridge to the loss of Nanchang, the Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway, besides its passenger and freight services, operated 1,700 military trains and transported 1,500,000 troops, 60,000 wounded soldiers, and 230,000 tons of military supplies.

The Chuki-Tengchiapu section was twice partially destroyed-once in October, 1940, and again in April- May, 1941-because of enemy attacks. On both occasions it was restored after the enemy retreated. 

This section was later totally demolished in May- June, 1942, when the Japanese made an all-out effort to destroy the "bomb Tokyo" airfields along the 

Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway after the Doolittle raid.

More than 4,000 workers of the railroad escaped on foot after they had hidden the rails and blown up most of the station buildings, etc.

After the Chinese counter-offensive in September, 1942, the railroad authorities were able to restore the 

104-kilometer (64 miles) Kiangshan-Kankow section, which was then under Chinese control. The Japanese, on the other hand, rebuilt the 185-kilometer (115 miles) section between Hangchow and Kinghwa besides repairing the Chientang River Bridge.

In the spring of 1945, the Japanese began to dismantle their section of the Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway when they realized the war was lost. They succeeded in destroying the Kinghwa-Chuki section before the V-J Day. When the war ended, the 1,004-kilometer (624 miles) Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway had only two sections in operation, totalling 181 kilometers (112 miles).

Administration Restored The Chekiang-Kiangsi Railway Administration was restored in April, 1946. Besides the 1,004-kilometer (624 miles) Chekiang-Kiangsi line, the Ministry of Communications assigned it the 128-kilometer (80 miles) Nanchang-Kiukiang Railway. Because of the lack of material and equipment, the Administration was able to repair only the 35-kilometer (22 miles) 

Chuhsien-Kiangshan section in 1946.

Greater efforts were made to restore the entire Chekiang-Kiangsi line in 1947. The UNRRA supplied the line with 54 locomotives, 599 wagons, and 40,000 tons of material and equipment. Work began simultaneously on six sections of the line. The 108-kilometer (67 miles) Chuki-Kinghwa section was restored in March, the 128-kilometer (80 miles) Nanchang-Kiukiang section and the 82-kilometer (51 miles) Pingsiang-Chuchow section in June, and the 82-kilometer

Kinghwa-Chuhsien section in August, 1947. Repairs on the 252-kilometer (157 miles) Shangyao-Nanchang section were completed in February, 1948, permitting through traffic between Hangchow and Nanchang. 

The 263-kilometer (163 miles) Siangtung-Pingsiang section was scheduled to be restored in July, 1948. 

Most of the repairs, however, were makeshift patchwork. Besides equipment and materials supplied by the UNRRA, the Railway had to salvage from its junk piles whatever material that could be utilized. 

The line today is way below standard. It needs new rails and steel girders for bridges, among other things.

China's Postal Service 

Initiated more than 3000 years ago, China's postal system did not undergo rapid modernization and record marked development until recent years under the National Government. 

Reporting to the National Assembly in April of this year, Minister of Communications Yu Ta-wei declared that the postal branch of government service, without mention of its splendid war-time record, was making new achievements and attempting new methods despite the Communist insurrection which curtailed land communications and made the operation of postal service in the combat zones difficult and dangerous. 

Among other things, he disclosed a greater reliance on air-lift and the fact that whereas in the entire year of 1927 only 35 tons of air mail were handled, 1947 recorded a total of over 6,000 tons. In the current year, air mail handled per month has been in the neighborhood of 600 tons. Before the war, a letter from Nanking to remote Tihwa in Sinkiang would take longer than a month. A letter travelling the same distance now takes from only 4 to 11 days.

Postal service in the rural areas has been the subject of continuous study and experiment. The Ningpo area in Chekiang and the Wusih area in Kiangsu have been named as experimental centers for the extension of rural postal service. Recent reports show that people residing in 7000 villages in the two experimental areas already have mail delivered to them instead of having to call at the designated stations. 

Mobile postal units found highly effective during the emergencies of the last war are being steadily increased and improved upon to enhance speed and safety of delivery.

Historical Survey 

The first Chinese postal system was established as far back as the Chou Dynasty (1122-255 B. C.) when a government courier service was maintained for the rapid and safe transmission of official communications. This system was preserved and expanded through all the dynasties and was known as the I Chan, recorded in Chinese literature and also the governmental documents. Besides the transmission of official dispatches the service provided transport for officials who were being transferred from one place to another.

As time went on a more practical system for transporting mail for the common people was organized. 

In the 15th century, under the Ming Dynasty, a postal service for the general public was created and called Min Hsin Chu. This was usually in the hands of a remittance bank or business establishment with connections in the interior. Although this system was not conducted by the government, it was used by the public to transmit its messages. It proved to be reliable and efficient and served the public well. However, it had one fatal defect: only the most profitable routes were developed, leaving wide areas unserved. 

Modern Postal System 

The growing contact with foreign powers and the example of the foreign postal systems became strong factors in hastening the setting up of a national postal system in China. The Chinese post office, was established by imperial decree on March 20, 1896. This system gradually replaced the two antiquated systems of I Chan and Min Hsin Chu, which were formerly relied upon by the officials and some of the people. 

Between 1902 and 1912 the post office grew into a full-fledged efficient public service. Branches extended throughout the country, with a greatly increased staff and international connections. In 1914 China joined the International Postal Union. 

In 1915 the revenue of the postal service exceeded expenditures for the first time. In the year of 1919 the postal savings bank was inaugurated. 

Since 1929 motor vehicles have been more generally used and bus services more widely operated for mail matters. In the same year mail matters were, for the first time, transported by air between Shanghai-Nanking and Shanghai-Hankow.

After 1934 post offices were installed in all county seats throughout the country and in most of the smaller towns. Every year more and more places were brought within the postal network. 

Service in Wartime

During the Sino-Japanese War the Postal Admin istration moved from Nanking to Hankow; later to 

Kunming and then to Chungking. In view of the importance of postal communication to the general public as well as the government, orders were given to all classes of postal employees throughout China to maintain postal services to the best of their ability. 

Among the most remarkable wartime achievements of the Post Office was the maintenance of mail service in the war zones and with foreign countries, despite tremendous transportation difficulties. Mail destined for foreign countries during the first part of the war was sent partly by sea and partly by air. Hongkong and Rangoon were the collecting centers of such foreign mail until the enemy occupation. Thenceforth international mail was almost entirely transported by air. Except for the Soviet Union, all air mail for 

Europe and America was sent by air from Chungking or Kunming to Calcutta, where connections were made with international lines. Air mail service to the 

Soviet Union was carried by Chinese commercial airlines up to Alma Ata. 

Postal Administration Today The Directorate-General of Posts directs all postal affairs in all of China, and guides and supervises all subordinate offices. At the present time the Directorate-General of Posts of the Ministry of Communications is headed by one director-general and two deputy directors-general-one of whom acts as the Director of the Postal Remittance and Savings Banks. The Di rectorate-General has twenty-seven district head post offices, one planning committee and the following departments: Secretariat; General Department; Staff 

Department; Business Department; Accounts and 

Audit Department; International Department; Supply Department; Inspecting Commission's Office.

The steady growth of the Chinese postal service can be seen from the figures released by the Postal Administration in connection with the 50th anniversary of the inauguration of the Chinese postal service. 

The report shows the increase of post offices as follows: 1906. 1916.. 1926. 1936. 

2,096 offices 8,797

.72,690 68,808

In July, 1947, the Postal Administration maintained more than 570,887 kilometers (354,749 miles) of courier, steamer, boat, railway, motor car, and air mail lines; and 60,602 post offices of various classes. Monthly mail matter handled amounted to more than 97,- 

940,000 pieces.²

Air Mail Development 

The history of air mail in China is not a long one. 

In the year 1929 commercial aviation in China received, for the first time, full government attention and support. Since then the network of domestic air mail lines has been considerably enlarged and direct international connections established with Europe, North Africa, Australia, the United States, and Soviet Russia.

Domestic air mail was carried before the war by the China National Aviation Corporation, South- West Aviation Corporation, and Eurasia Aviation Corporation. 

Aviation services established during the war included the Chungking-Hongkong, the Chungking-Kunming, the Chungking-Kiating, and the Hankow 

Changsha flights of the China National Aviation Cor poration and the Hankow-Sian and Kunming-Hanoi flights of the Eurasia Aviation Corporation. Although during the war Free China was blockaded by the Japanese, news and correspondence continued to move from one place to another inside as well as outside the country.

After the war, the China National Aviation Corporation played the same important part in Chinese air mail service. The Central Air Transport Corporation, which succeeded the Eurasia Aviation Corporation, also flies mail on its scheduled runs covering a wide part of the country. The Sino-Soviet Aviation Corporation, organized during the war, carries air mail to and from Sinkiang and the Soviet Union. 

According to the report of 1947, the increase of air routes within the last ten years is from 7,600 kilo- 2These figures are based on Postal Service in China Today, Government Information Office, Nanking, July, 1947. meters (4,723 miles) to 37,329 kilometers (23,196 miles).

Postal Remittances and Savings 

Money orders were sold in Chinese post offices at most of the commercial ports and cities as early as 1898. By slow and steady progress this method of transferring money gained in popularity as it became better known, and later was extended to the post offices of the interior. By 1910 the service was offered in 596 offices, issuing a total of Ch$5,280,000 in domestic orders. After a quarter of a century the number of offices had risen to 9,436 and the total value of remittances handled was Ch$169,952,900. 

The Chinese post office savings bank was first proposed in 1907, but it was not established until July, 1919. Postal savings and remittances in present-day 

China fall under the jurisdiction of the general Postal Administration. These activities are supervised by a central organization called the Postal Savings and Remittance Bank, the director of which is concurrently Assistant Postmaster General.

In spite of its short history, the Chinese postal money order system has been very successful. It is one of the most popular and useful services that is offered to the public by the Post Office. It is of particular value in regions where banking facilities are lacking. 

In 1935 simple life insurance started as an experiment in the Shanghai office of the Directorate of Postal Remittances and Savings Banks and in its Nanking and Hankow branches. The results were so encouraging that by the following year it was introduced into the District Head Office of Kiangsu, Anhwei, Chekiang, Kiangsi, Hupeh, Hunan, and Shanghai.

Improvements in 1946 and 1947 

Since the National Government moved back from Chungking to Nanking the Chinese postal system has been strengthened even further. The foundations laid before and during the war have been enlarged upon and new plans to meet the needs of the public have been carried into effect. Between May, 1946, and 

April, 1947, mail routes were increased by 56,968 kilometers (35,400 miles) and postal establishments by 8,367.

During the war it was necessary to develop mobile postal units in order to cope with the emergency created by war. Several types of mobile post offices were evolved. 

One was a train post office and the others were the steamer and bus post offices. Since this mobile system proved to be so advantageous, after the conclusion of the war it was decided to enlarge its use. At the present time, by train postal service the mail delivery between Nanking and Shanghai is shortened by half a day. The bus post office makes its service available to the public in inaccessible regions where inadequate communications exist. For the improvement of the postal service model post offices were recently initiated in the big cities of Nanking, Tientsin, Hankow, and Shanghai.

SEPTEMBER 1, 1948
