New Financial & Economic Measures 

Following are textual translations of the presidential mandate* on the new financial and economic emergency measures and of the measures themselves: 

Presidential Mandate on Financial and Economic Emergency Measures 

Acting under the temporary provisions of the Constitution of the Republic of China, and in accordance with the decision of the Executive Yuan, this mandate on financial and economic emergency measures is hereby proclaimed. The principal objects are as follows: 

I. As from this date, "Gold Yuan" (dollar) to be adopted as the basic unit of currency, Gold Yuan notes to be issued and backed by 100 percent reserve, and all fapi** and Northeast Circulation Notes out standing to be redeemed within a stipulated period. 

II. Gold, silver, silver currency and foreign currency notes held by the people to be bought over by the government within a stipulated period, after which none whatsoever to be permitted to hold them. 

III. Foreign exchange assets held abroad by nation als of this country to be registered within a stipulated period and placed under Government control, violations being liable to punishment.

IV. Finances to be reorganized and economic control strengthened, so as to stabilize prices and balance the national budget and international payments. 

The presidential mandate together with the 4 measures were promulgated in Nanking on August 19, 1948. 

** Legal tender. 

With the foregoing objects in view, the following regulations are hereby promulgated together with this mandate, each being considered as a part thereof; namely, (1) Regulations governing the issuance of gold yuan notes, (2) Regulations governing the surrender of gold, silver and foreign currencies held by the people, (3) Regulations governing the registration and control of foreign exchange assets held abroad by nationals of the Republic of China, and (4) Regulations governing the reorganization of finance and strengthening of economic control. Further, the Executive Yuan is hereby delegated with authority to issue such rules and/or supplementary measures in connection with the various regulations as it may deem necessary for the enforcement of this mandate. 

By Order of the President 1. Regulations Governing the Issuance of Gold Yuan Notes Art. 1. As from date of promulgation of these Reg ulations, the basic unit of currency of the Republic of 

China shall be the Gold Yuan (Dollar). Each Yuan shall have a legal content of 0.22217 grams of fine gold. Gold Yuan notes shall be issued by the Central 

Bank of China to be circulated at face value.

Art. 2. The subsidiary units of the Gold Yuan shall be chiao (10 cents) and fen (cent), with 10 fen making 1 chiao and 10 chiao making 1 yuan.

Art. 3. Gold Yuan notes shall be of 5 denomina tions; namely, 1 Yuan, 5 Yuan, 10 Yuan, 50 Yuan and 

100 Yuan.

Art. 4. Gold Yuan subsidiary coins shall be of 5 

denominations; namely, 1 fen, 5 fen, 1 chiao, 2 chiao and 5 chiao. These coins shall be made separately of copper, nickel and silver, as the case may be; and the 

Central Bank of China may issue Gold Yuan subsidiary notes for circulation simultaneously. 

Art. 5. As from date of promulgation of these regulations, the issuance of fapi and Northeast Circulation Notes shall be discontinued. Fapi notes previously issued shall be converted at the rate of three million dollars to 1 Gold Yuan, and Northeast Circulation Notes at 300,000 dollars to 1 Gold Yuan. They shall be exchanged for Gold Yuan notes without limitation in amount before November 20, 1948. During the period of conversion, their temporary circulation shall be permitted at the aforesaid rates of conversion. 

Measures for dealing with Taiwan and Sinkiang currencies shall be adopted by the Executive Yuan separately.

Art. 6. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, all public and private accounting shall be kept on the basis of Gold Yuan. In all matters in which registration is required by law and the amount of value has to be specified, alternation in registration must be effected within six months following the promulgation of these Regulations.

Art. 7. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, all public and private rights and obligations in terms of fapi and/or Northeast Circulation Notes shall be computed and settled at the conversion rates stipulated in Article 5 of these Regulations. Measures for dealing with outstanding fapi bonds issued by the Government shall be adopted by the Executive Yuan separately. With the exception of the 36th Year 

U. S. Gold Loan which shall be repaid according to its original regulations, the 27th Year Gold Loan, 29th

Year Reconstruction Gold Loan, 31st Year Allied Victory U. S. Gold Loan and 36th Year Short-Term 

Treasury Notes shall be converted at official exchange rates to a new Gold Yuan loan to be issued by the Government.

Art. 8. Gold Yuan notes issue shall be backed by 

100 percent reserve. The aforementioned note issue reserve shall be composed of: A minimum of 40 percent gold, silver and foreign exchange, and the remainder in bonds and securities and Government owned properties designated by the Government.

Art. 9. 

The total issue of Gold Yuan notes shall be limited to two billion Yuan.

Art. 10. For the inspection and custody of reserve of Gold Yuan note issue, a Gold Yuan Note Issue Re serve Supervisory Commission shall be established, the regulations governing its organization to be adopted by the Executive Yuan. 

Art. 11. Gold Yuan notes must be counter-signed by the Governor and the General Manager of the Issue Department of the Central Bank of China before they are put into circulation.

Art. 12. The Central Bank of China must, at the end of each month, report the monthly total of Gold 

Yuan note issue to the Ministry of Finance and the

Gold Yuan Note Issue Reserve Supervisory Com mission.

Art. 13. The Gold Yuan Note Issue Reserve Su pervisory Commission must, at the end of each month, conduct an examination of the amount of 

Gold Yuan notes issued by the Central Bank of China and the conditions of their reserve. It shall prepare an inspection report, publish it officially, and submit same to the Executive Yuan, sending duplicate copies to the Ministry of Finance and the Central Bank of China.

Art. 14. If and when the aforesaid Commission finds the reserve of the Gold Yuan note issue to be insufficient or the reserve in gold, silver and foreign exchange to be below the percentage stipulated in Section 2 of Article 8 above, it shall immediately notify the Central Bank of China to suspend note issue and to withdraw the amount of notes in excess of reserve, and at the same time report separately to the Executive Yuan and the Ministry of Finance.

Art. 15. Upon receipt of the above-mentioned notification, the Central Bank of China must either immediately withdraw the excess amount of Gold Yuan notes issued or make up the deficit in issue reserve. 

Without the consent of the said Commission after further examination, the Central Bank may not in crease the note issue.

Art. 16. Counterfeiting, alteration or deliberate damaging of gold yuan notes is punishable according to the provisions of the Regulations Governing Pun ishment of Damage to the National Currency. 

Art. 17. These Regulations shall come into force on the date of promulgation.

II. Regulations Governing the Surrender of Gold, Silver and Foreign Currencies Held by the People Art. 1. "People" used in these regulations include natural persons, juristic persons and other corporate bodies within the territories of the Republic of China.

Art. 2. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, the circulation, transaction or holding of gold, silver, silver currency and foreign currency notes is prohibited within the territories of the Republic of China.

Art. 3. People holding gold, silver, silver currency and/or foreign currency notes must convert them to Gold Yuan notes at the Central Bank of China or any of its appointed banks, before September 30, 1948, according to the following provisions: (1) In the case of gold, each shih liang (31.25 grams) of required fineness to be exchanged for 200 

Gold Yuan notes; (2) In the case of silver, each shih liang of required fineness to be exchanged for three Gold Yuan notes; (3) In the case of silver dollar coins, each dollar to be exchanged for two Gold Yuan notes; and (4) In the case of United States currency notes, each dollar to be exchanged for four Gold Yuan notes; notes in other foreign currencies to be exchanged at the exchange rates of the Central Bank of China. 

Art. 4. Holders of gold, silver, silver currency and foreign currency notes, aside from exchanging them for Gold Yuan notes under the provisions of the preceding article, may according to their wish dispose of them in one of the following two ways: 

(1) For purchase of the 36th Year U. S. Gold 

Loan Bonds; United States currency notes may be used in their original forms for buying the said bonds; gold, silver, silver currency and/or other foreign currency notes may be similarly used by conversion at the rates stipulated in the preceding Article. (2) For deposit with the Central Bank of China; foreign currency notes may be deposited in their original currencies; and gold, silver and silver dollars may be deposited after conversion to United States currency at the rates stipulated in the preceding Article. The deposits referred to in the preceding Paragraph may be used for the payment of imports covered by import licenses or for other purposes approved by the Ministry of Finance. 

Art. 5. Gold, gold sand and silver produced by domestic mines shall be bought over by the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks at prices to be fixed by them from time to time, and shall not be subject to the restrictions laid down in Article 3 above. 

Art. 6. Gold and/or silver required as raw materials by medical and industrial establishments or for other legitimate uses, may be bought upon application to and approval by the Ministry of Finance.

Art. 7. People holding gold and/or silver orna mental articles shall be permitted to continue such holding and to transfer them, but the gold and/or silver contents of such articles may not be bought or sold at prices exceeding the conversion rates stipulated in Article 3 of these Regulations. 

Art. 8. People may not manufacture gold ingots and bars into gold ornamental articles. 

Art. 9. No gold, silver, silver dollars and foreign currencies may be carried out of the country. But a person who carries out gold ornaments not exceeding a total of two shih liang and/or silver ornaments not exceeding a total of 20 shih liang, or a person who carries for travelling expenses foreign currency notes equivalent in total to not over United States $100 and covered by a certificate issued by the bank selling such foreign currency notes, is excepted from the above restriction. 

Art. 10. Persons who carry gold, silver, silver currency, gold or silver ornaments or foreign currency notes into the country must declare them before the Customs authorities. With the exception that a person is permitted to bring in and to hold gold ornaments not exceeding a total of 20 shih liang, any amount in excess thereof must be surrendered to the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks and be converted to Gold Yuan notes according to the stipulations of Article 3 above. Aside from the amount of gold and silver ornaments permitted to be brought in under the preceding Paragraph, travellers in transit or tourists who bring in gold, silver and/or foreign currency but must later take the same out of the country, shall at the time of entrance to the country declare such belongings before the customs authorities and hand them over to the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks for custody, but they may claim the same back at the time of their departure from the country. But those who fail to apply and claim back such holdings within six months following their entrance to the country shall be required to convert them to Gold Yuan notes according to the stipu lations of Article 3 above. 

With the exception of the Central Bank of China, no Chinese or foreign bank, unless duly appointed by the Central Bank of China, may buy, hold or take custody of gold, silver, silver currency or foreign currency notes.

Art. 12. In case of violations of the stipulations of Articles 3 and 4 of these regulations involving

failure to effect the required conversion or deposit within the prescribed period, or violations of any stipulations contained in Articles 8, 9, 10 and 11 of these Regulations, the gold, silver, silver currency and/ or foreign currency notes concerned shall all be confiscated. 

Art. 13. In case of violations of the stipulations of Article 7 of these regulations, or violations of the stipulations of Article 11 involving unauthorized pur chase of gold, silver, silver currency or foreign currency notes, the objects concerned shall be liable to confiscation; and, in addition, where violations of 

Article 7 involve transaction in gold ornaments at prices exceeding the prescribed conversion rates, or where violations of Article 11 involve unauthorized purchase of gold or foreign currency notes, such violations shall also be subject to punishment according to the Regulations Governing Punishment of Trans actions in Gold and Foreign Currencies. 

Art. 14. In case of violations of the stipulations of Articles 4 and 5 of these regulations involving failure to effect the required conversion or deposit within the prescribed period, or violations of Articles 

7 to 11 if the violations are discovered and objects concerned are confiscated as a result of information furnished to the authorities in charge, 40 per cent of the confiscated value shall be given as reward to the informant.

Art. 15. These Regulations shall come into force on the date of promulgation.

III. Regulations Governing the Registration and Control of Foreign Exchange Assets Held Abroad by Nationals of the Republic of China 

Art. 1. By virtue of the stipulations of Article 3, Section 9, of the National General Mobilization Law, foreign exchange assets are hereby designated as one of the resources subject to national general mobilization; and in accordance with the stipulations of article 7, Section 1, of the same law, the use, removal and transfer of such assets are hereby placed under control.

Art. 2. For the purpose of realizing the above mentioned object, registration shall be required of foreign exchange assets held abroad by nationals of the Republic of China.

Art. 3. "Nationals of the Republic of China" used in these regulations include natural persons, juristic persons and other corporate bodies. 

Art. 4. "Foreign exchange assets" used in these regulations refer to current and fixed deposits abroad, foreign currencies, gold ingots and bars held abroad, and all rights and benefits arising from payments derived or derivable abroad, including foreign currency securities of any foreign Government or the Chinese Government, stocks and shares, bonds and 

Treasury notes, land title deeds, insurance policies, annuities, forward payments collectable, advance pay ments in buying and selling, margin deposits in security transactions as well as all negotiable instruments.

Art. 5. With the exception of those nationals whose normal livelihood is verified to be established abroad and who are considered as Overseas Chinese, all nationals of the Republic of China shall, before 

December 31, 1948, file declaration and registration according to prescribed forms with the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks with respect to the foreign exchange assets they hold abroad as of August 21, 1948. Where any foreign exchange asset is acquired after August 21, 1948, the declaration and registration of same must be effected within two months following its acquisition.

Art. 6. Nationals of the Republic of China who presently reside abroad and who are subject to the restrictions of the preceding Article, may file the regis tration of their foreign exchange assets in accordance with the stipulations of the preceding Article with the local or nearby office of the Chinese embassy, legation, consulate or special commissioner of the Waichiaopu. Nationals referred to in the preceding Paragraph who hold foreign exchange assets of a value not exceeding U. S. $3,000, or its equivalent in other foreign currencies, are exempted from registration. 

Art. 7. Foreign exchange assets required to be registered under these Regulations include assets which are held in trust for any national of the Republic of China by any agent, trustee, broker, or any juristic person or other corporate body registered abroad. For eign exchange assets referred to in the preceding Paragraph, regardless of whether they are held jointly with a foreign national or foreign juristic person or other corporate body and whether they are under independ ent or joint control, must be declared and registered with respect to the portion held by the Chinese national concerned.

Art. 8. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, no national of the Republic of China shall be allowed to transfer his foreign exchange assets to any natural person, juristic person or other corporate body whatsoever at home or abroad with the intention of evading the registration. 

* Ministry of Foreign Affairs.

יך 

Art. 9. With respect to foreign exchange assets re quired to be registered under these Regulations, all bank deposits and foreign currencies as well as benefits derived from assets or the sale thereof, must be transferred in their original currencies to and deposited in the Central Bank of China or any of its appointed banks, with the exception of the prescribed amount which the holder may be permitted to keep by approval of the Ministry of Finance according to the following provisions: (1) Living and medical expenses for a national and his family residing abroad; (2) Educational expenses for a national or his children during the period of study abroad; and (3) Expenses for travelling abroad and return trip to China for a national or his family.

The nationals referred to in the preceding paragraphs are limited to those who are already abroad previous to the promulgation of these Regulations. 

The amount allowed to be kept shall be fixed separately.

Art. 10. Foreign exchange deposits transferred to and deposited in the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks may be utilized according to the following provisions: (1) For legitimate purposes approved by the 'Ministry of Finance; (2) For payment of imports covered by import licenses; and (3) For conversion to Gold Yuan notes, purchase of the 36th Year U. S. Gold Bonds or Gold Yuan bonds to be issued by the Government.

Art. 11. Nationals of the Republic of China who violate any stipulation of Articles 5 to 8 of these Regulations shall, in accordance with the stipulations of 

Article 5 of the Provisional Regulations Governing 

Punishment of Infringement on the National General Mobilization Law, be liable to imprisonment under seven years and may also be subject to fines. In case of above-mentioned violations of the stipulations of these Regulations, the foreign exchange assets held abroad shall, following the imposition of punishment, be confiscated by the Government after consultations with the foreign Government concerned. 

Art. 12. Where violations of Articles 3 to 8 of these 

Regulations are discovered and punishment and confiscation are enforced as a result of information furnished to the authorities in charge, 40 per cent of the value shall be given as reward to the informant. 

Art. 13. In each of the municipalities of Nanking, Shanghai, Tientsin, Canton and Hankow (and other localities to be designated by the Executive Yuan), a commission on direction or declaration and registration of foreign exchange assets of nationals of the Republic of China shall be established immediately following the promulgation of these Regulations. The commission shall consist of the Mayor, Chairman of the Municipal Council, a representative of the Ministry of Finance, a representative of the Central Bank of China, and 3 to 5 other members to be appointed by the Mayor from among the members of the Municipal Council and other local bodies organized according to law. The Mayor and Chairman of the 

Municipal Council shall be the convenors of the commission, the administrative staff of which shall be furnished by the Ministry of Finance, Central Bank of China and the local municipal government. The functions of the aforesaid commission shall be as 

(1) To acquaint the local community with the contents of these Regulations; (2) To direct and assist the applicants in the matter of Registration; (3) To be responsible for ascertaining the rich people and merchants in the local community and advising them in the observance of these Regulations; and (4) To receive secret information regarding evasion of declaration of foreign exchange assets and to refer such cases to the authorities in charge. 

Art. 14. In localities abroad designated by the 

Executive Yuan, the Chinese embassy, legation and/or local consulate shall be responsible for organizing a commission similar to the commission described in the preceding article, the detailed rules to be adopted by the Executive Yuan.

Art. 15. These Regulations shall come into force on the date of promulgation.

IV. Regulations Governing Financial Reform and Economic Control 

Art. 1. These Regulations are adopted with a view to balancing the national budget, adjusting international payments, and strengthening measures for the stabilization of prices and wages as well as the control of banking and financial operations. 

Art. 2. Revenues from various taxes are to be vigorously increased. Where tax rates are lower than their respective pre-war standards, they are to be ad-

justed accordingly, and rates on luxuries are to be raised.

Where existing laws require revision in connection with the aforesaid development of tax revenues, the Ministry of Finance shall immediately submit recommendations to the Executive Yuan for transmission to the Legislative Yuan.

Art. 3. Where charges made by state-operated public utilities and communications enterprises are lower than their respective pre-war standards, they may be adjusted accordingly so as to make the enterprises self-supporting. Subsidies provided by the National 

Treasury shall be limited to areas that are subject to destructions by military activities.

Art. 4. All state-operated enterprises must as far as possible economize expenses and remove superfluous personnel. Those having surplus profits shall be ordered by the respective Ministries and Commissions in charge to remit all such profits to the National Treasury.

Art. 5. The sale of "surplus supplies" as well as supplies and properties taken over from the enemy and puppets shall be accelerated as far as possible in order to augment treasury receipts.

Art. 6. Personnel in all civil and military organi zations as well as the number of soldiers are to be strictly checked; no false report to be condoned.

Art. 7. The budget of national receipts and ex penditures for the second half-year of the 37th year shall, following the issuance of Gold Yuan notes, be compiled anew in terms of Gold Yuan in accordance with this mandate on emergency measures. Where actual conditions require revision of estimates, the 

Executive Yuan shall request the Legislative Yuan for re-examination.

Art. 8. Government organs and people requiring foreign exchange for legitimate purposes may buy foreign exchange upon approval of the Government. 

Existing regulations governing control of foreign exchange by the Central Bank of China are to be revised according to the relevant stipulations of this mandate. 

Art. 9. Control of imports and exports shall be adjusted according to the following provisions: (1) Beginning with the 7th quarter,* the import quota shall be cut down by at least one-fourth on the basis of the average set for the 5th and 6th quarters. 

(2) In case of quota goods referred to in the preceding Paragraph and of certain other categories 

* August-October, 1948. 

** February-July, 1948. 

of goods to be designated, people may apply for permission to pay the value of such imports out of their foreign currency deposits in the Central Bank of China. (3) All foreign exchange realized by exporters from exports shall be sold to the Central Bank of 

China at the exchange rates fixed between Gold Yuan and foreign currencies. (4) In case of supplies that may be exported, their production shall be encouraged and restrictions may be imposed on their domestic consumption. 

Art. 10. Overseas Chinese remittances shall be sold to the Central Bank of China at the exchange rates fixed between Gold Yuan and foreign currencies, and Government banks are to extend to them all facilities. 

Art. 11. The foreign exchange rates to be used in connection with Articles 8, 9 and 10 above shall be fixed in the following manner: 

(1) One United States Dollar to 4 Gold Yuan. 

(2) Rates for other foreign currencies to be fixed from time to time by the Central Bank of China in relation to the exchange rate between the United 

States Dollar and the Gold Yuan. 

Art. 12. In case of supplies that must be imported from abroad, strict economy in their consumption is to be exercised according to the following provisions: (1) In Shanghai and other cities designated by the Executive Yuan, licenses for all kinds of motor cars shall be reduced by one-quarter to one-third within two months following the promulgation of these Regulations, and strict restrictions shall be enforced with respect to the quantity of gasoline consumed by such cars. (2) As from September 15, 1948, the sale of articles whose importation is prohibited shall be banned in designated cities, and violations shall be dealt with as smuggling, detailed rules to be adopted by the Ministry of Industry and Trade together with the Ministry of Finance. 

Art. 13. In all cities throughout the country, prices of all commodities and services shall be charged on the levels prevailing in each particular city on August 19, 1948, when converted in Gold Yuan at the prescribed rates. Local authorities shall see to it that this limitation is strictly enforced.

Art. 14. After the conversion of prices of all commodities and services in terms of Gold Yuan according

00 to the stipulations of the preceding Article, the Regulations Governing Punishment of Violations of Price Restrictions must be strictly enforced. No increase in price shall be permitted unless approved by the authorities in charge for special reasons.

Art. 15. All public utilities and communications enterprises shall collect charges in terms of Gold Yuan; aside from adjustments that may be made by stateoperated enterprises according to the provisions of Article 3 above and those made by private enterprises in keeping with the same Article as well as with actual costs, all of which adjustments being subject to approval of the authorities in charge, no increase may be sanctioned hereafter unless for special reasons. 

Art. 16. In Shanghai and other cities designated by the Executive Yuan, inspection of godowns and registration of incoming and outgoing cargoes shall be enforced. Violations of the Regulations Governing 

Prohibition of Hoarding and Profiteering in Articles of Daily Necessity During the Emergency Period shall be punished strictly according to law.

Art. 17. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, newspapers, news service dispatches and other printed matters shall not publish the blackmarket prices of gold, silver or foreign currencies or of any kinds of articles of daily necessity. Violations shall be punishable according to the stipulations of 

Article 10 of the Temporary Regulations Governing 

Punishment of Infringement of National General 

Mobilization. 

Art. 18. As from date on enforcement of the Gold

Yuan unit, all arrangements regarding payment of salaries and wages on the basis of the cost-of-living index shall be discontinued. 

Art. 19. The salaries of civil and military officials shall be paid in Gold Yuan notes according to the following standard. The basic amount shall be 40 dollars to be paid in full in Gold Yuan notes. Twenty per cent of the sum in excess of 40 dollars up to 300 dollars shall be paid in Gold Yuan notes, and ten per cent shall be paid in Gold Yuan notes for any amount above 300 dollars. The pay and food rations of the enlisted men shall be paid in Gold Yuan notes according to the prewar basic amount without discount.

Art. 20. Civil and Army personnel and soldiers in the Nanking-Shanghai area shall be paid according to the standard prescribed in the preceding article. 

In other areas where the original cost-of-living index numbers are higher or lower than those in the Nan-king-Shanghai area, a certain percentage of increase or decrease in pay according to the aforesaid standard shall be fixed by the Executive Yuan, based on the difference between the respective index numbers in such areas and those in the Nanking-Shanghai area for the month of July, 1948.

Art. 21. The pay for employees and laborers in state-operated enterprises shall be made in Gold Yuan according to the stipulations of Articles 18, 19 and 20 above. Excepting that employees of such enterprises may be allowed an additional pay not exceeding 30 per cent of the pay which civil employees of corresponding ranks or grades receive, any portion of pay which such an employee actually receives in excess of the maximum limit must be abolished. 

Art. 22. The salaries and wages for employees and laborers in private enterprises shall all be converted and paid in Gold Yuan, but the pay for half a month shall not exceed the equivalent amounts which such persons were entitled to receive in terms of fapi according to the original arrangements of the enterprises concerned during the first half-month of August, 1948. 

Art. 23. During the period of enforcement of these 

Regulations, closure of factories, strikes and go-slow strikes are prohibited. Violations shall be liable to punishment according to the stipulations of Section 4 

of Article 5 of the Provisional Regulations Governing 

Punishment of Infringement of National General Mobilization Law.

Art. 24. 

Government banks and other financial institutions shall absolutely stop the extension of loans of a commercial nature and shall be charged with the duty of examining the utilization of funds and the results thereof in connection with loans which they may be authorized to extend. Appropriate measures to this end shall be adopted by the authorities in charge and be strictly enforced. 

Art. 25. Commercial banks and native banks shall strictly observe the Banking Law and all regulations of financial control in the operation of their business activities. They shall not engage in any form of operations involving dealings in commodities. Violations shall be subject to cancellation of their business licenses and shall also be dealt with as hoarding and profiteering. 

Art. 26. Credit cooperative societies, besides accepting deposits from their members and from employing such deposits and the societies' capital funds for extension of credits to their members, are prohibited from engaging in other business activities of banking institutions. Violations shall be punished according to Regulations Governing Illegitimate Banking Establishments, aside from being ordered to be disorganized.

Art. 27. With the exception of banking institu tions, no company or shop whatsoever may receive deposits or extend loans. Violations shall be punished according to Regulations Governing Illegitimate Banking Establishments, aside from being ordered to be disorganized.

Art. 28. The Ministry of Finance shall inspect the business activities of Chinese banks maintaining branch offices abroad, and shall order the abolition within a prescribed period of these branch offices whose record is found unsatisfactory. 

Art. 29. Any banking institution shall have its business license revoked if found to have committed one of the following offenses: (1) If the clearance of its bills has been stopped by the clearing house; (2) If it violates economic control regulations; or (3) If its capital is too small and its business cannot be developed in the proper way. 

Art. 30. The Ministry of Finance shall, by refer ence to the provisions of the pre-war Banking Law with regard to the minimum capital of banks, revise the requirements concerning the minimum amounts of capital of banks, native banks and trust companies in the various areas and upon approval of its recommendations by the Executive Yuan, shall order the banks concerned to increase their capital up to the required minimum amounts within two months. The increase of capital shall consist of not less than 50 per cent in cash. Any bank unable to effect the required increase in capital within the prescribed period shall be ordered to suspend business and be liquidated within a fixed period. 

Art. 31. The stock exchange in Shanghai and Tien tsin shall suspend business temporarily and may not re-open unless approved by the Executive Yuan. 

Art. 32. Market interest rates shall be lowered and domestic remittance charge shall be adjusted by areas, so as to enliven financial activities and protect production enterprise. Effective measures to these ends shall be taken by the Ministry of Finance and the Central Bank of China.

Art. 33. These Regulations shall come into force on the date of promulgation.

SEPTEMBER 15, 1948

RURAL RECONSTRUCTION 

Notes were exchanged between China and the 

United States on August 5, 1948, providing for the establishment of a Sino-American Commission on Rural Reconstruction in China. The creation of the 

Commission is a sequel to the eleven-week field tour, covering 15 provinces in China, of the China-United

States Agricultural Mission in 1946. Basing its activi ties largely on the findings and recommendations of the 1946 Mission, the Commission will coordinate extension-type programs in agriculture, consult with the Chinese Government regarding ways and means of progressively carrying out land reform measures, initiate in suitable locations subsidiary projects in research, training, and manufacturing, etc., introduce health and sanitation measures, and recommend the distribution of assistance.

Undismayed by ill-informed critics and Communistsympathizing writers and commentators (who heed neither the history of rural China and the varying regional traditions and practices in the different sections of a vast country nor the elemental economic fact that the Chinese farm population has grown to a size no longer supportable by land available and antiquated farming methods), the Chinese Government has been steadfastly studying, experimenting with, and adopting realistic methods of relieving hardships and progressively introducing improvements. Communist rampage and ruthlessness have complicated farm problems in the wide and shifting combat zones, but the Chinese Reds, misrepresented in many quarters as "agrarian reformers" or saviors of China's peasantry, have not had the success or the rural support in the pursuance of their brutal land reform measures frequently attributed to them.

Contradicting the decisions proclaimed by the socalled Communist land conference in the autumn of 1947, the Central Committee of the Chinese Communist Party recently directed its agents in Communistdominated parts of Central China to abandon "land 

No. 17 reform" in favor of rental and interest reduction. 

According to the Communist press, the reasons that prompted the drastic reversal of policy are inadequate preparation and the "spectator-like" attitude of the alleged beneficiaries, the small farmers, who lack confidence in the success of Communist insurrection and doubt "the People's Liberation Army's" ability to hold out long. "Hoodlums” are said to be profiteering and spreading terrorism while poor farmers in general receive no benefit.

This Communist admission is highly significant for, on the one hand, it proves the fundamental stability and the level-headedness of China's peasantry and invalidates completely calculated misrepresentations like the portrayal of the Red rebellion in China as the spearhead of an organized agrarian revolt. On the other hand, it banishes the falsehood that forcible, indiscriminate, and irresponsible seizure and redistribution of land and farming equipment contribute either to agrarian security or to the well-being and prosperity of farmers in China.

Rural rehabilitation and reconstruction measures undertaken by the Chinese Government since Japan's surrender, and even before that, have been steady although slow. They take into consideration prevailing local traditions and conditions and are guided by the long-range interests of those who are to benefit rather than by short-lived, political expediency. Studies and evaluation of the results of experiments have been continuous. New land projects have been introduced where conditions permit. Capable farmers are being helped in increasing numbers to become owner-operators. Cooperative farms have been sponsored where they are practicable. Land reclamation, the extension of rural loans, and irrigation projects are being pursued simultaneously, for the mere transfer of title to uneconomical farms only means the redistribution of poverty or hardships.

Recently, a new plan whereby all capable tenant

farmers in China could become independent was submitted by the China Land Reform Association to the legislative branch of the Chinese Government for consideration. In the meantime, the Executive Yuan (Cabinet) instructed all provinces to work out a land distribution plan which would conform to local conditions and needs. 

Land Distribution

In a message to the National Land Administration 

Conference held in September, 1947, President Chiang

Kai-shek stressed that "the promotion of independent farming is conducive towards the gradual realization of Dr. Sun Yat-sen's ideal of 'land for the tillers.' 

The principal methods of increasing independent farming are: (1) purchase of land from big owners by the Government for distribution to farmers; and (2) purchase of land by tenant farmers with rural loans extended to them by the Government. 

Since 1943, the Chinese Government has bought over and distributed about 205,166 acres of land to 

52,117 tenant farmers. Most of the land distributed is in China's coastal Fukien Province, where a total of about 41,578 acres was apportioned to 31,466 farmers between September, 1943, and July, 1947.

The second largest land distribution is being effected in the densely populated Kiangsu Province. Up to September, 1947, the Government had allocated about 17,644 acres in this province in four counties north of the Yangtze River.

In the northwest Kansu Province, approximately 4,274 acres irrigated by the Huanghui Canal were bought by the Government in 1946 and then divided into 970 farms for allocation. Following the completion of the Tsing Feng Canal in the same year, arable land irrigated by the waterway was distributed to over 2,000 farmers. Another 1,968 acres were converted into cooperative farms.

In Suiyuan Province, Government land has been allocated to farmers at low cost, benefitting 15,521 farmers in 7 counties. The other 4 provinces where land distribution has been in force are Kiangsi, Kwangsi, Szechwan, and Hunan.¹

Government Rural Loans 

Side by side with the equitable distribution of land, the Government loaned money to farmers at low interest rates to enable them to redeem mortgaged land. 

In Fukien, a total of Ch$983,000,000 was loaned out in 1946 which redeemed for 408 farmers about 516 acres of land. In 1947, an additional loan total of 

Ch$860,000,000 was extended to the farmers in the same area. In Szechwan, over Ch$58,000,000 has been loaned to 559 farmers by the Government, enabling them to buy back about 848 acres of land. In Shensi, Ch$743,000,000 was loaned to 426 farmers in 1946, redeeming about 539 acres of land, and Ch$700,000,000 in 1947. In Chekiang, Ch$99,650,000 was loaned to farmers to redeem about 150 acres of land in 1946. 

1 Special Release SA:386, Voice of China, Jan. 7, 1948, Chinese News 

Service. Loans extended to farmers in other provinces in recent years by the Government are as follows: Kiangsi, Ch$110,000,000 in 1946 and Ch$1,200,000,000 in 1947; 

Kweichow, Ch$117,000,000; Honan, Ch$500,000,000 in 1947; Hupeh, Ch$21,400,000; Ningsia, Ch$270,000.

Summing up, a grand total of Ch$3,836,000,000 of rural loans has been extended by the Government to tenant farmers for land purchases or clearing mortgages.2 According to a statement of the Farmers Bank of China, 47,644 farming households in 75 hsien (counties) of 13 provinces received loans from the Bank since V-J Day for purchasing or redeeming an aggregate area of 115,000 acres.³ 

Land Progress in North China 

Of the Communist-disturbed North China provinces, Suiyuan has its land problem largely solved. 

Governor Fu Tso-yi initiated a land program in Suiyuan as early as 1930, and under the program much land was reclaimed and cultivated. Up to the end of 

1946, 383,874 acres of arable land had been added to the original 221,876 acres in that province. 

Statistics compiled by the Department of Land Administration of Suiyuan Province indicate that, in the western part of the province where the best land is found, there were at one time 33,859 households which did not possess land. Landowners and independent farmers totalled 13,776 households. Today, all the landless households have land of their own to till. 

Some 79,216 acres of public land have been turned into 8 cooperative farms which support 9,261 households. About 54,410 acres have been distributed among tenant farmers and hired hands, benefitting 6,260 households. Another 216 acres have been distributed among 28 veterans' families.

In Hopei Province, a new rural plan took shape last June under the same leadership after General Fu

Tso-yi was appointed Commander-in-Chief of the 

North China Bandit-Suppression Headquarters. One of the things the plan emphasizes is the encouragement of initiative on the part of the local population in their fight against the Communists. Special privilege is accorded to members of the People's Self-Preservation Corps, which is composed of Governmentequipped, local militia. Independent land tillers belonging to the organization are encouraged to regain their farms from the Communists. Others, who do not own land, will be so awarded for meritorious service. 

Landowners who are members of the Corps may retain their holdings provided they do not exceed the maximum acreage permitted under the new program. 

However, absentee landlords are to surrender their property to the Government in exchange for bonds. 

In Shansi, the semi-military land program of Governor Yen Hsi-shan has been successful. All arable land in this Communist-besieged province has been divided into "ration units" and distributed to soldier-farmers. 

The original landowners retain title to their property. 

Governor Yen was the first to allocate his own land. 

2 Op. cit. and the Central Daily News, Nanking, China, Dec. 30, 1947.

Chinese Government Land Reform Measures Since V-J Day, Chinese 

News Service, p. 4.

Reclamation Centers 

For the rehabilitation of over 4,000 veterans, five centers have been established under a Government land reclamation plan. The Government subsidy to these centers this year alone runs up to Ch$410,000,- 000,000.

Of the five reclamation centers, the best known is the Panshan Farm in Liaoning Province with about 16,667 acres of arable land. The Japanese, during their occupation of Manchuria, seized much food from this Farm. After the war, the Farm passed through several hands before it was finally set up as a center for retired Army officers. At present, 1,350 of them and their dependents work and live on the Farm. The acreage of the Farm is expected to be doubled by the end of this year.

Second in importance is the center located on the banks of Tung Ting Lake in Hunan Province. Ten tractors, gifts from UNRRA, are turning up the vir gin soil at the rate of five acres daily. Already 6,667 acres have been reclaimed and a similar acreage will be added before the end of this year. Working on the land are 1,300 officers and 4,000 dependents.

The Tienkiang Farm is located in the eastern part of mountainous Szechwan. Altogether 450 retired of ficers and over 1,000 of their relatives live on the Farm, raising tobacco and making good profits. If they carry on according to schedule, this reclamation center may expand to 5,000 acres this year. 

The fourth center is at Shuichong in Kiangsi Province, only some 18 miles from Kiukiang. Some 200 officers and 800 dependents settled there early this year. 

They plan to reclaim 2,333 acres by December. 

The fifth center is located in southern Shensi. To that center about 1,000 officers and their families have been assigned.

New Land Reform Plan 

On the basis of the findings and the needs of the present rural situation in China, the China Land Reform Association, a powerful farmers' organization with hundreds of chapters in 17 provinces, proposed on March 20, 1948, a new land program.¹ The detailed plan was published on July 4 prior to its submission to the Legislative Yuan for consideration. 

According to the plan, each farmer is permitted the ownership of only enough land to support a family of eight, and excesses are to be sold to other farmers. 

Payments may be made over a period of 14 years in equal installments. The total payments should not exceed seven times the annual rental of the land in question. In case of a famine or drought, payments may be postponed or reduced with the approval of the Government authorities.

Article 10 states that if the total annual income of a landowner from his holdings is double the earnings of an independent farmer the annual installment may be reduced by 25 per cent. If the property owner's income is three times the farmer's this reduction rate may be doubled, and if it is five times what a farmer 

Ta Kung Pao, Shanghai, March 21, 1948. earns all installments may be stopped. Soldiers in active service, veterans, and their dependents are to be provided with land by the Government. 

Meanwhile, new land measures for the provinces of Kwangsi, Kansu, and Yunnan were approved by the Ministry of Land at the end of July. The measures decree that land in excess of the maximum set shall be sold or the Government shall purchase it with land bonds. In Kwangsi, each household is allowed to own 

8.3 acres of first class rice fields, or 12.5 acres of second grade, or 16.7 acres of third grade land. The limitations set on dry land are double those of the three grades of rice paddies. In Yunnan, no household may own more than 10 acres of first class, or 16.7 acres of second class, or 25 acres of third class rice fields. There is no allowance for owning more of various classes of dry land. In Kansu, each household is allowed to own 

8.3 acres of first class, 16.7 acres of second class, 33.3 acres of third class arable land, or 50 acres of arid land. A household is taken to mean 10 persons. 

Landlords owning more acreage than the specified maximum are required to report their holdings to the local governments. After receiving such reports, the local authorities will announce public sales of the excess land. The prices are to be settled by the parties concerned. Should the landlords fail of their own accord to dispose of excess land within a time limit, the Government will purchase it with land bonds. 

In Kiangsi, a land reform program was recently adopted. Under the program, no individual may own more than 8.3 acres of superior grade wet land, or 12.5 acres of medium grade wet land, or 16.7 acres of inferior land. If it is dry land, the maximum holding may be doubled in all cases. If a farmer has a family of more than 10 persons, his quota may be raised proportionately.

Hunan, a neighboring province of Kiangsi, is considering the adoption of similar measures. 

Experimental Stations 

Among the projects for promoting independent. farming, those in Peipeh of Szechwan, in Lungyen of Fukien, and in the area of Huanghui Canal of Kansu have shown notable results. Incidentally, they illustrate, among others, three types of land problems in China.5

In Peipeh of Szechwan, farm land was once largely concentrated in the hands of landlords. About 64 per cent of the arable land in the area was tilled by tenant farmers. Lungyen of Fukien was under Communist control in 1929, when farm land was forcibly distributed and redistributed resulting in unprecedented confusion of ownership. In the irrigated area of Huanghui Canal in Kansu, about 5,000 acres of dry land at once became arable when the waterway was completed. As a result, land value suddenly soared. 

The Peipeh Project® 

The experimental program in Peipeh started in March, 1942, in a community of 126 households with 

Assistance to Independent Farmers and Protection of Tenants' Rights 

(in Chinese), published by the Government Information Office, Nanking, China, in November, 1947, pp. 12-13.

Op. cit., pp. 35-74; and Report of the China-United States Agricul tural Mission, published by Office of Foreign Agricultural Relations, U.S.D.A., Washington, D. C. in May, 1947, pp. 225-228.

a total population of 426 persons, under the joint sponsorship of the Peipeh County Government and the Farmers Bank of China. Of the 126 households, 19 were absentee landlords, 16 local landlords, 2 landlords who rented out part of their land and operated the rest, 30 owner-operators, 3 part owners, and 47 tenants. The cultivated area involved was about 238 acres, of which about 50 per cent was owned by 10 landlords.

All the farm land in the area was purchased by the 

Government from the landlords. A loan of Ch$1,999,- 

500 was made on May 1, 1943, through the Farmers 

Bank of China to finance the purchase. The landlords were paid in cash for their land. The purchased land was divided into 80 consolidated farming units ranging from 2.5 to 3.3 acres, redistributed to 80 selected tenant farmers of the area. 

Steps involved in the purchase were: (1) Each piece of land in the area was registered in order to clear titles; (2) a detailed survey was made of topography, soil type, and size of each area; (3) an appraisal was made of each piece of field; and (4) the County Government gave public notice to landlords to come into the office to submit their claims and accept payment.? 

Of the 238 acres of land, 102 acres were redistributed to tenants after consolidation. Owners were allowed to retain their original farm land for their own cultivation. The land-purchase price financed by the 

Farmers Bank of China was Ch$1,999,500 at the interest rate of 9.6 per cent per annum for a term of 15 years, but this sum was paid off by tenant purchasers in 3 years.

After the redistribution of land in Peipeh, it was not only that all the tillers at once became owners of their land but production of crops increased from 

203,490 pounds in 1943 to 285,950 pounds in 1944 and to 319,200 pounds in 1945. The increase was due to the improved irrigation system, the extensive use of fertilizer, the adoption of selected seeds, the consolidation of farming units, and the technical improvement of cultivation in addition to the enthusiastic efforts of the farmers. Production was increased without increase of operational expenses.

The Lungyen Experiments 

The total cultivated area of Lungyen in Fukien was about 47,165 acres, of which about 34,083 acres or more than 72 per cent were operated by tenant farmers. The number of tenant farmers represented 

89 per cent of the total population in the area. 

The Lungyen project was started in February, 1943. 

All the land in the designated area, one time distributed and redistributed by the Communists, was requisitioned by the Government. The local government gave notice to landowners to submit their claims 

Report of the China-United States Agricultural Mission, p. 225. 

8 Assistance to Independent Farmers and Protection of Tenants' Rights, pp. 14-24. within one month and at the same time made a detailed survey of topology, soil, irrigation, and other conditions of the land. The land was then divided into units ranging from about one to two acres according to the quality of land. Original independent farmers, tenant farmers, and families of military service men with ability of cultivation were given first priority. However, if the land of the original operator exceeded the amount of a unit, he had to give up the surplus to landless farmers. Tenant farmers who received land from the Government were to pay off the price to the Farmers Bank of China in ten years.

The Huanghui Canal Project⁹ 

This experimental district includes about 4,244 acres of land watered by the Huanghui Canal in Kansu, of which 3,843 acres were owned by local landlords, 348 acres by absentee landlords, and 46 acres by the Government. There were 521 landowners in the district. Of the 3,843 acres owned by local landlords, about 50 per cent was owned by 9 big landlords, while the 143 small landowners possessed only 4 per cent. There were also several hundred households of landless farmers in the district. 

The land project in the Huanghui district was started in 1943. All the land in the district which soared sharply following the completion of the Canal was bought over by the Government. The purchase was financed through a loan of Ch$12,800,000 by the Farmers Bank of China. The land was redivided into 

1,162 units and a cooperative farm of 43 acres. All farmers, male or female, of good conduct and with proven capability received land. Priority was given to those who originally operated farms. 

More Land, Industrialization Needed 

The breaking up of large land holdings and reasonable and progressive land ownership equalization are important rural reconstruction measures in China. 

However, such measures alone cannot solve China's land problem for the aggregate farm land available in China is not increased by mere breaking up and redistribution. The surplus farm labor and their dependents who overburden the farms have to be absorbed and supported by more handicraft and industrial enterprises. In China, a farm household has less than 4 acres on the average. Even by the most intensive farming, the yield of such acreage affords only the barest living. Along the Yangtze and in South China, the average size farm is even less than two acres, while an average American farmer operates about 170 acres. 

China's farm land can be increased gradually and the standard of living of the farm population progressively raised only through responsible planning and realistic action based upon the findings and recommendations of qualified and unbiased experts who know what they are talking about. 

Op. cit., pp. 24-35.

OCTOBER 1, 1948 NEW SOCIAL MOVEMENT 

President Chiang Kai-shek, in his capacity as one of China's citizens, launched on September 15, in a radio speech to the nation, a new social movement calling on the people to reduce consumption and increase production so as to hasten the successful conclusion of the Communist-suppression campaign and to expedite national reconstruction. In addition to the treacherous rebels, the Chief Executive said, social evils such as speculation, manipulation, and high living are also to blame for the present crisis in 

China. The new movement, aiming at industriousness, higher productivity, and thrift to promote national reconstruction, is expected to be vigorously pushed to reduce disparity of living standards between the wealthy and poor, to rectify social injustices, and to raise the morale of the people and the fighting services. 

The movement will be closely coordinated with the Government measures on mobilization against the Communist rebels, monetary and fiscal reforms, economic regulations, and decrees against corruption. 

Initial endeavors will be centered around Nanking, Shanghai, and other leading cities, to be extended gradually to the other parts of the country. The maximum number of persons in a basic unit of the movement is limited to 15.

Following is a full-text translation of the president's radio speech as well as the working program of the 

To Stabilize Social Order 

"Our most urgent need today is to deliver our people from suffering and to stabilize social order. As a prerequisite we must put down the Communist rebellion at an early date. This is the unanimous yearning as well as the common responsibility of our people as a whole.

"It follows that we should all realize the weightiness of our responsibility and the stupendousness of our mission. It behooves us all to muster all the resoluteness and courage that is in us and work together as a team in order to overcome the last obstacle to the successful conclusion of our national revolution. This is also the only way whereby we can attain the common goal of our people as a whole. "My fellow-countrymen, we should also understand that, in addition to the treacherous rebels who are rampant, speculation, manipulation, and high living to the point of lasciviousness on the part of the social parasites in our midst are also to blame for our crisis today. Likewise, not only economic dislocation but extravagance and moral degradation on the part of the exploiters are hindering us in our national reconstruction efforts. These factors have caused the existing low morale and social injustices among our people. "Therefore, in my capacity as one of China's citizens, I am initiating today this Industriousness and Austerity for National Reconstruction Movement and I am also going to announce its working program. 

It is my intention to wash away the social dregs by opening the floodgates of public consciousness and social justice. In this way we shall be further insuring victory in our campaign against the Communist rebels and success in our program of national reconstruction. "I fervently hope that all our people will rally behind this movement and observe its working program implicitly, thereby discharging their responsibility under our dual national endeavor, namely, suppressing the rebellion and rebuilding our country.

"Program for the Movement: 1. 

The Essentials of the Movement 

"Following the victorious conclusion of the War of Resistance against Japan, China has met two new serious threats. One is the all-out armed rebellion of the Chinese Communists, and the other is the resultant economic destruction and social injustices. 

Whether these two threats can be removed will determine our chance of building a new China or even the very existence of the Chinese nation. "Today, all our patriotic armed forces and civilians are engaged in a life-and-death struggle against the traitorous Communists, the public enemy of the nation. The majority of the people, though subsisting barely above the starvation level, are contributing both money and energy, while others are sacrificing their lives to sustain the war against the rebels and for national salvation. There are, however, some people who, like parasites, do not work and produce, and others who, as unscrupulous speculators, are engaged in hoarding and manipulation so as to seize profit and wealth. Leading a licentious life, they have not only undermined the nation's productive power but also created a demoralizing effect on the armed forces and the people at large. "To correct this state of affairs and positively to enforce the total war plan which requires the concentration of the nation's manpower and material and financial resources, we cannot but ask the parasitic elements and speculators to repent, and through selfawakening and self-denial, to learn to be industrious and frugal. This is the way to rid themselves of laziness and licentiousness and to keep pace with the working masses in a common effort to increase production and to reduce consumption. "This is the essence of our Industriousness and Austerity for National Reconstruction Movement. This movement is not only a starting point for improving social practices and effecting social reforms but also a link in the general mobilization for the suppression of the Communist rebellion and for national reconstruction. In this movement, it is hoped our people will do everything to realize the following two points: (1) To reduce consumption in public and private life to the lowest possible level; (2) To increase work, production and the fighting strength to the highest possible degree." 

2. The Purpose of the Movement 

"This movement is a practical step at this stage of our national reconstruction. Its mission, in point of history, is to rally all patriotic people to become selfreliant, self-helping, self-strengthening, and self-denying. They must lead an industrious life and influence others through persuasion, conversion, and service, so as to harness the social strength of spontaneity and self-control and to check the tendency towards the two extremes of wealth and poverty. Eventually, life at the front will move downward to the soldiers' level and life in the rear to the common people's level. "Economically, the movement aims at removing the excessive inequality through the control and reduction of consumption, and at increasing production through the extension of work to more of the people and through heightened efficiency. On the other hand, the people's livelihood and the nation's economic wellbeing must be well adjusted through the prevention of uncontrolled extravagance on the part of big merchants, wealthy gentry, and high officials. On the other hand, we must preserve and develop the nation's potential for economic reconstruction and national defense through hard work and increased production. 

The eradication of wastefulness will result in the stabilization of commodity prices, of the currency, and in the balance of the country's international payments. "Politically, the movement aims at stamping out corruption and at increasing efficiency through industriousness for higher production and through frugality. 

The total war plan must be thoroughly enforced, and the nation's manpower and financial and material resources must be geared to military needs in order to fortify the fighting power of our armed forces for a quick termination of the Communist rebellion. With life at the front moving down to the soldiers' level and that in the rear leveling down to life of the common people, the existing disparity between the wealthy and the poor will be narrowed. As a result, psychological solidarity and unity in action will be possible and the nation can become a fighting body." 

3. Pact for the Movement "For the enforcement and promotion of the movement, a 10-point pact has been enacted as a common goal. Any one taking part in this movement must observe this pact: (1) Work hard for national reconstruction; (2) Be punctual and orderly; (3) Eradicate inefficiency and corruption; (4) Do away with extravagance and wastefulness; (5) Equality in life and equality before the law; (6) Hold rebellion suppres sion and national reconstruction above everything else; (7) Everything is for the front; (8) Everything is for production; (9) Respect the soldiers and help the armed forces; (10) Protect the farmers, industrial workers, and render social service."

4. 

The Enforcement of this Movement 

"The movement has as its historical mission the reform of the nation's social structure, economy, and politics. But in enforcement it has to start with individual citizens. Everyone must practise diligence and frugality in one's daily life. Every participant must make good his pledges and begin observing the following simple rules: "(1) For the promotion of manual labor: "1. Every Chinese who is sound in body and mind must depend on his own labor for a living. Whatever work one can do himself shall not be delegated to others, so that one's capability may be fully explored, the labor of others may not be wasted, and the total strength of the nation may be put to the best usage. "2. One must use one's time to the best advantage and gain time by improving efficiency and accuracy in one's work. If there is leisure, one should devote it to the improving of one's mind and physical health or to social welfare. In this way the intellectuals will have a chance to better their physique and the laborers a chance to acquire knowledge. "3. Sound health is the source of strength. To increase efficiency, productivity, and fighting power, we must promote physical education with a view to building up the nation's physique through constant exercise. "4. We must instill in our youth a sense of obligation to the nation, increase their self-confidence, and urge them to study hard and develop their personality. 

Furthermore, we must apprise them of the present needs of China, particularly in the field of scientific research and the advancement of modern technology, so that, when out of school, they may become useful members of society and contribute in national reconstruction." 

Must Solve Unemployment "5. We must increase production and help solve the unemployment problem. This can be achieved through the following two ways: "A. The concentration in the rear of large numbers of refugees, loafers, and the unemployed represents not only a sheer waste of manpower, but the things they consume have strained the resources of the Government at a time like this. We must, therefore, act together to find jobs for them and also to help those who are improperly employed to change to something beneficial to national defense or to the people's livelihood. Thus the consumers can be turned into producers. "B. We must persuade people to invest their money in productive enterprises beneficial to the national economy and the people's livelihood, such as the purchase of stocks issued by the Government-operated or public-owned business concerns, the operation of industrial or mining enterprises as a means to absorb idle capital, the establishment of factories and the laying out of farms with collective efforts, the promotion of road-building, forestation, reclamation, irrigation, and other reconstruction projects. We must also start work on relief projects so that those on Government relief may be engaged in productive activities, thereby utilizing the otherwise wasted manpower to help complete the nation's reconstruction program. "6. For the promotion of the total war plan we must: "A. Make the people realize the importance of the rebellion-suppression campaign. Inasmuch as the campaign was launched to safeguard the independence of the nation and to serve the interests of the people, the participation of the people themselves in the total war plan is necessary and they must assist the Government in the enforcement of its orders."

Obeyance of Law, Order 

"B. Persuade the people to obey the law and observe social order and to prevent riots and disturbances so that social peace and security may be maintained. "C. Set a personal example in promoting the organization of self-defense forces, in tightening up the organizations of the masses, in exercising a close check on the registration of households, in improving the Pao-Chia (small administrative units) system, in rounding up Communist spies and saboteurs, and in strengthening the counter-espionage works so that the security of the rear may be safeguarded. "D. Help in the registration of foodstuffs, in controlling national economy, in reporting on smuggling activities, and in carrying out the national policy of economic blockade against the Communists so that both military and civilian food stockpiles in Government areas may be replenished. "E. Encourage the youth to enlist in the armed forces, organize on an extensive scale groups to supervise conscription, report any irregularities on the part of the Pao and Chia chiefs, make sincere efforts toward bettering the living conditions of the draftees and increasing their payment, respect the dignity of the recruits, and safeguard the livelihood of their families, so that the morale of the fighting forces may be given an uplift and their fighting strength augmented.

"F. Promote a movement to honor and serve the military personnel through the organization of groups to see the soldiers off to their camps, to render first-aid, to comfort troops, to celebrate victories and engage in other activities designed to foster cordial relations between the army and the people, and support the campaign against the Communist rebels." 

Enforcement of Austerity 

“(2) Regarding the enforcement of austerity: "1. Thrifty consumption of gasoline and economical use of public property: "A. That public property should be used sparingly and the practice of using public property for private purposes should be stopped. "B. That gasoline rationing measures should be strictly enforced. "C. That the use of passenger cars should be reduced and bus services increased. "2. Curtailment of the use of manpower: "A. That Government offices, public organizations, and corporations should save their manpower by paying off redundant personnel. "B. That the number of guards, attendants, and servants in Government offices and residences should be reduced. "3. Thrifty consumption of food: "A. Thrifty use of foodstuffs. "B. Limited consumption of tobacco, wine and liquor, and sugar and candies. "C. Promotion of curtailment in the consumption and transaction of all kinds of meat."

Curtailment of Expenses 

"4. Curtailment of expenses in social functions: "A. That expenses in social ceremonies, such as weddings and funerals and in sending gifts, should be cut down. "B. That banqueting and dinner parties should be reduced. "C. That expenses used for superstitious purposes should be saved. "5. Promotion of savings and limited use of foreign 

"A. That a general drive for savings should be promoted. "B. That the deposit of foreign exchange should be encouraged. "C. That both the selling and buying of imported luxury articles should be stopped. "D. That thrift should be exercised in the use of imported goods which are not necessities of daily life or national defense. "6. Curtailment of use of water and electricity in 

"A. That the practices of stealing water and electricity and the refusal by Government offices to pay. power bills should be severely punished. "B. That power consumption should be saved through a system of progressive charges for power used over a certain limit. "C. That a general habit of economy in power consumption should be formed among all users. "7. Avoidance of improper recreation: "A. That commercialized dancing halls should not be patronized. "B. That proper recreation should be encouraged while gambling and other vices should be deprecated. "C. That heavy taxes should be imposed upon luxurious activities."

Promotion of Products 

"8. Promotion of national products, utilization of old articles as substitutes for the new, and making best use of discarded articles. "Note: "1. The articles listed above are to be enforced in big cities where the Industriousness and Austerity for National Reconstruction Movement is first launched. 

When the program extends to other parts of China, other items will be added to meet the practical needs of the situation. "2. The above items will be enforced by both Government decree and regulations to be laid down by the organizations responsible for the movement, in coordination with the force of public opinion mobilized for the purpose."

5. Organization of the Movement "(1) The Industriousness and Austerity for Nation al Reconstruction Movement is a revolutionary social movement. It must have a compact organization in charge of its promotion, and such an organization must come spontaneously from the people. '(2) To make this organization sound and powerful, we must expand gradually. Initial organizational endeavors should center around Nanking, Shanghai, and other leading cities to serve as examples, to be extended gradually to other parts of the country. "(3) In accordance with the "action-speaks-louder" principle, organizational work in each area should be preceded by a far-reaching and effective publicity campaign and should go through a period of adequate planning and preparation. The preparatory work should proceed centrifugally from the Central Government. Those in charge must possess exemplary qualifications and the spirit of self-denial and service. '(4) The movement must have a sound basic organization to supervise the fulfillment of the various aspects of the movement. To insure proper coordination and smooth action, the maximum number of persons in each basic unit should not exceed 15." 44 

Participation in the Movement (5) All those who pledge to observe the fact of the Industriousness and Austerity for National Reconstruction Movement and who measure up to the prescribed standards may participate in the organization of this movement. They must, however, first join the basic units of their own volition and must be determined to carry it in full themselves. "In the course of development, the principle of careful selection should be adhered to by those in charge. Should anyone in any unit be found guilty of violating the pact, he must be punished. All units must be purged of rotten elements as well as rumormongering speculators in order to insure the purity of the organization and enhance the forward-moving spirit of the components. 

'(6) Members of the Industriousness and Austerity 

Movement practising groups must be constantly up on themselves and supervise each other with a view to greater exertions. Not only should they carry out their own pledges but they should also assume leadership and exert influence upon those around them. Above all, they should encourage qualified kinsfolk and friends to join them in order to bring about expansion of the present movement. 

(7) The basic organization units, in addition to regional setups, should be extended to factories, stores, schools, army units, offices, and corporate and professional bodies. If, in one region or setup, there are a number of basic units, a joint liaison organization should be formed to strengthen this movement at the lowest level. "(8) When the organizational work of an area has reached a certain stage, there shall be established in that area a central organization to lead and direct the promotion of the movement there. (9) The headquarters of the movement is to be set up in the national capital to lead the various organizational units in the country in the promotion of this movement." 

6. Promotion of the Movement "(1) An extensive and far-reaching publicity campaign should be launched prior to the start of the movement in order to drive home to the masses that this is a revolutionary social movement aimed at weeding out social injustices and that this movement is intimately linked with the very interests of the people. Thus there will be enthusiastic response to the movement. 60 (2) Welcome warmly the industrial and laboring masses to participate in the organizations so as to invigorate the movement. They should be accorded honor and encouragement so that they may set good examples for the others. Through their participation in this movement, they may become an organized moral force. Above all, they must be conceded the right to criticize and censure any indolent and extravagant individual, thereby vindicating the cause of moral sanction. 66 (3) The movement must be closely coordinated with the measures which the Government has promulgated on mobilization against the Communist rebels and on recent monetary and fiscal reform and economic control as well as its anti-corruption decrees. 

Self-awakening and introspection should go hand in hand with persuasion and compulsion." 

Mobilization of Public Opinion 

(4) In conjunction with exposing lazy and extravagant elements, organs of public opinions must be mobilized. Names of those who repeatedly violate the pact and facts about their extravagance and waste will be announced in the newspapers or broadcast over the radio. An atmosphere must be created to make the

people look upon extravagance and examine indulgence as dishonorable behavior. At the same time, praise must be given to the honest and hard-working civil and military officials and to those gentry and others in cultural, educational, industrial, and commercial circles who exercise frugality, so as to give them encouragement and consolation and to impart an uplifting effect on social morals. "(5) Accurate estimation must be made in regard to the goals of the work, objective circumstances, and probable forces of obstruction. In designing a working program, it is necessary that the place, time, people, and situation should be taken into consideration so as to make it enforceable despite the differences in occupation, social status, and living conditions of the people. The contents must be solid and realistic and totally devoid of prosaic and stereotyped stuff. Do not start anything that is beyond any hope of accomplishment. Any work already begun must be carried out with concentrated effort despite all difficulties. 66 (6) Study should be constant in order to derive experience from one's work. Rectify past mistakes and grasp the real meaning of the movement. This great movement cannot be expanded, popularized, and perpetuated without unbending spirits, constant effort, and lively enthusiasm."
