OCTOBER 15, 1948 

No. 19

MAINTENANCE OF PEACE 

Dr. Wang Shih-chieh, Minister of Foreign Affairs and chairman of the Chinese Delegation, addressed the plenary session of the United Nations General 

Assembly in Paris on September 24. 

If world peace was to be preserved, Dr. Wang said, tolerance must replace the present ideological warfare which so decisively complicated relations between states today. He rejected as false the assumption that the world must choose between Communism and Capitalism. Other possibilities existed, he declared, citing 

Dr. Sun Yat-sen's principle of People's Livelihood in 

China, which was identifiable with neither.

The imposition of any ideology by an outside power on any people, through whatever means, was both impossible and dangerous, the Foreign Minister said.

He urged that every effort be made to strengthen the United Nations. Only through increasing tolerance of one another and a fuller realization of responsibilities under the Charter could the nations be truly united. 

Dr. Wang urged the UN to continue its efforts in pressing for a unified Korea and called for international cooperation to expedite the economic development of Asia.

The text of Foreign Minister Wang's address follows: 

"It is my pleasure to begin my brief speech by expressing the thanks of my Delegation for the hospitality of France. It would be difficult to find another place more suitable for our deliberations, either from the viewpoint of material comfort or from the viewpoint of spiritual inspiration, than Paris. France has made great contributions to human progress and the world has need of further contributions which the genius of the French people eminently qualifies her to make. We of the Chinese Delegation are glad to be in France. "As a representative of an Asiatic country, I recognize that the United Nations has made some progress over the old League of Nations, both in the representation of the peoples of Asia and the Far East and in its active interest in the problems of that vast region. 

Nevertheless, I must state frankly that, in my opinion, Asia and the Far East are still under-represented in the Security Council and the Economic and Social Council. In these two bodies, China's voice is the only voice from all the peoples east of the Suez. "In the work of the United Nations during the past year, I am gratified to find that problems arising in my part of the world received a fair amount of attention from the organs of the United Nations. I have in mind particularly the problem of Korean independence, the dispute between Indonesia and the Netherlands, and the work of the Economic Commission for 

Asia and the Far East. Mr. President, I wish to say that, in relation to all these matters, China is satisfied that good work has been done. "As the Commission to meditate in the dispute between India and Pakistan is just beginning its important work, I will refrain from offering any comment. It goes without saying that, since both India and Pakistan are friends and neighbors of my country and important members of the United Nations, we, in China, hope that their difficulties will be settled in a conciliatory spirit, with the aid of the United Nations, and settled in a way that the friendship between them will be strengthened."

The Problem of Korean Independence 

"The problem of Korean independence deserves the continued attention of the General Assembly. "The Temporary Commission for Korea has done some arduous work. It was made all the harder by the non-cooperation on the part of the Soviet Army of Occupation in Northern Korea. Nevertheless, in spite of this non-cooperation, the Commission has been able to make important contributions in achieving free, democratic elections in Southern Korea. "Whatever people may say about the former colonial administration of Japan in Korea, one thing is certain: that administration did not try to prepare the Korean people for an independent and democratic life. The general election in Southern Korea this spring was the first time that the people of Korea had a chance to express their own wishes in regard to their national destiny. Mr. President, the Korean people demonstrated beyond any doubt that they are eager and ready for free self-government; over ninety per cent of the registered voters went to the polls on May 10th. As to the final result, the judgment of the Temporary Commission itself is both fair and authoritative. I am happy to find that the Secretary-General, in his report to the General Assembly, concluded his chapter on 

Korea with the following paragraph: 'On 25 June 

1948 the Commission resolved to record as its opinion that the results of the ballot of 10 May 1948 were a valid expression of the free will of the electorate in those parts of Korea accessible to the Commission, parts in which the inhabitants constituted approximately two-thirds of the population of all Korea.' "After the elections of May 10, the Korean people constituted their Provisional Government. The Government of China thought it only right that de facto recognition should be given. We are glad that the

Government of the United States and that of the 

Philippines did likewise. "The work of the Temporary Commission for Korea is, however, not complete. Northern Korea has been excluded from the beneficent efforts of the Commission was not able to send any observer to the north of 

38° parallel. Korea remains a truncated Korea. "The future of Korea is vitally related to the peace and prosperity of the Far East and the world. The 

Korean people, by their ancient culture, their habits of industry, and their general devotion to peace, not only deserve independence and unity but, if united and independent, could contribute to the stability of the Far East. I plead with the General Assembly not to relax its effort with regard to the problem of Korean independence. We must go forward to enable the 

Korean people to achieve complete unity and independence. My Government will do its utmost to cooperate with other member-states toward this end. 

We have no ambitions whatever in Korea. The more independent and prosperous Korea is, the better for China." Indonesia “In the handling of the Indonesian question, the Security Council and its Commission of Good Offices deserve, in my opinion, the thanks of the United Nations. We stopped the war and have helped to maintain a truce without major incidents. We have also paved the way for the eventual settlement between the Government of the Netherlands and the Govern ment of the Republic of Indonesia. Both sides agree that there should be a United States of Indonesia and that there should be a union of equal partners between the Netherlands and Indonesia. Mr. President, I con gratulate both sides for agreeing to these great principles. "Serious difficulties have arisen in the implementation of the Renville Agreement, mainly connected with questions of procedure and somewhat aggravated by the fact that the Committee of Good Offices has bound itself with inelastic rules. While I do not wish to minimize the difficulties ahead, I cannot conceive that leaders on either side will allow procedure to defeat the aims of peace and democracy. "In relation to this question, I wish to offer two fur ther observations. Her Gracious Majesty, Queen Wil helmina, told the peoples of the world a year ago that colonialism was dead. The statement of Her Majesty really summarized the spirit of our age. I can venture a paraphrase: The national aspirations for freedom must be acknowledged by all. It is only by acknowl edging the right of all peoples to self-determination that we can hope that nationalism will not take abnormal, exaggerated, or anti-international aspects; and this leads me to my second observation. We, in China, are naturally glad that Indonesia is about to achieve political freedom. I wish to express the hope that the political freedom of Indonesia will be followed by orderly progress, actuated by the spirit of cooperation with her close neighbors as well as with distant friends, particularly with the Netherlands, with whom so many common bonds will inevitably remain."

Economic Commission for Asia and the Far East "I wish now to offer some observations on the work of the Economic Commission for Asia and the Far East.

ta 

"Of the numerous subsidiary organs of the United Nations, the Economic Commission for Asia and the Far East of the Economic and Social Council occupies a unique position, for it is charged with the task of promoting the economic progress of one half of the human race living in Asia and the Far East. Mr. President, my country and, I will presume to say, the neighbors of my country also, all cry for the benefits of modern science and modern technology. In Asia and the Far East, poverty is the primary problem. If there is one war that is worth fighting by the entire world, it is the war against want. "The main responsibility for economic advancement in Asia and the Far East must naturally be borne by the peoples and governments of this region, but the industrially advanced countries can do much to help. "Mr. President, it is not my purpose to attempt, in the course of this general debate, a detailed discussion on the economic problems of Asia and the Far East. 

But I would like to take advantage of the present occasion to state what seem to me to be the guiding principles in this matter. While we hope that the economic development of Asia will be hastened by international cooperation, we think that outside countries must not interfere with the social and economic systems which the peoples themselves wish to adopt and develop. There is no social system ready-made anywhere which could be exported to Asia. We reserve the right, and we will struggle for maintaining the right, to develop our social system in accordance with our needs and our ideas of social justice. "The Economic Commission for Asia and the Far East has held three sessions. We conceive the work of the Commission to be mainly promotional. It must start with the collection of data, but we hope it would not turn itself into an academic institution. It has to have conferences and resolutions, but we hope resolutions will not remain on paper. "In its latest session in India, the Commission recommended the establishment of a Bureau of Flood Control. I am glad to note that the Economic and Social 

Council has approved the formation of the Bureau of Flood Control in view of the great importance and urgency of the problems of flood control which affect the livelihood of millions of human beings in the valleys of the great rivers in Asia and the Far East. 

Some constructive, concentrated, and cooperative effort in this pivotal field of economic improvement will immediately stimulate interest and support of the peoples of Asia. We, therefore, hope that this work will be started as soon as possible and that it may serve as a concrete example of constructive cooperation of the United Nations in action.”

The Economic and Social Council 

"Having observed on the work of one of the Regional Commissions of the Economic and Social Council, I would like to say a few words on the general progress of the Economic and Social Council itself. "Speaking of the manifold activities of the Economic and Social Council, I wish to lay stress on its two bold efforts, one of which is connected with the removal of international grade barriers, while the other concerns the promotion of world-wide freedom of information. 

Both these efforts have resulted in noteworthy accomplishment in the form of either signed agreements or draft conventions. In spite of the fact that China's stage of economic development and her domestic political conditions are not as advanced or as tranquil as desired, her determination to support such constructive efforts of the United Nations has been no less ardent. It is the belief of the Chinese Delegation, Mr. 

President, that in advancing the work of the Economic and Social Council, member-states must be always ready to subordinate as far as possible national considerations to the needs of international cooperation." 

Veto Power 

"My Delegation is pleased to note that the Interim Committee, which was asked by the General Assembly in its last session to study the problem of the veto, has submitted a report which indicates conscientious work. "In this regard, the recommendations of the Interim Committee deserve the consideration of the Assembly. 

The Committee singles out a number of possible Council decisions which it considers to be unmistakably procedural in nature and, therefore, free from the veto and indicates another number which it considers to be less subject to political considerations and, therefore, possible of being taken out of the unanimity rule if the permanent members of the Council could be induced so to agree. It also suggests rules for the exercise of the veto which it considers to be what the permanent members of the Council should have normally followed. It seems that some such arrangements as recommended by the Interim Committee will go a long way in eliminating abuses. "It has been the view of the Chinese Government that the veto power is provided in the Charter to secure the maximum authority for every decision of a substantive nature which the Security Council may make. It was primarily intended to enhance the prestige of the United Nations, not to lower it. However, should it be abused, it might reduce the most vital organ of the United Nations to a state of paralysis.

Consequently, the permanent members of the Security Council must exercise restraint in its use and respect such limitations upon the scope of application of the power as rational interpretation of the terms of the 

Charter may dictate."

General Peace 

"Before I conclude, Mr. President, I would like to say a few words on the general problem of peace. "It is clear that the maintenance of peace cannot be achieved by one single effort or one single organization. War and peace are made by a multitude of forces along many fronts. The problem is as old as history. 

Politics, economics, and strategy have all exercised a mighty influence on the problem. "Through her recorded history of three thousand years, my country has had a variety of experience with the problem of war and peace. At times, we have been victorious conquerors. At other times, other peoples have conquered China. But, Mr. President, in our long experience, we never committed the sin of ideological imposition. We have, at all times, let other peoples alone in their religious practices and in their economic and political systems. If they found anything valuable in the culture of China and wished to borrow it, we welcomed them in so doing. We never attempted, however, to impose our ways on others. "Mr. President, I mention this matter mainly be cause what is peculiar about the problem of peace as it faces us today is this very ideological conflict. With the possible exception of the period of the Thirty Years' War in Europe, I recall no time when relations between states were so decisively complicated by a conflict in ideology as they are today. The general discussion of the conflict falsely assumes that the peoples of the world must choose between Communism and Capitalism. In fact, there are other possibilities. 

My country, for instance, believes in a principle called by the founder of the Chinese Republic the principle of People's Livelihood, which is identifiable with neither Communism nor Capitalism. 

"The imposition of any ideology by an outside power on any people, through whatever means, is both impossible and dangerous. We must learn again in the middle of the twentieth century what the European peoples in the middle of the seventeenth century once learned that is, toleration. Of course, the differences between the churches remain, and there are real differences which people may evaluate each in his own way. But civilized society has learned that people can very well worship God in a variety of ways. While a man can be devout in his own chosen way, he is bound to respect the religious practices of others. What took the European peoples a Thirty Years' War to learn in regard to religion, we should learn without the Thirty Years' War in regard to economic and social systems. "This does not mean that the world should be devoid of a common faith. And, indeed, we must subscribe to the fundamental ideas of the United Nations Charter in which we should all have faith but beyond which we must practice tolerance. If world peace is to be preserved, tolerance must replace the present ideological warfare, while every effort should be made to strengthen the authority of the United Nations, whose Charter we have all solemnly accepted and signed. 

Permit me to add, Mr. President, that the moment any member-state, especially any permanent member of the Security Council, begins to feel satisfied with a weak United Nations, that state is not being true either to world peace or to herself. "At our Second Session, I pointed out that the United Nations had reached a point where its future development and possibly its very existence hung in the balance. Events in the last twelve months did little to allay anxiety. If anything, the situation looks graver than that of a year ago. We are meeting at a time which, in its major aspects, is no better than 1939. Our responsibility in the maintenance of universal peace has, therefore, become heavier and of more immediate urgency since we last met. Only through increasing tolerance of one another and a fuller realization of our responsibilities under the Charter may we hope for the day when these nations may be truly united. In that mighty task, China yields to no one in her determination and dedication."

NOVEMBER 1, 1948 

No. 20

REPORT ON JAPAN 

Following a three-week visit in Japan during which he exchanged views with General Douglas MacArthur, Japanese cabinet ministers, and many others, General

Chang Chun reported to the Chinese people on September 28 in a radio address from Nanking, The former Premier, Foreign Minister, Governor of Szechwan, and Mayor of Shanghai described and evaluated conditions in Japan from military, political, and economic standpoints.

Advocating a positive policy towards Japan and the extension of conditional assistance, the former Premier pointed out that Japan could not recover economically without China's aid. He proposed a balanced development of Asiatic economy through the coordinated efforts of all the peoples concerned in which a peaceful and democratic Japan would have her rightful place. Japan was an Asiatic problem, he said, which should be treated and settled against the background of other Asiatic problems.

General Chang favored the early convocation of a conference to conclude peace with Japan. He reiterated China's policy of non-retaliation and stressed the need of a farsighted policy which takes into consideration the interests of all Asiatic nations and the formation of an economic bloc dedicated to the mutual assistance and benefit of all Asiatic peoples.

A translation of General Chang's report follows: 

"My recent trip to Japan has aroused much interest and attention both at home and abroad. My activities and utterances in Japan have met with an affinity of feeling and encouragement from one group of people and have, at the same time, caused speculation and criticism in another. Since my return, friends from all quarters have called on me. I regret not having been able to answer their questions one by one. I have just gone over the materials gathered during my visit and propose to make a general report. I shall also express my views from the standpoint of the future of entire Asia and the efforts that China may exert in this respect. I hope that this report may help our people to understand the problem of Japan and may serve as a reference for the Government in shaping its policies toward Japan. "Since the Meiji Restoration over 80 years ago,

Japan, on the one hand, fostered a spirit of militarism out of her traditional Shintoism and Bushido and, on the other, absorbed modern scientific knowledge as a foundation for her industry and armaments. 

Utilizing these two means, the militarists, Zaibatsu, and politicians resorted to stimulating the Japanese people for external aggression under the pretext of Japan's 'limited land, over-population, and insufficient production.' China, being geographically adjacent to Japan, became the first victim. Following the first Sino-Japanese war over 50 years ago, China suffered from Japanese aggression. Upon the completion of the Northern Expedition in 1928, the forces of the national revolution in China came face-to-face with Japanese imperialism. Events from the Mukden 'incident' of 1931 to the Marco Polo 'incident' of 1937 made a conflict inevitable. Resolutely, our people rose to resist. In the eight years of war, China suffered inestimable losses in lives and property, but as a result Japanese militarism collapsed and the external restrictions imposed on China's sovereign rights for the last 100 years were removed. Most of the peoples of Asia have since gained independence and freedom. 

This is the outcome of the last war and it is also the contribution which China, after terrific sacrifices, made to Asia and the world."

Delay of Peace Conference 

"After the Japanese surrender, the Allied forces occupied Japan. The policy-making organ for the occupation of Japan is the Far Eastern Commission in Washington, D. C., while the S.C.A.P. (Supreme Command of the Allied Powers), under General Mac- Arthur, is responsible for actual enforcement of the Allied control. As China suffered immensely at the hands of Japanese militarists, Zaibatsu, and politicians, she is naturally concerned and anxious to see what kind of a Japan will emerge from Allied control. 

Unfortunately, though three years have elapsed since the end of the war, the peace conference for Japan, like that for Germany, has yet to be convened due to the complicated world situation and the difference of opinion among the Allied Powers. As information between China and Japan has been scanty, it is difficult to obtain a clear and accurate impression of Japan from China. The Chinese people, out of extreme con cern, have become uneasy as a result of doubt and anxiety. They ask: Is it possible for Japanese militarism to revive as a consequence of international changes? Is it possible to establish a democratic government in Japan? Is it possible for Japan to return to the road of totalitarianism? Is it possible that

Japan will rearm and hinder China's economic reconstruction once she is economically recovered? Isn't the policy of control under the S.C.A.P. too lenient? Is there any mistake? From these doubts and apprehensions has stemmed the Chinese movement against the United States policy toward Japan. For many years before the war, I had not visited Japan. Two years ago, on my return from America after medical treatment there, I stayed for only three days in Japan and had not the opportunity to see much for myself. 

I could not then give satisfactory answers to the above questions. To acquaint myself with the real conditions in postwar Japan and also in response to the encouragement given me by fellow members of the East 

Asia Association, I decided to visit Japan. "I arrived in Japan on August 21, 1948, and left on September 13, so I stayed there altogether for three weeks, during which period I met General Mac- 

Arthur four times and heard reports from Japanese government and political leaders as well as important people in other circles. On the basis of information obtained from these meetings and reports, and from materials furnished by the S.C.A.P. and the Chinese 

Mission, coupled with my own observations, I wish to report on conditions in postwar Japan briefly as follows: 

Disarmament by S.C.A.P. "Militarily: At the beginning of the Allied occupation, there were still 2,700,000 armed forces in Japan. 

These were disarmed within a short period of time. 

Over 7,000,000 Japanese civilians and prisoners of war were repatriated to their native places. All Japanese army, naval, and air force organizations were dissolved. 

Warships, airplanes, forts, and other military equip ment and installations were either confiscated or destroyed. As to Japan's naval harbors, airfields, police, and maritime security force, so far as I know, this is what has happened to them. The naval harbor at 

Tokuyama was abandoned immediately after the war. 

Kure is now being used by the British navy, while Yokosuka and Sasebo are the main bases of the United States naval forces in Japan. Maizuru and Ominato are being used by the United States navy as secondary bases. The fortifications in the above-mentioned harbors have all been dismantled or destroyed. "There were originally over 200 airfields in Japan. 

Since the occupation began, all of them have been turned into either farmland or salt fields or other non-military uses. Only 33 others are kept for the 

British and American air forces, a few of which have been expanded and rebuilt to meet actual needs. "The entire Japanese police force used to belong to the state, under the command of a central organ. 

Now there are 125,000 security police in Japan. Every five of them share the use of a pistol. Among them, only 30,000 are directly under the Central Government, while the remaining 95,000 are members of local police forces. They are all professional policemen who have joined of their own volition, and there is no prescribed duration of service. Police clerks and employees total 30,000. Ex-officers of the armed forces are not allowed to enter the police force. In addition, there are about 9,000 railway police under the Ministry of Transportation, 5,000 economic police under the Economic Investigation Administration, and 2,000 labor supervisory officers under the Ministry of Labor. 

Altogether the entire Japanese police force is about 

170,000 strong. 

"The Maritime Security Administration has now 

29 unarmed patrol boats, each of about 100 tons with a speed of 10 nautical miles. It also has 15 tugboats varying from 50 to 200 tons each, 60 20-ton small boats, and two supply ships of 2,000 tons each loaned by the S.C.A.P. It is the duty of these boats to rescue vessels in distress, to prevent smuggling, and to deal with matters relating to the restoration of lighthouses and the salvage of sunken vessels, etc. "Judging from the above facts, we can say that the S.C.A.P. has done a thorough job in disarming 

Japan during the past three years. The present Japanese police, patrol boats, and the quantity of arms they possess, deemed by the S.C.A.P. the minimum requirement for the maintenance of peace and order, both on land and at sea, are inadequate as a foundation for the rebuilding of a Japanese army and navy.

As to Japan's basic need for rearmament, she has lost over 43 per cent of her territory and large coal and iron resources. She has no oil, rubber, and other materials needed for war. Japan strived several decades for territorial expansion, securing strategic resources, and for building up her military strength. 

Yet she went down in defeat in World War II. Of late, great advancement has been made in war weapons, and the pattern of warfare has changed. On the strength of her present resources and technical knowhow, Japan's chance of building up an adequate military defense to cope with another war is slim indeed." 

Changing Political Scene 

"Politically, Japan has always styled herself as a divine nation and the Mikado as an incarnation of the Divinity. Since the Meiji Restoration, Japan has embraced western science and parliamentary government as well, but there has been no change in its theocratic ideas. Hence, Japan's constitutional government could at best be described as her own prototype, there being no counterpart elsewhere. Acting on the fanatic belief and spirit that the Mikado is the Divinity itself and that Shinto is at one with politics, the Japanese people were so obsessed with their militaristic ideas that they became warlike and disdained death. "On the other hand, the Japanese militarists, working hand in glove with the Zaibatsu and politicians, managed to use as much of the Japanese national economic strength as possible for national defense and armament. Moreover, by virtue of their special status in the old constitution, the Japanese military virtually had the power of life and death over any Japanese cabinet and in the formulation of Japan's national policy. All this, plus the air-tight police system, denied the Japanese people every freedom and liberty. They had to rest content with their meager wages and long hours. About the 'slave-driving' militarists, the squeezing Zaibatsu, and the beguiling politicians, they dared not think anything, still less say anything. It was on this psychological and material foundation that Japan in the course of 50 her years built up power. "Since the Allied occupation of Japan began, the spirit on which Japan as a nation was based has undergone a change. Under the new Japanese constitution, the Emperor is invested only with the administration of 10 affairs of the state. These are confined to rituals and legal procedure. Political affairs have been put beyond the scope of the Emperor's powers. He has, therefore, become merely the symbol of the state with no divine attributes whatsoever. Today the Japanese people are free to discuss questions concerning the Mikado system and the abdication of Hirohito-something unheard of before the war." 

Dissolution of Shintoism “Although the S.C.A.P. allows Shinto worship to be continued among the Japanese people, its exploitation by the government is now forbidden, thus totally separating politics from religion. Government subsidies to Shinto shrines also have been suspended, while the number of shrine caretakers and worshippers has been gradually on the decrease. "In political matters, General MacArthur has aimed at forestalling the rise of two extreme forces in order to expedite the growth of the 'middle of the road politics.' In political organization, the S.C.A.P. has been fostering the centripetal system in contrast to the traditional centrifugal practice in Japan. It also has granted the Japanese people a high degree of freedom, thereby giving them democratic privileges and familiarizing them with the democratic spirit. 

All these measures can be considered correct. "The S.C.A.P. has carried out a strict purge in 

Japan of aggressive elements. From the day of occupation up to quite recently, it has purged more than 10,000 key members of militarist organizations, more than 6,200 wartime police chiefs and members of

Japan's special police, more than 5,600 educational administrative personnel and school teachers, and more than 200,000 public functionaries. All professional army officers have, with no exception, been cast out. "It cannot be denied, however, that in Japan today certain traces of historical poison still are in evidence. 

These vestiges are being nurtured in the Japanese people's thoughts and beliefs, customs and habits, and literature and drama. It goes without saying that these pernicious factors are hindering the establishment of peace and democracy in Japan. From this we may also see that it is easier to establish democratic institutions than to build up a democratic outlook and a democratic way of life. I have invited the attention of the S.C.A.P. and I have also sounded a warning to Japan's leaders. I have time and again reminded the Japanese people that they must stage a revolution in thought and carry out a psychological reconstruction. That is to say, they must lay a spiritual foundation in the interests of Japanese peace and democracy. "Come what may, now that Shinto government has been thrown overboard and the Mikado has been deprived of all divine attributes, it would not be easy to restore one or the other. Nor would it be any easier to make the Japanese people forego freedom now that they have already had a taste of it. It can safely be said, therefore, that it by no means will be easy for the Shintoist and Fascist politics of the Japanese prototype to raise its head."

Importing Daily Necessities 

"We come next to the Japanese economy. This question may be discussed from two angles. "First, the present economic situation in Japan. 

At the time of surrender, the Japanese economy was topsy-turvy. Production was at a standstill; there was a dearth of essential goods; commodity prices shot up; black marketing ran amuck; the bank deposits of the people were frozen; and there was but a scanty supply of ration goods. The Japanese people indeed lived a life of hardships. "During the last three years, as the result of S.C.A.P. control and American aid as well as the self-exertion on the part of the Japanese themselves, things have gradually improved, resulting in economic stability. 

Beginning from October of this year, the Japanese Government will increase the staple food rations and daily necessities for all the people in Japan proper. 

Nevertheless, the Japanese economy as it is today has yet a long way to go towards self-sufficiency, let alone a complete economic revival. Although the Far 

Eastern Commission has ruled that Japan may restore its standard of living as of the 1930-1934 period, maximum efforts made by the S.C.A.P. in Japanese industries during the past year or so have resulted only in increasing Japan's production to 55 per cent of the level for that period. "There is a huge gap between Japanese production and consumption. Current American aid to Japan is designed to fill this gap. The United States has been spending each year three to four hundred million dollars in American currency, or roughly one million dollars every day, in Japan almost entirely for foodstuffs and daily necessities. It is premature to talk of economic revival and reconstruction of Japan. "In order to lessen the burden of the American taxpayers, the United States has made every effort to encourage the increase of Japanese production. But it is generally believed that because of high wages, wartime shipping losses, exorbitant freight charges, exhaustion of stock piles of raw materials, and difficulties in obtaining through importation raw materials from foreign countries, Japan certainly has no hope of attaining self-sufficiency within three or four years."

Japan's Recovery Plan 

"Second, the economy reform in Japan. In this respect, the S.C.A.P. has been engaged in the dissolution of the Zaibatsu and in the bringing about of agrarian reforms. "Regarding the dissolvement of the Japanese capitalists, the S.C.A.P., at the beginning of the occupation, ordered the Japanese Government to annul all laws and decrees restricting free competition and to enact bills against monopoly. Orders were issued for the disbandment of the Zaibatsu companies which had in the past abetted Japan's aggression against other countries and for the purge of privileged figures in economic circles. "In regard to agrarian reforms, under the existing 

Japanese Farmland Readjustment Law, the state is to buy four-fifths of the holdings of the landowners for distribution among the tenant farmers. During last year the land holdings thus purchased amounted to 70 per cent of the estimated total. "We know that only when a nation has a democratic rule internally will it be at peace with the outside world, and that only when it practises economic democracy will it have a solid foundation for political democracy. In order to convert the aggressive 

Japan into a peaceful Japan, we must democratize its politics, and political democratization, in turn, is predicated upon economic democratization. China should give greater attention to the S.C.A.P. moves in this particular direction and earnestly seek their full implementation in order to achieve the purpose of building a peaceful Japanese economic democracy. "In this connection, I wish to call your attention to the one particular fact that the Japanese people, following their disastrous defeat and after having tasted the bitterness of abject poverty, are one and all anxious for an early economic recovery. To achieve this aim, they once laid out a five-year plan, specifying that it would be launched in 1948 and accomplished in 1952. Because of revisions that will have to be made and delay in preparations for its enforcement, it has been postponed for one year to start in 1949 and finish in 1953. "The plan, based on the estimated population of 89,000,000 in 1953, listed the supply and demand estimates. According to the Japanese estimation, the program cannot be enforced without external aid on account of Japan's shortage in foreign exchange. The 

Japanese planners figure they would need approximately US$1,600,000,000 to see the plan through, in addition to a revolving fund of from US$200,000,000 to US$300,000,000 for the purchase of food, cotton, wool, coal, iron, timber, and other raw materials from abroad. "On the basis of the calculation of Chinese economic experts, 40 per cent of the imported materials under the five-year Japanese recovery program will have to come from Asia, and another 50 per cent from the countries in the U. S. dollar bloc. "Before the war, Chinese materials, including those exported from the Northeastern provinces and Taiwan, constituted more than 25 per cent of Japan's

import trade. Under her economic recovery plan,

Japan, in the case of import, will need the coal, iron, salt, sugar, soya beans, cotton, and fodder that China produces; and in the case of export, although she will have to draw heavily from countries in the U. S. dollar bloc for raw materials, Japan does not expect to be able to dump her finished products, such as cotton goods, machinery, chemicals, and woolens in these countries. She cannot but depend on Asiatic countries and, in particular, on China as a market." 

Military Resurgence Unlikely 

"From the analysis made above, we should not be unduly alarmed over a possible revival of Japanese militarism. Whether militarily Japan can stage a comeback will depend chiefly on her ability to effect rearmament, which, in turn, is determined by the presence of the necessary economic conditions. Let us glance at the Japanese economic recovery program.

The bulk of the essential materials she needs will have to be imported from China, and she will have to sell her manufactured products in the Chinese market.

The key to Japan's economic recovery is, therefore, in the hands of China. If China refuses to cooperate with the Japanese, their economic recovery program cannot be smoothly enforced. "Irrespective of whether Japanese Shintoism has been on the decline, whether the divinity of the 

Mikado has gone into eclipse, whether the majority of the Japanese people have realized from the bitter experience of defeat the futility of militarism, whether they have tasted the bliss of freedom under the Allied occupation, or whether the authority of the Central Government has so diminished that totalitarianism will not have a chance to raise its head again, psychological and institutional changes are bound to have some restraining effect on the resurgence of Japanese militarism. "Economically, the Japanese are unable to reestablish a military state strong enough to menace world peace. But the key lies in our hands. We should dispel the passive and useless suspicion and apprehensions on our part and stop criticizing and pointing a censuring finger at others. We should instead assume an active attitude and adopt a positive policy toward Japan." 

Reconstruction of Asia "We must recognize that reconstruction in the world from now on will be centered in Asia. We can even say that, following the conclusion of World War II, the

Asiatic century has already begun. Asia is most rich in natural resources, largely undeveloped. Asia has an aggregate population of 1,000,000,000, with the lowest living standards. The biggest problem confronting Asia is how to tap undeveloped resources, manufacture goods, and raise the standard of living. "This task must be tackled with the combined efforts of all Asiatic countries and peoples, in the spirit of the provisions of economic cooperation in the United Nations Charter and in coordination with the func tions of the Far Eastern Commission, so as to form an Asiatic economic bloc dedicated to mutual assistance and mutual benefit. In this Asiatic economic bloc, China must take an active stand. If we do not underestimate our own strength, China should take the initiative and consult the countries concerned with the view to drafting a plan for the economic reconstruction of the area, and China should take the lead and spearhead the recovery program. A peaceful and democratic Japan, needless to say, will not be excluded. Only in the consideration of the future of 

Asia as a whole could a peaceful and democratic Japan be given her rightful place. "Before Japan's defeat, the Japanese militarists advocated what they called the Greater East Asiatic Co- 

Prosperity Sphere. That 'Sphere' was intended to be an economic bloc dominated by Japan with other Asiatic peoples as her slaves. It was extremely aggressive and exclusive in nature. Because of the existence of Japanse militarism, Asia then did not have the opportun ity to effect balanced, economic development. Now that Japanese militarism has been eradicated, it is possible to establish an Asiatic economic bloc to the mutual interest of all nations and peoples concerned. "We should, therefore, guard against the resurgence of Japanese militarism on the one hand so that she will not threaten world peace again, and direct her on the other hand to the road to economic democracy, so that her strong assets such as the scientific knowledge she has acquired during the last 50 years and the diligence and fortitude of the Japanese people may be fully utilized in the reconstruction of Asia's economy. We should, under the general principle of preventing Japan from being rearmed and of coordinating the reconstructive efforts of Asia as a whole, conditionally assist in the economic recovery of Japan."

Japan an Asiatic Problem 

"We may recall that, shortly after the conclusion of the war, President Chiang Kai-shek declared that we would not retaliate on Japan. On hearing this declaration, the Japanese people were deeply moved. 

By 'no retaliation,' in a passive sense, we mean we want to show the magnanimity of the Chinese as a people. In a positive sense, it should be an expression of our national policy, that is to say, when we come to deal with the Japanese problem, we will not only

passively refrain from retaliating on Japan, but positively do something about the situation. We must follow a farsighted policy, taking into consideration the interests of all Asiatic countries and peoples as a whole and the Sino-Japanese relationship on a longrange basis. We must work for the early convocation of the Japanese peace conference. We must determine, under a plan which takes in Asia as a whole, the economic relations between China and Japan and between China and the other Powers. "The economy of Asia must be developed and made prosperous. But economic development must be balanced, and prosperity must cover the entire area. The existence and security of the Asiatic peoples must be safeguarded. But no national existence will be possible unless that nation has self-determination; nor will there be security unless it is maintained by collective efforts. Moreover, the problem of economic development and prosperity and the problem of national existence and security are interrelated and cannot be solved separately. We must first secure for the Asiatic peoples the freedom from want before they can be assured of freedom from fear. The Japanese problem is, after all, an Asiatic problem. If it is treated and settled as such and against the background of other Asiatic problems, then all historical grievances and postwar suspicions can be properly eliminated. "These are my observations on the problem of Japan. I avail myself of this opportunity to submit them to you, my compatriots. I shall be grateful if those of you who are particularly concerned over the Japanese problems will favor me with your views so that we can make the question a subject of common study."

NOVEMBER 15, 1948 Reassertion of Determination 

Addressing a large group of Chinese officials and 

Kuomintang leaders on November 8 in Nanking, President Chiang Kai-shek vowed that China's Northeast, overrun by the Communists, would be recovered and solemnly declared that the Red Rebellion-Suppression Campaign would be pursued to the end in spite of temporary setbacks. The Chief Executive did not minimize the losses sustained in the battles of the 

Northeast. Instead, he said: "From the battles of the

Northeast, we have learned the greatest lesson in the history of our Revolution."

Communist sabotage and military reverses were described by President Chiang as the principal obstacles in the enforcement of the Government's currency reform and price control plan. If economic warfare against the rebels could be stepped up in an effort to stamp out their black market activities and artificially created buying sprees in cities and rural supplying centers, the nation's economy could still be stabilized.

Recalling the Government's earnest desire for peace after V-J Day and its repeated concessions in the protracted negotiations which only ended up in an allout armed rebellion by the Communists, the head of the state warned the nation to exercise vigilance and not to become victims of the poisonous Communist rumor offensive or the proffered false inducements or promises. He deplored the action of a group of misguided and defeatist intellectuals who recently agitated for peace-at-any-price in Nanking. "We shall never be able to rise again once placed under the Communist yoke," he cautioned, reiterating that the object of the National Revolution has been, since its inception, the realization of the Three People's Principles, unity and independence for the nation, and survival and freedom for the people. 

No. 21

The Chief Executive assured the nation that despite the military failure in the Northeast, the military, political and economic foundation south of the Great Wall has not weakened. He recalled how with unswerving faith and confidence, Communist obstruction of the National Revolution in 1924 and 1925 were eventually subdued. To overcome the current crisis, President Chiang urged composure, perseverance, strengthened coordination, deeper faith, and above all, a solid united front. He was confident the Communist tide could be turned.

A translation of the full text follows: 

"The loss of such important points in the Northeast as Chinchow, Changchun and Mukden recently, together with defeatist rumors created by the Communists, has caused the present state of uneasiness among our people. This is a natural phenomenon. We all remember that when the Mukden Incident of 1931 broke out, resulting in the loss of the Northeast to the Japanese, not only did all the Chinese people become alarmed and uneasy, but the whole world was shocked. Now, in our recent setbacks, it is but logical that the people should entertain similar feelings. However, we must realize that temporary losses do not necessarily effect the final success or failure of the Revolution." 

To Recover Every Part of Northeast 

"In 1945, my provisional headquarters was set up in Changchun and was prepared to take over the entire Northeast. Obstacle after obstacle was encountered until the office had to be moved to Shanhaikwan. 

Responsible comrades then feared that, having lost the Northeast for 14 years, we could no longer safeguard our sovereignty there. But we were determined to complete the objective of our War of Resistance,

Even if the Northeast were to be lost again for 14 years, we would fight to the bitter end to recover every part of it. "From the battles of the Northeast, we have learned the greatest lesson in the history of our Revolution. 

We suffered the greatest reverses and sacrificed the most. This we must always remember. The late Gen. 

Liao Yao-hsiang and his officers and men were determined to live or die at their posts. They reported to me that, if Chinchow could not be recaptured, they would never return to Mukden. Later, Communist forces encircled his forces and Gen. Liao, Army Commander Li Tao and their troops were all killed in action. This demonstrates the spirit of our revolu tionary soldiers, loyal to the nation and the people. 

It proves, too, the importance of the battles in that region in the over-all Rebel-Suppression Campaign. "Economically, the Government has been faced with many obstacles in the enforcement of its currency reform and price control plan, this because of Communist sabotage and military reverses. The Government has adopted remedial measures, but these should not be considered a panacea. Economic reforms cover a wide sphere and are most complicated. They cannot be expected to remove all evils. If we can weed out the evils and strengthen economic warfare against the Communist rebels, stamping out their black market activities and their creation of buying sprees in the cities and the rural districts, it is my belief that our currency reform can still stabilize our economy. "What the Government regrets most is that, after the enforcement of the currency reform, our patriotic people, in supporting the national policy, have converted their hard-earned gold, silver and foreign currencies into the gold yuan, only to suffer financial losses because of the fluctuation of commodity prices. 

The Government plans to recompense them for the injustice and will take effective measures to remunerate them for their losses. In the meantime, we hope that our people will not be misled by the many rumors. Rather we should continue to observe laws and regulations in order that economic stability may be secured."

Communists Launch Rumor Offensive 

"On the heels of our military reverses, the Communist rumor offensive began to gain momentum, so much so that not only has the general public been taken in by the vicious propaganda and become panicky, but the intellectuals, ignorant of the basic interests of the nation, have also become shaken in their belief. Of late, a few of these intellectuals in 

Nanking even came out openly with a statement agitating for peace. In doing this, they have seriously impaired the national spirit. They are nothing but a group of defeatists. "Dr. Sun Yat-sen said on his death-bed: 'You shall never yield to the enemy.' He said this to Wang

Ching-wei, who later not only yielded to the Communists, but to the Japanese as well. Consequently, Wang paved the way for his doom, at the same time dealing a telling blow to the revolutionary cause. 

Today, these people, oblivious of the national interests and forfeiting their citizen status, are playing into the rebels' hands by responding to their rumor of fensive in advocating surrender to the Communists. 

Besides expressing their own willingness to be Communist slaves, they want to sell once and for all the freedom of the entire Chinese race to a foreign power." 

Mao Opposed to Middle Course "Recently, the Communists instructed their docile disciples to spread two slogans: seek a livelihood and seek peace. These are copied from the Soviet Union, which had in her civil war days three slogans: soil, bread and armistice. Their Chinese followers expected to captivate the people of the entire nation and demoralize the spiritual anti-Communist front by the dissemination of these slogans. But in their calculations, they have greatly underestimated the discernment of the Chinese people who recognize the differences between the current situation in China and the situation in the Soviet Union at that time. "Ever since our National Revolution began under the leadership of Dr. Sun, our only object has been the realization of the Three People's Principles. In other words, we have continuously sought unity and independence for the nation, and survival and freedom for the people. In the days shortly after the conclusion of World War II, the Government, out of its anxiety to preserve internal peace so as to proceed with its national reconstruction, stooped to compromise with the Communists. Many concessions were made in its protracted negotiations with them. Time and again, cease fire was ordered by the Government, but to no avail. These are all documented facts and they should be obvious to all. In the end the Communists spurned every peace offer and started their all-out armed rebellion."

Mao Supported World Revolution 

"Let us now ask who has provoked the war and who has denied the people of their livelihood. Naturally, the people themselves know the correct answers. 

These intellectuals, if they are not willing tools of the Communists, should go over to the Red areas and demand peace and livelihood from Mao Tze-tung, the Communist chief. Actually, Mao has already put himself on record as deadly opposed to a middle course, and openly solicits a worldwide revolution. Shall we or can we, then, seek peace from this traitorous rebel who is bent on selling the national interests down the river?

You he Byz me TO 1012 eú d: C: "To suppress the Communist rebellion is our avowed national policy. We must carry it through with our aggregate strength. All the prevailing peace rumors can only aid and abet the Communist propaganda offensive; but they will never affect our determination to put down the rebellion. However serious the national crisis may become, the demarcation line between the right and the wrong must be carefully drawn. Responsible Party and Government officials and military officers, together with social leaders, shall all share this responsibility." 

Warns of Red Propaganda 

"Furthermore, we must heighten our vigilance against the Communist hoax of a so-called 'liberal policy.' Lately, they have been persistent in their propaganda that, in the occupied areas, they would no longer carry out land reform or 'struggle' or 'liquidation.' They have also declared that they would accord protection to private property. Since the fall of Tsinan, they have been launching an even more vigorous propaganda campaign to this effect. "But let it be known to all of our countrymen that the Communists' pledges of 'non-struggle' and 'nonliquidation' have been designed purely to anesthetize their newly-captured areas. In due course of their occupation, after their military strength is secured and they are in a position to control society as a whole, they will certainly disclose their true color and commence their consistent practice of merciless killing to purge dissident elements from their ranks. "Let me cite a few examples. Before Tsinan fell, the Communists pursued a policy of 'liberality' towards the people of Weihsien (a rail town about half-way between Tsinan and Tsingtao.) They administered identical treatment to the populace of Loyang before the Government troops evacuated Chengchow. Following the fall of Tsinan and the evacuation of Chengchow, however, people who escaped from Weihsien and Loyang revealed that they had met the same sad fate that had befallen their countrymen north of the Yellow River. The Communists took away all foodstuffs and other materials they could lay their hands on and subjected the people to untold misery." 

Northeast Will Be Re-won "Let us check these facts with people fleeing from 

Communist rule. Should our fellow countrymen fail to exercise due vigilance and to rally behind the Government's struggle to safeguard our national foundation and individual freedom, we shall never be able to rise again once placed under the Communist yoke. "Finally, we must all realize that the present military campaign is actually a prolongation of our War of Resistance. It is a long-term national war. In February, 1946, I said to my countrymen in Shanghai that we had gone through a bitter struggle for eight years before we actually secured our national independence and racial freedom. "Precisely for the recovery of the Northeast and the safety of our national life-line, we have engaged for eight years in the War of Resistance and three years more in the Rebellion-Suppression Campaign.

Even though the Northeast is lost for the time being, we will surely marshal all our national strength to regain that region."

Government's Position Assessed 

"I want to assure everyone today that, although our military operations in the Northeast have ended in failure, there is not the slightest weakening of our foundation below the Great Wall, be it military, political or economic. We still stand favorably compared with the Communist strength. That which counts most today is whether or not we among ourselves can increase or deepen our faith and become solidly united, whether or not we can boost our morale and marshal our strength. If we allow, as we are doing today, the Communist spies to spread their poison and propaganda, they, who otherwise are not strong, undoubtedly will continue to intimidate us by every means conceivable. "Conversely, if we meet the Communists with a united front, spiritually and physically, however difficult and critical the present situation, we can still turn danger into safety, defeat into victory. "We should all recall the years of 1924 to 1925 when the Communist reactionary forces were rampant in obstructing the National Revolution. Thanks to our unswerving faith and confidence, these forces were eventually subdued. "The history of the Revolution of the past 20-odd years will attest to the veracity of my statement. "In this troubled hour, our prime and most vital concern is the seeking of stability. Only through stability and calmness can we find our way out. It is my fervent hope that all responsible comrades in the various departments of the Central Government will demonstrate perseverance and composure and that, under the principle of military considerations first, they will support and earnestly and concertedly carry through our decision. Thus, not only can the situation be improved, but the Communist rebels can be weeded out at an earlier date. "With the Communists today, their military needs take precedence over all other things. Their party, political and military affairs are all conducted on a war basis. With us, however, everything still adheres to the peace-time course. In our loose and complicated organization, there is hardly any coordination.

Confident Tide Can Be Turned "It is therefore imperative that every one of us give serious thought to the question as to how we can so concentrate our energy and conserve our time as to better coordinate our endeavors and steady our steps. Successful tackling of this problem will enable us to resolve the crisis and save the situation. "My way of going about my tasks has always been such that, once I start an undertaking, I stop at nothing short of its full accomplishment. Nor would I take up any responsibility or office lightly. Once I pledge myself to a task, I aim to fulfill my responsibility to the fullest extent, whatever the setbacks, difficulties and defeats. This has been especially true of every military campaign I have conducted for the last 30 years or more. No such campaign had ever been launched without the most careful and serious deliberation. 

"Our Rebellion-Suppression Campaign henceforth will be little different from our resistance to Japanese aggression. Although we shall encounter numerous hardships and tribulations, we shall surely achieve our objective in the end. You can rest assured that I have the confidence to turn the tide. We must all strengthen our faith as well as assert our self-confidence. It is because of these traits that the Three 

People's Principles and the nationalistic spirit can never be destroyed and that ultimate victory is certain.” 

Double Tenth Message Calling the Chinese Communists "the Fifth Column of an international organization," President Chiang

Kai-shek, in his address delivered on the 37th Anniversary of the Founding of the Chinese Republic, October 10, 1948, in Nanking, asked the nation to persist resolutely in the effort to wipe out China's last enemy in order to safeguard her national unity and independence. 

A full translation of his address follows: "Today is the national day of the 37th year of the 

Republic of China. Thirty-seven years ago, despite adverse conditions, Dr. Sun Yat-sen successfully led the revolutionaries and the entire people in overthrowing the 260-odd-year-old Manchu rule and in replacing the centuries old monarchy with the Republic of China. This is the most glorious and greatest event in the history of the Chinese people. On this occasion, while recalling the spirit of sacrifice and struggle of Dr. Sun and the revolutionary martyrs, we must also realize that our own responsibilities are exceptionally heavy, and that it behooves us to continue to work hard in unison in order to complete the task left unfinished by them."

Constitutional Government Established "The Republic of China was founded by Dr. Sun with the Three People's Principles as the basis for national reconstruction. During the past 50 years, our revolutionary martyrs fought and sacrificed for the purpose of translating these principles into facts. 

In March, this year, the National Assembly met, and, in accordance with the Constitution adopted by the Assembly, a Constitutional Government has since been established. The initial success of the principle of the people's rights has thus been secured, and the Republic of China has become a Republic both in name and in reality. "Dr. Sun once said: "The Republic of China prior to 1924, because of the fight for power among the warlords and politicians, was not a Republic in the true sense of the word.' Twenty-four years have elapsed since. During this period, though our country has encountered more difficulties and hardships than any other country in the course of national reconstruction, our Government and people have worked indefatigably for the realization of the Three People's Principles, and have at last made ours a Republic both in name and in reality. Such an achievement, gained through difficulties and resolute effort, is most memorable and ought to enhance our confidence in the future of our nation. Now that the principle of the people's rights has been successfully consummated, there should be no difficulty in realizing the principle of people's livelihood. By adhering to Dr. Sun's teachings and by working hard, we shall automatically reach a state of general prosperity."

Most Urgent Task Confronts People 

"What I wish to impress upon my colleagues in the

Government and the entire people is the most urgent task confronting us today, namely, the realization of the principle of nationalism. We all know that this is really a prerequisite to the realization of the principle of the people's rights and the principle of the people's livelihood. Only when our national independence is secured can there be any firm basis for the people's rights and livelihood. If the principle of nationalism remains a theory, neither exercise of the people's rights nor improvement in the people's livelihood will be possible, for the entire nation and people will have been conquered by others. "It was thought that the downfall of the Manchu regime in 1911 marked the completion of the principle of nationalism. At the victorious conclusion of the War of Resistance against Japan, everybody again thought that the principle of nationalism had materialized fully at last. The present situation shows that our accomplishment in this respect has been incomplete. Our national enemy is still there. In fact

he is a formidable and dangerous one. His destruction is even more difficult to bring about than the overthrow of the Manchus and the defeat of Japan. 

This is because the Manchus and Japan were external enemies, easy to recognize, and the entire people, after having suffered from their atrocity and oppression, rose to resist. Our present enemy is the poisonous and vicious Communists, who masquerade as Chinese but actually are the Fifth Column of an international organization to victimize their fellow countrymen. They shout slogans of democracy and freedom, yet their actual conducts are hostile to China's democracy and freedom. Because they are Chinese, it is not so easy for the people at large to recognize them for what they are and to ignore the deceitful propaganda. Their designs and tricks are such that some of our educated persons have been hoodwinked. "As a result, the Government is confronted with more and more obstacles in its campaign to suppress the rebels. Therefore, I believe that since the beginning of our National Revolution as led by Dr. Sun, we have only now met with one last enemy, and the principle of nationalism is being subjected to a final test. At this moment when our nation is in a life and death struggle, our entire people, in order to avert the miserable fate of enslavement, must be vigilantly aware of the true character of the Communists. As true patriots we must resolutely persist in our present effort to wipe out our last enemy in order to safeguard our national unity and independence. Then we can really carry out Dr. Sun's Three People's Principles." 

Reason for Government Reverses "Early this year when our Constitutional Government was being inaugurated, I predicted that the Communists south of the Yellow River would be cleared out by the end of November. Now that October is already here, and yet not only have we failed in clearing the said region of the rebels, but Tsinan recently fell under Communist onslaught. The loss of 

Tsinan constitutes a great national misfortune. I feel personally responsible for what has happened. "Why should I make such a prediction at that time? I had hoped that, following the inauguration of our Constitutional rule and with the people of the entire country sharing political responsibilities, they would be able to rally behind the Government in the campaign against the Communists. Subsequent events proved that I had overlooked one fact. It is that those who have not lived under the Communist rule were taken in by the Red propaganda, thinking that after all the Communists are Chinese too and that thev in their own way also practise democracy and guar antee political freedom. In consequence, they mistook the Communist rebellion as an ordinary internal disturbance and they underestimated the Communist viciousness as compared with the Japanese imperialists'. "This psychological error has cost us the concentration of our will and the fortification of our spirit, thereby keeping us from using our strength to the best advantage. As a result, the campaign against the rebels has not had the benefit of coordinated efforts on the part of the army and the people, and we have suffered many setbacks. This is the main reason for our reverses. "If we do not pause and reflect and correct this erroneous attitude now, and if we fail to recognize in the Communists the arch enemy of the Chinese people and see the threat to our country and press on in our struggle with determination, then our path would be strewn with more difficulties and our military position would become even more serious than it is today. "Dr. Sun often said: 'Spiritual things are more important than material things.' The success or failure of the Communist-Suppression Campaign and the outcome of our national reconstruction program now hinges on our determination or lack of it. If we will study the causes of our failures, redress our mistakes, and, starting anew today, consolidate our will and concentrate our strength, we will succeed in our present task. The more critical become the conditions under which we have to work and the more onerous are our tasks, the speedier will be our success. In our 

30-odd years of National Revolution, there is not a single instance that does not bear out the veracity of this point. However trying conditions may become, so long as we abide by our revolutionary principles and remain faithful to our revolutionary mission, and so long as we think nothing of sacrifices and personal dangers, the final victory inevitably will be ours. "On the other hand, the Communist rebels are bound to fail. While giving lip service to the cause of revolution, they really lack the revolutionary spirit. 

They are but a group of opportunists, bent on benefitting themselves at the expense of others. Eventually they will meet with destruction. "By virtue of our indomitable courage and our tireless spirit, our chance for success will increase with the number of difficulties encountered. It is true that during the last six months many of our weaknesses and shortcomings have been exposed. Meanwhile, we have discovered many mistakes and encountered numerous unexpected difficulties. But these would redound to our benefit instead. For the exposure of drawbacks would provide us with some material for reflection, the discovery of mistakes would start us on the way to improvement, and temporary reverses

could be only the base from which we will march onward to victory. So long as we are willing to effect reforms and insist on progress, eventually we shall be able to attain success. "Henceforth, the Communist-Suppression Campaign will not be won in a few months, or even in one or two years. But as the saying goes, while adversity may lead to well-being, indulgence may cause death. The times that try men's souls are the times that present us with many opportunities for rebirth. So long as those in the Government and the people in general will undergo a self-examining process and give up the age-old habits of laissez-faire, indolence, lack of organization, and despondency, and become united in strength and spirit, the Communists will be pushed by their own defects to their ultimate failure."

Reds Public Enemy of People 

"In the past, the Communist rebels boasted of their ability to overthrow the Government before the end of this year. Now they have revised their timetable to five years. We must realize that time is working to our advantage. The longer the rebellion lasts, more 

Communist shortcomings will be exposed. It will be easier for the people to see the rebels in their true light, and the Communists will be that much closer to their total destruction. Already people in the Communist-occupied areas are demonstrating an intense abhorence for the Red rule. They would revolt immediately against the Communist tyranny whenever and wherever the rebels are not strong enough to keep the people in check. This tends to show that the rebels are the public enemy of our people, regardless of regional or avocational differences. Face to face with our people's love for the country, the rebels cannot last long. "Those working in the Government and the people as a whole must realize the seriousness of the present situation and the burden of their responsibilities. 

From now on, workers in every Government organi zation and the people of all social strata must concentrate their efforts on the anti-Communist campaign. 

They must pay full attention to their duties and prac tise austerity in their daily life. Meanwhile, they must assist the Government in carrying out its political and economic reforms. They must strive toward the same goal of eliminating our common enemy and carry out Dr. Sun's principle of nationalism. When this is done, the enforcement of the principle of people's rights and the principle of people's livelihood can be assured of success. "These are my observations on the anniversary of our national independence. I wish to offer them as food for thought for us all in the hope that, henceforth, we will all work harder and in greater unison toward suppressing the Communist rebellion and advancing our task of national reconstruction a step closer to its fulfillment."
