30 Rockefeller Plaza, New York 20, N. Y.

DECEMBER 1, 1948 

Gold Yuan Regulations Revised

The Executive Yuan, at its 13th special session held on November 11, revised the Regulations Governing the Issuance of Gold Yuan Notes and the Regulations Governing the Surrender of Gold, Silver and Foreign 

Currencies Held by the People, which were promulgated on August 19, 1948 by a Presidential mandate.

Whereas the original regulations governing the issuance of the gold yuan, which replaced the fapi (legal tender), provided that "the basic unit of currency of the Republic of China shall be the gold yuan," the revised regulations make the "gold yuan coins" the basic unit of currency. It is also stipulated in the revised regulations that each gold yuan shall have a legal content of 4.4434 milligrams of fine gold, to be minted by the Government for issuance through the 

Central Bank of China.

The revised regulations provide: "Those who deposit gold yuan notes in banks designated by the Central Bank of China, besides receiving the regular interest upon the completion of one year, may at the time of making the deposit exchange an equivalent amount of gold yuan notes for gold yuan coins. Pending the minting of gold yuan coins, they may change them for gold or silver dollars at the stipulated rate of exchange. The Central Bank of China shall, within ten days following the promulgation of these Regulations, announce the names of banks designated to accept such deposits and also the date when the exchange of gold yuan notes for gold and silver may commence." 

Regulations governing the conversion, drawn up by the Central Bank of China and approved by the Ministry of Finance, have subsequently been published. According to these regulations, only the Bank of China, the Bank of Communications and the Farm- 

No. 22 ers Bank of China, in addition to the Central Bank, will handle the conversion as appointed banks. Pending the minting of gold yuan coins, all conversions above one-half shih liang (each shih liang is equivalent to 31.25 grams), will be paid in gold. Amounts below one-half shih liang will be paid in silver dollars, containing 88 per cent pure silver. Exchange of gold yuan notes for gold and silver dollars by depositors began on November 22 as scheduled in seven leading cities, namely, Nanking, Shanghai, Chungking, Canton, Hankow, Peiping and Tientsin. 

Holding of Gold, Silver, Etc. Permitted

By virtue of the Revised Regulations Governing 

Surrender of Gold, Silver and Foreign Currencies, holding of gold, silver, silver coins as well as foreign currencies is permitted by law. However, with the exception of silver coins, the circulation of and transaction in gold, silver and foreign currencies shall be prohibited. 

Conversion rates between the gold yuan and gold, silver, silver dollars and United States currency banknotes have also been revised. The following stipulations are given in the regulations: (1) An ounce (shih liang, or 31.25 grams) of gold may, according to its fineness, be exchanged for 15 gold yuan coins; (2)

An ounce of silver may, according to its fineness, be exchanged for 15 gold yuan coins; (3) A silver dollar may be exchanged for ten gold yuan coins; and (4) U. S. banknotes may be exchanged for 20 gold yuan coins per US$1. Other foreign currencies may be computed at foreign exchange rates of the Central 

Bank of China.

Formerly, each dollar in United States currency was stipulated to be exchanged for four gold yuan in notes.

People holding gold and/or silver ornamental articles shall be permitted to continue such holdings. 

Transfer of gold and/or silver ornamental articles is also permitted. 

Use Deposits to Pay for Imports 

Holders of gold, silver, silver coins and foreign currencies, it is further stipulated, may deposit their holdings in the Central Bank of China. They may deposit their foreign currency holdings in the original forms. Gold, silver and silver coins may be deposited after conversion into gold yuan according to the specified rates of exchange. Such deposits may be used to pay for imports or for such purposes as are permitted by the Ministry of Finance.

Gold and/or silver required as raw materials by medical and industrial establishments or for other legitimate uses may be bought upon application to and approval by the Ministry of Finance. 

Special provisions are made for travellers in transit or tourists visiting China. For such travellers and tourists, the regulations provide that at the time of their entry into China, they shall declare before the 

Chinese Maritime Customs authorities any gold, silver and/or foreign currencies which must later be taken out of the country. The travellers in transit or tourists are also required to turn over such holdings to the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks for custody, but they may claim the same back at the time of their departure from the country. However, those who fail to apply and claim back such holdings within six months following their entry into China shall be required to convert them into gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes according to the specified rates of conversion.

Following are textual translations of the two sets of revised regulations: 

REVISED REGULATIONS GOVERNING THE ISSUANCE OF GOLD YUAN NOTES Article 1. The basic unit of the currency of the 

Republic of China shall be the gold yuan coins. Each yuan shall have a legal content of 4.4434 milligrams of fine gold, to be minted by the Government for issuance through the Central Bank of China.

Article 2. Subsidiary units of the gold yuan coins shall be chiao (10 cents) and fen (cent), with 10 fen making one chiao and 10 chiao making one yuan. 

Article 3. Gold yuan coins shall be of five de nominations, namely, one yuan, five yuan, ten yuan, 50 yuan and 100 yuan, to be made of gold and silver respectively. Meanwhile, gold yuan notes shall be issued by the Central Bank of China for simultaneous circulation.

Article 4. Subsidiary coins of the gold yuan shall be of four denominations, namely, five cents, ten cents, 20 cents, and 50 cents, to be made of copper and nickel respectively. Meanwhile, the Central Bank of China shall issue subsidiary gold yuan notes for simultaneous circulation.

Article 5. As from the date of issuance of the gold yuan notes, the issuance of fapi notes and Northeast circulation notes shall be discontinued. Fapi notes previously issued shall be converted at the rate of three million dollars to one gold yuan, and Northeast circulation notes at 300,000 dollars to one gold yuan. They shall be exchanged for gold yuan notes without limitation in amount before November 20, 1948. During the period of conversion, their temporary circulation shall be permitted at the aforesaid rates of conversion. Measures for dealing with Taiwan and Sinkiang currencies shall be adopted by the Executive Yuan separately.

Article 6. As from date of issuance of the gold yuan notes, all public and private accounting shall be on the basis of gold yuan. In all matters in which registration is required by law, and the amount of value has to be specified, alteration in registration must be effected within six months following the issuance of gold yuan notes.

Article 7. As from date of issuance of gold yuan notes, all public and private rights and obligations in terms of fapi and/or in Northeast circulation notes shall be computed and settled at conversion rates stipulated in Article 5 of these Regulations. Measures for dealing with outstanding fapi bonds issued by the Government shall be adopted by the Executive Yuan separately. With the exception of the 36th Year U. S. 

Gold Loan which shall be repaid according to its original regulations, the 27th Year Gold Loan, 29th

Year Reconstruction Loan, 31st Year Allied Victory U. S. Gold Loan, and the 36th Year Short-Term 

Treasury Notes shall be converted at official exchange rates to new gold yuan loan to be issued by the Government. 

Article 8. Gold yuan notes shall be backed by 100 per cent reserve, which shall be composed of 40 per cent gold, silver and foreign exchange, and 60 per cent in bonds and securities and Government-owned properties designated by the Government. 

Article 9. Holders of gold, silver, silver coins and foreign exchange may have them converted into gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes according to stipulated rates. The aforesaid rates shall be determined separately.

20: da EQ The To 

Article 10. Holders of gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes may buy foreign exchange according to Government regulations governing foreign exchange. 

Portions of the aforesaid regulations governing foreign exchange dealing with export and import trade shall be prescribed in accordance with the export and import link system. 

Article 11. Those who deposit gold yuan notes in banks designated by the Central Bank of China, besides receiving the regular interest upon the completion of one year, may at the time of making the deposit exchange an equivalent amount of gold yuan notes for gold yuan coins. Pending the minting of gold yuan coins, they may change them for gold or silver dollars at the stipulated rate of exchange. The 

Central Bank of China shall, within ten days following the promulgation of these Regulations, announce the names of banks designated to accept such deposits and also the date when the exchange of gold yuan notes for gold and silver may commence.

Article 12. The total issue of gold yuan notes shall be determined by Government mandate.

Article 13. For the inspection and custody of re serve of gold yuan note issue, a Gold Yuan Note

Issue Reserve Supervisory Commission shall be estab lished, the regulations governing its organization to be adopted by the Executive Yuan. 

Article 14. Gold yuan notes must be countersigned by the Governor and General Manager of the Issue 

Department of the Central Bank of China before they are put into circulation.

Article 15. The Central Bank of China must, at the end of each month, report the monthly total of the gold yuan note issue to the Ministry of Finance and the Gold Yuan Note Issue Supervisory Commis sion.

Article 16. The Gold Yuan Note Issue Reserve Su pervisory Commission must, after the end of each month, conduct an examination of the amount of gold yuan notes issued by the Central Bank of China and the conditions of their reserve. They shall prepare an inspection report and submit same to the Executive 

Yuan, sending duplicate copies to the Ministry of 

Finance and the Central Bank of China.

Article 17. If and when the Gold Yuan Note Issue 

Reserve Supervisory Commission finds the reserve of gold yuan note issue to be insufficient or the reserve in gold, silver, and foreign exchange to be below the percentage stipulated in Article 8, it shall immediately notify the Central Bank of China to suspend note issue and to withdraw from circulation the amount of notes in excess of reserve, and at the same time report separately to the Executive Yuan and the Ministry of Finance.

Article 18. Upon receipt of the above-mentioned notification, the Central Bank of China must either immediately withdraw the excess amount of gold yuan notes issued or make up the deficit in issue reserve.

Without the consent of the Gold Yuan Note Issue 

Reserve Supervisory Commission after further examination, the Central Bank of China may not increase the note issue.

Article 19. Counterfeiting, alteration, or deliberate damaging of gold yuan notes is punishable according to the Regulations Governing Punishment for Damage to the National Currency.

Article 20. These Regulations shall come into force from the date of promulgation.

REVISED REGULATIONS GOVERNING SURRENDER OF GOLD, SILVER AND FOREIGN CURRENCIES 

(Note: "Ounce" used in these Regulations means shih liang, equivalent to 31.25 grams.) 

Article 1. "People" used in these Regulations include natural persons, juristic persons and other corporate bodies within the territories of the Republic of China.

Article 2. As from date of promulgation of these 

Regulations, the people may be permitted to hold gold, silver, silver coins, and foreign currencies, but with the exception of silver coins, their circulation and transaction shall be prohibited. 

Article 3. Holders of gold, silver, silver coins or foreign currencies may exchange them for gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes at the Central Bank of China or other designated banks according to the following stipulations: (1) An ounce of gold may, according to its fineness, be exchanged for 1,000 gold yuan coins. (2) An ounce of silver may, according to its fineness, be exchanged for 15 gold yuan coins. (3) A silver dollar may be exchanged for ten gold yuan coins. (4) United States banknotes may be exchanged for 

20 gold yuan coins per US$1.

Other foreign currencies may be computed at foreign exchange rates of the Central Bank of China. 

Article 4. Holders of gold, silver, silver coins and foreign currencies, besides exchanging them for gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes, may, according to their own wishes, dispose of their holdings by either one of the following two methods: 

(1) To purchase the 36th Year U. S. Gold Loan

Bonds: In the case of U. S. currencies, these may be used to buy the said bonds directly; in the case of gold, silver, silver coins or other foreign currencies, they may be used to buy the said bonds according to rates of exchange mentioned in the foregoing 

Article. 

(2) To deposit their holdings in the Central Bank of China: In case of foreign currencies, they may be deposited in their original forms; in case of gold, silver, and silver coins, they may be deposited after computation according to the rates of exchange mentioned in the foregoing Article.

The aforesaid deposit may be used to pay for imports or for such purposes as are permitted by the 

Ministry of Finance. 

Article 5. Gold, gold sand and silver produced by domestic mines shall be bought by the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks at prices to be fixed by them from time to time and shall not be subject to the restriction embodied in Article 3. 

Article 6. Gold and/or silver required as raw materials by medical and industrial establishments or for other legitimate uses may be bought upon application to and approval by the Ministry of Finance. 

Article 7. People holding gold and/or silver orna mental articles shall be permitted to continue such holdings and to transfer them. 

Article 8. No gold, silver, silver coins and foreign currencies may be carried out of the country, but a person who carries out gold ornaments not exceeding a total of two ounces and/or silver ornaments not exceeding a total of 20 ounces or a person who carries out foreign currencies equivalent in total to not over US$100 is exempted from the above restriction. 

Article 9. Persons who carry gold, silver or foreign currencies into the country may, according to their own wishes, exchange them for gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes at rates stipulated in Article 3 or use them to buy the 36th Year U. S. Gold Loan Bonds or to deposit them according to provisions in Article 4.

Travellers in transit or tourists who bring in gold, silver and/or foreign currencies but must later take the same out of the country, shall at the time of entry into the country declare such belongings before the Customs authorities and hand them over to the Central Bank of China or its appointed banks for custody, but they may claim the same back at the time of their departure from the country. But those who fail to apply and claim back such holdings within six months following their entry into the country shall be required to convert them into gold yuan coins or gold yuan notes according to the rate of conversion stipulated in Article 3.

Article 10. With the exception of the Central 

Bank of China, no Chinese or foreign banks, unless duly appointed by the Central Bank of China, may convert or buy or sell gold, silver, silver coins and foreign currencies.

Article 11. In case of violations of stipulations of 

Articles 2, 8, 9 and 10, the gold, silver, silver coins or foreign currencies concerned shall all be confiscated. 

Article 12. In case of violations of stipulation of 

Article 10 of these Regulations involving unauthorized conversion or purchase or sale of gold, silver, silver coins, and foreign currencies, the person or persons concerned shall be punished according to the Regu lations Governing Punishment for Dealings in Gold and Foreign Currencies, in addition to the confisca tion of the gold, silver, silver coins or foreign currencies concerned.

Article 13. In case of violations of stipulations of 

Article 2, 8, 9 and 10, if the violations are discovered and objects concerned are confiscated as a result of information furnished to the authorities in charge, 20 per cent of the confiscated value shall be given as reward to the informant.

Article 14. These Regulations shall come into force from the date of promulgation.

DECEMBER 15, 1948

The Harbor Near Tientsin 

The spreading hostilities in North China are engaging the serious attention of the New Tangku Harbor Construction Bureau which has spared no effort in completing the harbor at the mouth of the Hai Ho, 50 miles from Tientsin. For, completed and protected, the harbor will greatly facilitate international trade in that part of China.

The new harbor is being built under the lengthening shadow of the Communist threat. Hsing Fan-sen, director of the New Tangku Harbor Construction 

Bureau, announced early this year that, despite the fact that the Communists had penetrated to the suburbs of Tangku more than 50 times in eight months, all work had continued without a hitch. Because of the proximity of Communist raiders each time, the workers' families had to be evacuated to Tientsin, but the 4,000 workers remained on their jobs and construction proceeded according to schedule. 

Work on the harbor, which had begun under Japanese occupation, is being completed by the National Government on a three-year program at an estimated cost of US$23,500,000. When completed, the ice-free harbor will accommodate four 30,000-ton ships at the same time, with facilities for repair of coastal vessels and ship-rail transportation through Tientsin to all parts of North China. It will eliminate the costly transshipment of cargoes from large vessels, which at present have to unload outside the Taku Bar at the mouth of the Hai Ho (River).

While the Japanese originally planned for the harbor to handle up to 27,000,000 tons a year, the present program is designed to care for an annual volume of two million tons by 1950, with the total capacity to be increased eventually to ten million tons. When this goal is realized, the harbor will not only be a boon to the economy of North China but also will provide an important port for international trade in the northern Pacific.

Present Facilities Inadequate 

Among the principal ports of China before the war, Tientsin ranked second in importance, next only to Shanghai. In 1936, a typical prewar year, the percentage of China's foreign trade handled by the five largest ports were as follows:¹ Port Shanghai Tientsin

Tsingtao Canton Kowloon % Exports 51.26 % Imports. 

Total % 58.78 55.56 16.67 7.69 11.54 7.29 5.80 6.44 6.01 3.27 4.45 0.88 6.09 3.86

However, the facilities for ocean shipping at Tientsin have always been inadequate, due to the shallowness of the Hai Ho. Most ships have to moor off 

Tangku, near the mouth of the river, about 50 miles from Tientsin. This cargo is sent to and from Tientsin in lighters, which involves considerable delay and expense.

The problem of the Hai Ho is complicated by the fact that it is the common outlet for almost all the rivers running through the province of Hopei and is too small to discharge the waters in flood season. One of the streams, the Yungting Ho, carries a heavy silt charge which accumulates in an enormous bar-known as the Taku Bar-in the open sea.

Formerly, the Hai Ho Conservancy Commission tried to improve navigation by constant cutting and dredging of the river and deepening of the channel over the bar. Still, few vessels drawing more than hai.

The Chinese Year Book 1937, p. 663. The Commercial Press, Shang-

fifteen feet have managed to cross the bar, and since the end of December, 1934, the permissible draught for steamers navigating the river from Taku to Tientsin has been only thirteen feet.

Beginning of the Project 

As early as 1921, Dr. Sun Yat-sen, in his International Development of China, had proposed the construction of an ice-free, deep-water harbor on the Po Hai (Gulf of Chihli), in connection with his scheme for the development of three large ocean ports, one each for North, Central, and South China. He chose for its location a point on the coast between Taku and Chinwangtao.2 

The Japanese, following their invasion of North 

China in 1937, decided to construct a harbor and port for the better exploitation of that region. The 

South Manchurian Railway engineers actually favored the location proposed by Dr. Sun, but the military picked Tangku for its proximity to Tientsin and the advantages of existing communications.

In June, 1939, the Japanese established a temporary office for the projected Tangku harbor under the aegis of their China Development Company, and began the initial work of surveying. Construction began in 1940. 

A year later, in October, 1941, the project came under the jurisdiction of the North China Communications.

Company, which set up the New Tangku Harbor Bureau.

At one time, the project employed as many as 

10,000 workmen, besides 2,500 technicians and 1,000 clerical workers. The cost was then estimated at nearly a billion dollars in North China "puppet" currency.

With the Japanese surrender in August, 1945, construction came to a complete halt.

Plan of Construction 

The plan of construction of the new harbor, located on the northern bank of the Hai Ho, calls for: (1) the building of a breakwater system consisting of a northern arm of approximately eight miles, a southern arm of approximately 10.5 miles, and a cross-channel section of about 2,100 feet; (2) a ship channel eight miles long and 650 feet wide; (3) two adjoining wharves with a combined berthage of 4,600 feet; (4) a ship lock 590 feet long and 69 feet wide; and (5) rail spurs and roadways connecting the wharves with the 

Tangku station.

At the time of the Japanese surrender, about half of the breakwater system had been completed; the ship channel had been dug to a width of about 430 feet and a depth of 16 feet. Wharf No. 1, about 2,300 feet long, was about 90 per cent finished; Wharf No. 2, half as long as the other, was about 60 per cent com- 

2 Sun Yat-sen, The International Development of China, p. 10-13.

Reprinted by Chinese Ministry of Information, Chungking, 1943. pleted. In addition, the ship lock at the entrance to the Hai Ho had been built in part, and about six miles of rail spurs and four miles of roadways had been finished.

Work Resumed in 1946 

After the Japanese surrender, a great deal of damage was done to the machinery, equipment, buildings, and vessels of the harbor construction. In the twelve months following, the channel became clogged with silt, particularly around the wharves and the lock. The unfinished breakwaters were deteriorating. Total losses were estimated at more than US$100,000,000. 

In October, 1945, the Ministry of Communications began to take over the harbor, and in April, 1946, a 

New Tangku Harbor Construction Bureau was or ganized by the Ministry to undertake the job of repairing the damages and resuming the work of construc tion.

Work on the harbor was resumed in July, 1946, and for the next six months consisted mainly of repairs to the wharves and lock, dredging of the channel, and restitution of the vessels formerly belonging to the harbor administration.

As a result, the channel and lock were reopened in 

December, 1946, and, in the next three months, a total of 64 vessels passed through the lock on their way up the river to Tientsin. Wharf No. 1 is now prepared to berth seven ships of from three to five thousand tons.

A Three-Year Program 

In order to continue with the harbor project, a tentative three-year program has been proposed by the technical staff of the bureau, as follows: 

First Year 

1. Work on the unfinished portions of the breakwater system, including about 15,000 feet of the southern arm and 12,000 feet of the northern arm, requiring about 4,000,000 cubic feet of stone, 400,000 cubic feet of concrete blocks, and 5,000 feet of single layer steel sheet piles. 

2. The main ship channel to be dredged to a width of 230 feet and a depth of 20 feet; the depth at Wharf 

No. 1 to be increased to 20 feet, at Wharf No. 2 to 

26 feet, and at both ends of the lock to 16 feet. 

3. Repair of two coal chutes at Wharf No. 2; com pletion of the 1,200-foot lighter quay to the east of the wharf; and construction of an oil landing with mud dredged from the channel. 

4. Filling in of the land around the lock with about 

4,000,000 cubic feet of earth; installation of new elec tric control machinery for the lock; and addition of

5. Filling in of the land for the wharves with mud dredged from the channel, estimated at 60,000,000 cubic feet, enough for about 115 acres. 

6. Continued dredging of the channel which will require the purchase or renting of one or two large dredges in addition to those now in use or repair. 

Also the purchase of one or two ice-breakers for use in clearing the channel in winter. 

7. Completion of a 2,000-ton dry dock started by the 

Japanese; conversion of the Japanese caisson works into a skidway; addition of another simple repair dock, plus machine shops, water tenders, and barges. Also the addition of about ten each of lighters and tugs. 

8. Improvement of land installations, including the addition of two 25-ton cranes, two three-ton movable cranes, and several warehouses. Repair of present rail spurs and laying of another seven miles of tracks. 

Addition of three miles of roadways, first surfaced with gravel, later to be macadamized. 

9. Addition of power installations, to include four 

1,000-kilowatt generators and a 6,000-kilowatt transformer, in order to provide power for dredging operations. Also the installation of a water-filtering reservoir and the laying of 16,000 feet of water pipes. 

10. Installation of 20 buoys and 11 floating markers, all fastened by three-ton anchors; also two foglights, six beacons, three signal stations, four deepwater buoys, and two lighthouses at the eastern end of the breakwater.

Second Year 

1. Continuation of work on the breakwaters, including 24,000 feet of steel sheet piles each for the northern and southern arms, requiring about 115,000 tons of steel. Also, an additional 5,000,000 cubic feet of stone and 2,000,000 cubic feet of concrete blocks. 

2. Dredging of the channel to an additional width of 100 feet and to a depth of 26 feet, at the same time deepening two anchorages to a depth of 36 feet. 

3. Filling in of another 113,000,000 cubic feet of earth from the dredging of the channel. 

4. Purchase of one each additional dredger and icebreaker.

5. Additional work on the rail spurs and roadways; supplementary power installations; 30,000 feet more of water pipes.

Third Year 

1. Completion of the work on the breakwaters, with an additional 50,000 tons of steel piles and 600,000 cubic feet of concrete blocks. 

2. Continued dredging of the channel to a total width of 430 feet and a depth of 26 feet, with two more anchorages of 36-foot depth. 

3. Additional filling in of 60,000,000 cubic feet. 

4. Laying of another three miles of rail spurs. 

5. Purchase of four three-ton movable cranes, two ten-ton movable cranes, six rotary movable cranes of one to three tons each, and one 150-ton floating crane. Also, the construction of five warehouses of 

100 by 300 feet and the addition of two more coal chutes at Wharf No. 2. 

Results in Increased Facilities

On completion of the first-year program, the harbor will possess the following facilities: 

1. Passage of 3,000-ton vessels through the lock directly to Tientsin. 

2. Berthing at one time at Wharf No. 1 of seven 

3,000-ton vessels or those under 16.5 fet draught, and the handling of up to 1,000,000 tons of goods a year. 

3. Coaling at Wharf No. 2, up to 1,000,000 tons of coal a year. 

4. Use of the lighter quay and oil landing to the east of Wharf No. 2. 

5. Anchorage of large vessels outside the Taku Bar, with warehouse facilities for 15,000 tons and first steps toward ship-rail transportation.

With the completion of the second-year program, two ocean vessels of 30,000 tons can anchor in the harbor. In addition, the breakwaters having been largely finished, any necessary lighterage operations in the harbor will be protected.

When the three-year program is completed, there will be anchorage within the harbor for four 30,000ton vessels, complete harbor facilities for navigation and for repairs of coastal vessels, and full operation of ship-rail transportation.

Since much of the equipment and machinery will have to come from abroad, the cost figures are given in U. S. dollars. Furthermore, the figures in the estimate are based on the price level of April, 1946, and may have to be readjusted at various times to prevailing prices. Item

The above estimate of the total cost of the project comes to slightly more than US$23,500,000. It does not include either United States government taxes, export duties, and other levies, or harbor charges, storage fees, and unforeseen emergency expenses.

The Future of the Harbor 

The original aim of the Japanese in building the new Tangku harbor was to facilitate the export to Japan of such raw materials as salt, iron, coal, and aluminite, and the import to China of manufactured goods from Japan. No consideration was given to 

China's international trade or economic policy. Now that the harbor is in Chinese hands, careful attention must be paid to China's actual economic condition and foreign trade in general, and the commercial and industrial needs of North China in particular. 

In view of these factors, it was decided to proceed at this time with the modest program recommended by Chinese and American experts, leaving room for future expansion as may be warranted. The original 

Japanese plans called for a handling capacity of eight million tons a year at the end of the first stage, and envisaged an eventual goal of 27 million tons. The goal of the three-year program is a two-million-ton capacity, which may be increased to four million tons with improved loading facilities, and eventually reach ten million tons when additional wharves and other installations are built.

The site of the new harbor is large enough for considerable development as a port. Further growth northward may possibly link the Tangku port with the area of Peitang on the Chiyunho (canal). Thus it can, in time, not only become one of the most important harbors in China, but also rival the chief centers of international trade in the northern Pacific.

President's 1949 Message

Declaring that the key to the problem of peace or war rests with the Communists, President Chiang Kaishek, in his New Year message to the nation, stated that if the Communists were sincerely desirous of peace, the Government would be only too glad to discuss with them the means to end the war. He would be satisfied, he said, if a negotiated peace were not detrimental to the national independence and sovereignty but would contribute to the welfare of the people, if the Constitution were not violated and constitutionalism preserved, the democratic form of gov ernment maintained, the entity of the armed forces safeguarded, and if the people's free mode of living and their minimum standard were protected. He said if peace could be secured, he would not be concerned about his own position. The full text follows: 

"My Fellow Countrymen: 

"On this thirty-eighth anniversary of the founding of the Republic of China and the first anniversary after the introduction of constitutional Government, I observe with great regret that our national reconstruction efforts have come to a state of suspension and the Three People's Principles still remain to be achieved. "Since the end of the war against Japan, the main object of the Government has been to lay down a firm foundation for peace and reconstruction so as to alleviate the sufferings of the people. The task which the Government considered as of great importance was to recover the Northeast, thereby preserving China's national sovereignty and territorial integrity. But, unfortunately, we have not been able to do this. "On the other hand, Tsinan was lost to the Communists and Chinchow, Changchun, and Mukden fell into their hands in succession. The Mukden tragedy of 1931 has repeated itself. Commercial and industrial cities as well as cultural centers in North and East China are now being menaced by the Communists. "During this national crisis I cannot but blame myself for my inadequate leadership. I am sorry that

I have not lived up to the high expectation of the people." 

Will Not Abandon Liberty 

"The military situation has entered upon an exceedingly perilous stage. The fate of the nation and the historical and cultural continuity of our people will soon be decided. The issue of this struggle will determine whether the Chinese people will continue to live as free men and women or as slaves, and whether they will live at all or perish. "Everyone is concerned over the policy which the Government has pursued in dealing with the situation. 

We are convinced that all patriotic citizens will not tolerate the Communist method of 'liquidation' and 'struggle' and that they are not willing to abandon their liberty and to remain inactive at this critical moment. "But we are also fully aware that military operations have increased the people's burden and that they hope for an early conclusion of the war. Shouldering the responsibility of the conduct of national affairs, I have carefully studied the situation and considered the wishes of the people. "The Father of our Republic once said: "The aim

of national reconstruction of the Republic of China is peace.' Being a strong believer in the Three People's 

Principles and abiding by Dr. Sun Yat-sen's bequeathed teachings, I did not have any intention to fight the Communists at the end of the war. "Immediately after V-J Day, the Government declared its principles for peace and reconstruction. 

Later it went one step further by seeking to solve the Communist question by means of political consultation. "In the subsequent one and a half years, the Communists disregarded every agreement and obstructed every peace effort that was made. As a result, these agreements and the programs which were agreed upon were not implemented. "In the end, the Communists started an all-out rebellion, thereby endangering the very existence of the nation. Unwillingly, the Government was forced to order a general mobilization and to proceed with the anti-Communist campaign. I am sure that these historical facts are still vivid in minds. your "Communism has already had a history of twentyfive years in China. In this period I have never for a moment given up the hope that the Communists would place the national interests above that of their own, would follow the regular courses as befitting a political party, and would join hands with the Government in finding ways to work for peace and national existence." 

Challenges Reds' Sincerity "Such was the purpose of the political consultation which took place shortly after the war, and such remains the objective of the Communist-Suppression Campaign. The key to the problem of peace or war and to the happiness or suffering of the people is not in the hands of the Government, nor can the problem be solved by popular appeal for peace to the Govern ment alone. The problem can be decided only by the 

Communists. So the sincerity of the Communists for peace must be ascertained before the problem can be solved. "If the Communists are sincerely desirous of peace, and clearly give such indication, the Government will be only too glad to discuss with them the means to end the war. "If a negotiated peace is not detrimental to the national independence and sovereignty, but will contribute to the welfare of the people; if the Constitution is not violated and constitutionalism preserved, the democratic form of government maintained, the entity of the armed forces safeguarded; and if the people's free mode of living and their minimum liv. ing standard are protected, then I shall be satisfied. "In my devotion to the cause of the National Revolution, I have known nothing except loyalty to the nation, service to the people and the realization of the Three People's Principles, thereby fulfilling my sacred duties as a Revolutionist. If peace can be secured, I am not at all concerned about my own position. In this I will follow only the consensus of the people. "If, on the other hand, the Communists are not sincerely desirous of peace and will insist on continuing their armed rebellion, the Government, with no other alternative, will fight them to the finish. As the political nerve center of the country, the Nanking- Shanghai area will be held at all costs, and the Government is determined to throw in all available forces for a decisive victory. I firmly believe that the Government will win out in the end, and it also will mark the turning point of the war. "The people of the nation should realize that only by carrying on this war of self-defense can a real peace be secured; and only by making sacrifices can a glorious victory be won. It has been almost forty years since 

I joined the National Revolution. In every major and prolonged battle, I have suffered many setbacks and have been subjected to vicious propaganda. No matter how serious was the reverse, I never lost confidence in the final victory. And as a rule, victory was obtained in the end. "National spirit, justice, and righteousness, such as they are generally recognized, must constitute our mainstay in this fight. The brutal force of the Communists can wrest from us the Northeast, but it can never subdue our national spirit. "The Communists can penetrate into our heartland, but they can never soil our national character. 

Righteousness is the strength for victory, and right will always triumph over might. We of this generation have seen the greatest cataclysm in our history; we have on our shoulders an unprecedented mission. "We can, we must, endure temporary afflictions and sacrifices and struggle for the existence of the nation, the continuity of our history and culture, our free way of living, and the prosperity of our offspring. "My countrymen, at this time of national crisis, I feel all the more keenly the weightiness of my responsibilities and the difficulties in fulfilling my mission. 

It is my hope that all patriotic countrymen will advise me and join hands in the fight for the sanctity of the Constitution, the maintenance of China's territorial and sovereign integrity, the freedoms of the people, and the continuance of our culture. "Dr. Sun said: 'Final victory belongs to the one who struggles until victory.' I hope that all of us will bear this motto in mind."

Premier Sun's Broadcast 

In a broadcast over the Central Broadcasting Station in Nanking on New Year's Day, Premier Sun Fo said now that the Government has declared its desire for peace, it was his hope that the people of the nation would give their organized support to the peace effort so that the Communists might realize what the people desire and end their military action in the interest of their own countrymen. The people, he said, should urge the Communists to contribute to the peace and to endorse a national policy based upon the principles of constitutional democracy and the nation's desire for a free way of living. The text of the speech follows: 

"My Fellow Countrymen: 

"The year 1948 with all its turbulence has passed. 

As I speak to you, we stand on the threshold of a new year, and our thoughts, as always, turn to hopes for peace and stability. "The President, in his New Year's message issued today, likewise reaffirmed his own determination as well. 

This message, I am sure, will not only cheer the hearts of the Chinese people but also will be welcomed by our friends throughout the world."

Why Peace Movement Failed 

"You will recall that, shortly after V-J Day, a political consultative conference was called, which was attended by representatives of all political parties and leading independents. The Government decided to call this conference because it was generally realized that the country and the people needed recuperation and peace so that rehabilitation work could be started. 

After three weeks of concerted efforts, and thanks to the good offices of General George Marshall as President Truman's special envoy to China, a program for the settlement of all disputes was worked out. "Had these measures been carried out at that time, all of us would have seen more prosperity and happiness in our midst. Unfortunately, all the parties concerned could not completely abandon their own selfish ends, and the people in general did not exert sufficient influence in promoting this peace movement. "As a result, the war broke out again. In the past three years, our fellow countrymen have suffered incalculable losses in life and property. The hopes that we cherished on the day of victory after eight long years of war became shattered. "Now our President has reiterated his determination to win peace. It was indicated that, if peace could be achieved, the question as to whether he should continue to retain his present position could be settled by the wishes of the people. The President's statement has given the fullest expression to his candor and greatness. "Personally, I have never shirked from any effort for peace and stability. Now, as President of the Executive Yuan, I shall devote my highest effort to bring about peace for the welfare of the people. But peace cannot be achieved easily by the effort of a few persons. The attainment of this common aim depends upon the united exertion of all of you. "First of all, our officers and men at the front must continue to stand by their posts and perform their duties with courage and determination. They must not be distracted by the fact that the Government has made known its desire for peace. At this juncture, in the present battles of Hsuchow-Pengpu, Taiyuan, and Peiping-Tientsin, members of our armed forces have already turned the tide of the campaign by making supreme sacrifices, which have won the acclaim of the nation. At this time when the Government is reaffirming its desire for peace, the officers and men must redouble their efforts in order to win peace. "Secondly, peace is not a one-sided affair. It can be achieved only when the two parties at conflict are both sincerely inclined to it and are equally determined to see it come to pass. "Now that the Government has declared its desire for peace, it is my hope that the people of the entire nation, seeing that this is an issue of vital concern to the future welfare of every citizen, will give their organized support to the peace effort so that the Communists may realize what the people of the country desire and end their military action in the interests of their own countrymen. They should urge the Communists also to contribute to the peace effort and to endorse a national policy based upon the principles of constitutional democracy and the people's desire for a free way of living."

To Ensure Livelihood 

"My fellow countrymen, during the eight years of War of Resistance, you already had contributed your utmost and made great sacrifices for the nation. But your suffering did not end with the termination of the war. My colleagues in the Government and I are

fully aware of the continued sufferings of the people, aggravated by conscription, food requisition, and the mounting inflation. "The Government is now determined to adopt and to enforce reforms in conscription methods and food requisition in order to ensure the greatest benefit for the nation's farming populace. The Government is also carefully considering practical means of stabilizing price levels, thereby ensuring at least a minimum degree of security for the people's livelihood. "The Government will not lose time in doing everything possible to alleviate the sufferings of the people and to promote their welfare. With your support and your cooperation, I hope that the Government will be able to achieve what I have said in this message. I wish all of you a happy New Year."

Note: Instead of appearing on the 1st and 15th of each month as heretofore, the Bulletin, beginning January, 1949, will be published irregularly.
